Section 1: Introduction

p1-p5

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p1 - p2

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the basic definition of sterilizing filtration: removing microorganisms from a liquid without affecting product quality
  • Learn the history of PDA TR 26 (1998 → 2008 → 2025) and what drove each revision
  • Recognize the key historical event behind the shift from 0.45 µm to 0.2/0.22 µm sterilizing-grade filters: Dr. Frances Bowman's FDA finding
  • Understand the origin and significance of the ASTM F838 standard test method
  • Understand the positioning of the revised TR 26: a scientific guidance document, not a mandatory standard
1.0 Introduction

Original Text

Sterilizing filtration is the process of removing microorganisms* from a fluid stream without adversely affecting product quality (1-4). This technical report is intended to provide a systematic approach to selecting and validating the most appropriate filter for liquid sterilizing filtration applications.

PDA's original Technical Report No. 26: Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids, (TR 26) published in 1998 described the use and validation of sterilizing filtration to a generation of pharmaceutical scientists and engineers. A revision of the original report was developed in 2008 in response to enhancements in filtration technologies and recent additional regulatory requirements within the pharmaceutical industry at that time. This current revision has been implemented to reflect current best practices and regulatory expectations, as well as clarify the roles of filter suppliers and end-users. References are provided for scientific publications and international regulatory documents where more detail and supportive data may be found.

When membrane filters entered the commercial scale, 0.45 µm-rated membranes were considered “sterilizing-grade” filters and were used successfully in the sterilizing filtration of parenterals. These filters were qualified using Serratia marcescens as a standard bacterium for qualifying membranes used for water quality testing. In a paper published in 1960, however, Dr. Frances Bowman of the U.S. Food and Drug Administration (FDA) observed a 0.45 µm “sterile-filtered” culture medium to be contaminated with an organism subsequently shown to repeatedly penetrate 0.45 µm-rated membranes in small numbers at challenge levels above 104 – 106 colony forming units (CFU) per cm² (5). This led to the development of American Society for Testing and Materials (ASTM) F838, a standard test method for evaluating sterilizing-grade membrane filters (6). Challenge organisms are discussed further in Section 6.0.

*Note: Sterilizing filtration is not intended to remove viruses.

"Sterilizing filtration is the process of removing microorganisms from a fluid stream without adversely affecting product quality."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Sterilizing Filtration: a process that removes microorganisms from a liquid by filtration so that the filtrate is sterile. Two elements are key here:

  • Removing microorganisms — this is not killing them (that is sterilization); the microorganisms are physically retained on the upstream side of the membrane
  • Not affecting product quality — this point is often overlooked. Sterilizing filtration must not change the active ingredient, concentration, pH or other critical quality attributes (CQAs) of the drug product

Sterilizing-Grade Filter: a filter that passes the ASTM F838 test and is able to completely retain the challenge organism Brevundimonas diminuta (at least 107 CFU per cm²) under standard conditions.

ASTM F838: a standard test method published by ASTM International (American Society for Testing and Materials) to evaluate the bacterial retention capability of sterilizing-grade filters. It is the globally recognized standard for qualifying sterilizing-grade filters.

Analogy

The water purifier upgrade story:

Imagine the water purifier at home. The earliest filter cartridges (0.45 µm) removed most impurities, and everyone felt reassured. Then one day an inspector (Dr. Bowman) found that some especially small bacteria could "slip through" the gaps in the cartridge, particularly when the water carried a high bacterial load.

This finding shocked the whole industry, like discovering that your door lock cannot actually stop a burglar. People then began developing finer cartridges (0.2 µm) and set a strict "burglar-proof test standard" (ASTM F838) to make sure the new cartridges were truly safe.

Key Notes

Sterilizing filtration ≠ virus removal filtration: the original text points out in a footnote that the purpose of sterilizing filtration is to remove bacteria and other microorganisms, and that it does not include viruses. Viruses (20-300 nm) are far smaller than the pore size of a sterilizing-grade filter (0.2 µm = 200 nm), so virus removal requires a dedicated virus-retentive filter, typically with a pore size of 20 nm or 50 nm.

Historical turning point: Dr. Bowman's finding in 1960 was the most pivotal turning point in the history of pharmaceutical filtration. It showed that "passing a standard test" does not mean "achieving sterility under all conditions", a lesson that remains a core principle of sterilizing filtration validation today.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the definition of sterilizing filtration specifically stress "not affecting product quality"? If a filter retains 100% of bacteria but adsorbs 30% of the active ingredient, can it be called a suitable sterilizing filter?
  2. Why did Dr. Bowman's 1960 finding fundamentally change the whole industry's understanding of sterilizing filtration? If you found a similar problem at your CDMO site today, what process should you initiate?
  3. TR 26 clearly states that it is not a "mandatory standard", so why do almost all pharmaceutical companies refer to it? Think about the relationship between technical reports and regulations.

Key Notes: TR 26 Version History

Each revision was driven by industry practice and regulatory change, which shows that sterilizing filtration is a continuously evolving field, not knowledge you learn once and are done with.

1.1 Purpose & Scope

Original Text

The primary objective of the TR 26 revision team was to develop an updated scientific technical report on sterilizing filtration. This report does not always address region-specific regulatory expectations but it provides up-to-date scientific recommendations for use by industry and regulators in establishing a sterilizing filtration policy. This report should be considered a guide and is not intended to establish mandatory standards for sterilizing filtration. Concepts presented in TR 26 pertain to processes in which filtration is used to render a liquid sterile, with all accompanying assurances, and may not be universally applicable to all filtration processes, such as upstream bioburden reduction steps.

The revision team was composed of European and North American industry professionals to provide a diverse perspective, thus ensuring that the methods, terminology and practices of sterilizing filtration presented are reflective of sound science and can be used globally. This report underwent review by global technical peers that included feedback from experts from the Americas, the Asia-Pacific region and Europe.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Scientific guidance vs mandatory standard: TR 26 is positioned as a "Scientific Technical Report", not a regulatory requirement. This means:

  • Guidance — it provides science-based recommendations and best practices
  • Non-mandatory — it is not a legal requirement, and companies may adapt it to their own circumstances
  • Global applicability — it is not aimed at the regulations of any particular country or region but establishes general scientific principles

Bioburden Reduction: the original text specifically notes that the content of TR 26 mainly applies to the "sterilizing filtration" process and does not necessarily apply to upstream bioburden reduction steps. These are two different levels of filtration purpose.

Analogy

Michelin Guide vs health regulations:

TR 26 is like the "Michelin Guide" of the pharmaceutical world: it represents the industry's highest professional consensus and best-practice recommendations, and all outstanding restaurants (pharmaceutical companies) refer to it to raise their own standards. But it is not a "food hygiene regulation" (such as FDA regulations or EU GMP Annex 1); regulations are the legally binding mandatory requirements.

In practice, however, when a regulatory inspector visits your site and your practice deviates seriously from the recommendations of TR 26, you had better be able to give a scientifically sound justification, because inspectors also refer to these industry consensus documents.

Key Notes

The importance of a global perspective: the author team of the 2025 revision included industry experts from Europe and North America, and the document was reviewed by experts from the Americas, Asia-Pacific and Europe. This global review process ensures the content is broadly applicable.

Significance for CDMOs: as a CDMO taking orders from customers worldwide, you must meet the requirements of different regulatory systems at the same time, such as FDA (US), EMA (Europe), PMDA (Japan) and NMPA (China). TR 26 provides the scientific basis that spans these systems, giving you a recognized starting point when establishing your sterilizing filtration policy.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: suppose a European customer requires your sterilizing filtration validation strategy to "comply with the latest version of TR 26", while a US customer requires "compliance with the FDA Guidance on Sterile Drug Products Produced by Aseptic Processing".

You do not need to build two completely different systems, because the scientific principles of TR 26 are highly consistent with the FDA guidance. Your strategy should be to use the TR 26 scientific framework as the foundation and then add to or adjust it for the specific requirements of each regulation.

Practice Questions

  1. TR 26 is positioned as "scientific guidance" rather than a "mandatory standard", but in an actual inspection, what could happen if your practice is inconsistent with the TR 26 recommendations? How would you respond?
  2. The original text states that TR 26 does not apply to "upstream bioburden reduction steps". Explain how sterilizing filtration and bioburden reduction filtration differ in purpose, validation requirements and filter selection.
  3. As a CDMO taking on projects from customers in many countries, why is having a common sterilizing filtration policy based on TR 26 more efficient than building a separate policy for each customer?

Section Summary

  • Sterilizing filtration is defined by its core elements: removing microorganisms from a liquid without affecting product quality. Both elements are indispensable
  • Historical turning point: in 1960 Dr. Bowman found that 0.45 µm filters could not completely retain all microorganisms, which led to the establishment of ASTM F838 and the industry shift from 0.45 µm to 0.2/0.22 µm pore size ratings
  • Positioning of TR 26: a globally recognized scientific technical report (not a mandatory standard) that provides a systematic approach to the selection, validation and use of sterilizing filtration
  • Key points of the 2025 revision: it reflects current best practice and regulatory expectations, and in particular clarifies the division of roles between filter suppliers and end users
  • CDMO strategy: build a common sterilizing filtration policy on the TR 26 scientific framework, then adjust it for the specific requirements of each regulatory market

Section 2: Glossary

p6-p10

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p3 - p7

Learning Objectives for This Chapter

  • Build a core terminology base for sterilizing filtration and understand the precise definition of each term
  • Distinguish easily confused terms, such as Sterilization vs Sterilizing Filtration, Extractable vs Leachable, and Diffusive Flow vs Forward Flow
  • Understand the "upstream ↔ downstream" orientation of a filtration system and how the various pressure terms relate to each other
  • Master the key differences between Double Filtration and Redundant Filtration and their impact on integrity testing
  • Become familiar with the standard abbreviations used in TR 26 and link them to the terminology in later chapters
2.0 Glossary (A–C)

Original Text

Definitions have been provided to help clarify the concepts discussed in this document. While some definitions used vary, for instance, among companies and geographic location, the definitions described below are consistently used within this technical report. Where a definition is based on another published source, the source is cited.

Adsorption
The retention of solutes, suspended colloidal particles or microorganisms to fluid contact surfaces, e.g., the surfaces of pores in filtration membranes.

Aseptic
Conditions that minimize viable microbial population.

Assay
Analytical method used to determine purity or concentration of a specific substance in a mixture.

Bacterial Retention Study
An experimental test study used to demonstrate that a filter can effectively retain a defined level of challenge microorganism under specified conditions.
Note: This is also referred to as “Bacterial Challenge Test”.

Bioburden
The type and number of microorganisms per unit of material (7).

Bracketing Approach
A validation method that tests the specification extremes of a process or product. The method assumes the extremes will be representative of all the samples between the extremes.

Bubble Point
The measured differential gas pressure at which a wetting liquid (e.g., water, alcohol or product) is pushed out of the largest pores of a wetted porous membrane and a steady stream of gas bubbles or bulk gas flow is detected.
Note: This is also referred to as “Transition Point”.

Bubble Point Test
A test to indicate the maximum pore size of a filter. The differential gas pressure at which a liquid (usually water) is pushed out of the largest pores and a steady stream of gas bubbles is detected from a previously wetted filter under specific test conditions. Used to test filter integrity with specific, validated, pressure values, wetting liquids and temperatures for specific pore-size (and type of) filters.

Capsule Filter
A self-contained filter device or assembly.

Cartridge Filter
A filter device requiring a housing for use.

Compatibility
Proof that no adverse interaction between the filter, process fluid and filtration conditions has occurred.

Critical Process Parameter (CPP)
A process parameter whose variability has an impact on a critical quality attribute and therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality (8).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Adsorption vs Absorption: These two words are often confused. In filtration:

  • Adsorption — a substance attaches to the "surface" of the membrane, like dust sticking to a wall
  • Absorption — a substance is taken "into" the interior of the material, like a sponge soaking up water

In sterilizing filtration, adsorption is one of the important retention mechanisms, but it can also cause active ingredients in the product to be "taken up" by the membrane. This is why Compatibility testing is required.

Bacterial Retention Study: This is the most central experiment for validating a sterilizing-grade filter. It is not just "testing whether the filter can stop bacteria"; it must demonstrate the filter's retention capability under simulated actual process conditions (including process fluid, pressure, temperature, filtration time, and so on). See Section 6.0 for details.

Bioburden: The types and number of microorganisms present per unit of material. It is not only "how many organisms" but also "which organisms", because different microorganisms differ in size and penetration ability, which affects sterilizing filtration performance.

Analogy

An integrity test is like checking the door lock before you leave home:

The principle of the Bubble Point Test is actually quite intuitive. Imagine a wet sponge: you blow air from one side, water fills all the small pores, and air cannot get through. As you gradually raise the air pressure, water is eventually pushed out of the largest pore, and you see bubbles begin to stream out steadily. That "critical pressure" is the bubble point.

The smaller the pore, the greater the pressure needed to push the liquid out. So a higher bubble point = a smaller maximum pore size = a tighter membrane. It is like testing a door lock: the more force needed to pry it open, the stronger the lock.

Key Notes

Capsule Filter vs Cartridge Filter: key differences:

  • Capsule Filter: A self-contained design with the membrane and housing integrated, usually single-use, which removes the need for cleaning and sterilization
  • Cartridge Filter: Used with a stainless steel housing; the housing is reusable and only the cartridge is replaced

In a CDMO environment, capsule filters are increasingly popular because they lower the risk of cross-contamination and save the effort of cleaning validation. However, they cost more and generate more waste. The choice depends on your product portfolio and production scale.

Key Notes: CPPs and Filtration

The definition of Critical Process Parameter (CPP) is very important: it is a "process parameter that has an impact on a CQA". In sterilizing filtration, typical CPPs include:

  • Differential pressure — too high may cause membrane deformation or microbial penetration
  • Filtration temperature — affects liquid viscosity and microbial activity
  • Filtration time — too long may allow microorganisms to proliferate on the membrane surface
  • Bioburden level — an excessive challenge level may exceed the retention capability of the membrane

Practice Questions

  1. How is the Bracketing Approach applied in sterilizing filtration validation? For example, if you have the same product in three different batch sizes (10 L, 50 L, 200 L), which conditions would you test?
  2. Why does the definition of Compatibility emphasize "no adverse interaction" rather than simply saying the membrane material is "chemically resistant"? Think about the difference between the two.
  3. Your CDMO site decides to move from cartridge filters to capsule filters. What factors need to be considered? List at least five aspects.
2.0 Glossary (D–F)

Original Text

Diffusive Flow
The movement of a dissolved gas across a liquid-wetted membrane based on concentration (e.g., gas pressure) differential.

Diffusive Flow Test
A test to determine the integrity of a filter based on a measurement of the diffusive flow rate of a gas through wetted filter membrane at a set pressure limit.
Note: “Diffusive Flow” and “Forward Flow” are generally synonymous terms, “diffusive flow” will be used in this document.

Double Filtration
Filtration through two filters configured in series whereby both filters are required to achieve a sterile effluent as determined by the product and process-specific bacterial retention validation study (i.e., both filters must meet post-use integrity test specifications).
Note: This is also referred to as “Additive Filtration”.

Downstream Side (of a Filter)
The filtrate or outlet side of the filter.

Effective Filtration Area (EFA)
The total surface area of the filter available to the process fluid.

Effluent
Fluid that flows out of a process step.

Endotoxin
The major constituent of the outer membranes of Gram-negative bacteria composed of lipid A, the core polysaccharide, and the O-antigen polysaccharide. Endotoxin is also known as lipopolysaccharide (LPS).

Extractable
A chemical component that is removed from a material by application of an artificial or exaggerated force (e.g., solvent, temperature, time).

Filter (noun)
A device used to remove particles from a fluid process stream that consists of a porous medium and a support structure.
Porous material through which a liquid or gas is passed to remove viable and nonviable particles (6).

Filter (verb)
To pass a fluid through a porous medium whereby viable and nonviable particles are removed from the fluid.

Filterability Test
A test to determine the suitability and sizing of a filter with a given fluid that typically involves measuring the rate at which a liquid can be filtered through a specific filter and the maximum filter capacity before the filter becomes clogged/fouled.

Filter Efficiency
A measurement of how well a filter retains particles. It is usually expressed as the percentage, or ratio, of the retention of particles of a specific size by a filter.

Filter Element
The basic filter unit from which cartridges or capsules are assembled.

Filtrate
Fluid that has passed through a filter.

Filtration
The process by which particles are removed from a fluid by passing the fluid through a porous material.

Flow Rate
The volumetric rate of flow of a solution, expressed in units of volume per time (e.g., L/min or gal/day).

Flux
The rate of filtrate flow divided by the membrane area.

Forward Flow Test
See “Diffusive Flow Test”.

Fouling
The result of solutes blinding or blocking membrane pores. It is observed as a decrease in the flux (at constant pressure) or an increase in the filtration differential pressure (at constant flux).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Double Filtration vs Redundant Filtration: These are the two concepts most easily confused in the industry, and they must be distinguished precisely.

  • Double Filtration: Two filters in series, and both must pass the post-use integrity test. This means a single filter cannot achieve sterilization on its own; the retention performance of the two filters must be "added together" to ensure sterility.
  • Redundant Filtration: Two filters in series, but it is sufficient for only one to pass the integrity test. The second is a "backup" and only needs to perform if the first fails.

This distinction is critical for batch disposition decisions: in Double Filtration, an integrity test failure of either filter = the batch may be unacceptable. In Redundant Filtration, it is a problem only if both fail.

Key Concepts: Extractable vs Leachable

Extractable vs Leachable:

  • Extractable: Chemical substances "forced out" of the membrane material under extreme conditions (strong solvents, high temperature, long duration), representing "what could possibly be released in the worst case"
  • Leachable: Chemical substances that migrate from the membrane into the drug solution under normal use conditions, representing "what actually comes into contact with the product"

E&L (Extractables & Leachables) studies are a necessary step in membrane selection. Extractables data are usually provided by the filter supplier, while leachables studies must be performed by the end user with the actual drug product.

Analogy

Fouling is like a clogged water pipe:

Imagine the water filter under your kitchen faucet. When first installed, the water flows smoothly (high flux), but as time goes on, more and more impurities build up on the filter element. You will notice one of two things:

  • If the water pressure stays the same, the flow becomes smaller (flux declines)
  • If you want the flow to stay the same, you need to raise the water pressure (differential pressure rises)

This is fouling (clogging/blocking). In pharmaceutical filtration, fouling affects not only efficiency but may also affect retention performance, because clogging can cause local pressure increases, potentially affecting the integrity of the membrane.

Key Notes

Flux vs Flow Rate: do not mix them up:

  • Flow Rate: The volume of flow per unit time (e.g., L/min), describing "how much flows overall"
  • Flux: The flow rate per unit membrane area (e.g., L/m²/h), describing "the efficiency per unit of membrane area"

Why does this distinction matter? Because you can raise the flow rate by increasing the EFA (effective filtration area), but the flux does not change as a result. Flux reflects the permeability of the membrane itself and the characteristics of the liquid. In Filterability Testing, flux is the key indicator for evaluating membrane selection.

Formula

Flux = Flow Rate / EFA

Example:
Flow Rate = 10 L/min
EFA = 0.5 m²

Flux = 10 / 0.5 = 20 L/m²/min
     = 1,200 L/m²/h (LMH)

LMH (liters per square meter per hour) is the flux unit most commonly used in the industry. When evaluating membranes from different suppliers, comparing flux values is more meaningful than comparing flow rates, because it removes the effect of differences in membrane area.

Practice Questions

  1. Your site needs to filter 500 L of drug solution per batch, and the Filterability Test shows a flux of 800 LMH for this product. If you want to complete filtration within 1 hour, what is the minimum EFA required?
  2. Under what circumstances would you choose Double Filtration rather than Redundant Filtration? Think from a risk management perspective.
  3. Why does Endotoxin appear in the glossary? Can sterilizing filtration remove endotoxin? If not, what method should be used?
2.0 Glossary (G–P)

Original Text

Gamma Irradiation
Ionizing radiation, generated by decay of a radioactive nucleus, that can be used to sterilize a material.

Gauge Pressure
Gauge pressure is the difference between a given fluid pressure and that of the atmosphere.

Hydrophilic
Literally “water loving”, a filter that will wet with aqueous solutions to allow flow at low pressure differential.

Hydrophobic
Literally “water fearing”, a filter that repels aqueous and other high surface tension fluids and therefore cannot be wetted unless subjected to a high-pressure differential. When prewetted with low surface tension fluid, such as alcohol, the membrane will allow water flow at low pressure differential.

Influent
Fluid that flows into a process step.
Note: This is also referred to as “Feed”.

Integrity Test
A nondestructive physical test that can be correlated to the bacterial retention capability of a filter/filter assembly (6).

Leachable
A chemical component that migrates from a contact surface into a drug product or process fluid during storage or normal use conditions.

Materials of Construction (MoC)
Polymers or other materials that make up the components of the filter.

Medium
In filtration, the porous material that retains particles as a fluid passes through during the process of filtration.

Membrane
A thin, microporous medium used to remove particles and microorganisms from a fluid stream under pressure.

Module
Filter element that is incorporated into a cartridge or capsule.

Multi-Round Housing
Filter housing designed to allow multiple filter elements installed in parallel to increase the effective filtration area (EFA).

Non-Fiber Releasing
Refers to a filter that does not shed fibers into the filtrate.

Particle
Any discrete unit of material structure; a discernible mass having an observable length, width, thickness, size and shape.

Particulate
Relating to, or occurring in the form of, particles.

Pore
The channel(s)/path(s) in a membrane through which a fluid may pass.

Porosity
The ratio of void volume to bulk volume of the filter media.

Prefilter
Any filter placed upstream of the sterilizing filter(s).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Hydrophilic vs Hydrophobic membranes: This is the most basic classification when selecting a membrane.

  • Hydrophilic membranes: Used for filtering aqueous solutions, including most injectables, ophthalmic solutions, and so on. Materials include PVDF (polyvinylidene fluoride) and PES (polyethersulfone). They can be wetted directly with water and pass liquid at low pressure
  • Hydrophobic membranes: Used for gas filtration (e.g., vent filters) or filtration of non-aqueous solvents. Materials include PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene). They cannot be wetted directly by water and must first be pre-wetted with a low surface tension liquid (e.g., alcohol)

Key point: Whether a membrane is hydrophilic or hydrophobic determines which liquids it can filter, how the integrity test is performed, and which wetting fluid is used.

Key Concepts: Integrity Test

The definition of an integrity test is worth careful reading: "a non-destructive physical test that can be correlated to bacterial retention capability". A few key words:

  • Non-destructive — the membrane can still be used after the test, unlike the Bacterial Retention Study, which destroys the membrane
  • Physical test — based on physical parameters such as pressure and flow, not a microbiological test
  • Correlatable — the test result must be validated to establish a correspondence with actual bacterial retention capability

There are three common integrity test methods: the Bubble Point Test, the Diffusive Flow Test, and the Water Intrusion Test (used for hydrophobic membranes).

Analogy

Hydrophilic vs hydrophobic is like the two sides of a raincoat:

Imagine a high-quality raincoat. The outer layer (hydrophobic) makes rain roll off in beads without soaking into the fabric. The inner layer (hydrophilic) absorbs sweat so the wearer feels dry.

Sterilizing-grade membranes work on the same principle. A hydrophilic membrane "welcomes" aqueous solutions (like the inner layer of the raincoat absorbing sweat), while a hydrophobic membrane "repels" water (like the outer layer shedding rain) and needs special treatment before water can pass.

In practice, your injectables (aqueous solutions) will almost always use hydrophilic membranes. Hydrophobic membranes are often used for vent filtration in the process, to keep outside microorganisms from entering the sterile area through humid air.

Key Notes

The strategic value of a Prefilter:

A prefilter is defined as "any filter placed upstream of a sterilizing filter". This seemingly simple definition carries important strategic meaning:

  • Protects the sterilizing-grade membrane — removes large particles and high bioburden first, extending the service life of the sterilizing-grade membrane
  • Reduces filtration risk — lessens the retention load on the sterilizing-grade membrane and lowers the risk of penetration
  • Reduces cost — prefilters are usually much cheaper than sterilizing-grade membranes

Practical advice for CDMOs: do not skimp on prefilters. A good prefilter can extend the life of your sterilizing-grade membrane by 2-5 times, and the overall filtration cost ends up lower.

Practice Questions

  1. Your process uses a hydrophilic PES membrane for sterilizing filtration of an aqueous solution. If you also want to install a sterilizing-grade filter on the vent of the storage tank, what type of membrane should you choose, and why?
  2. A multi-round housing can increase the EFA, but what potential risks arise in practice from using multiple cartridges in parallel? Think about the complexity of integrity testing.
  3. A membrane with higher porosity usually has a higher flow rate, but does this necessarily mean its retention performance is worse? Why?
2.0 Glossary (P–X)

Original Text

Pressure
Force applied per unit area usually expressed as psi, mbar, kPa or kg/cm².

Back Pressure
Pressure applied downstream of a filter or other piece of equipment, via restriction in flow.

Differential Pressure
The difference in pressure between the upstream (feed or influent) and downstream (effluent) sides of the filter. (May be modified with the following terms: applied differential pressure, available differential pressure, clean differential pressure, dirty differential pressure, initial differential pressure, or maximum differential pressure.)
Note: This is also referred to as “Delta P (ΔP)”, “Psid”, or “Pressure drop”.

Inlet Pressure
The applied pressure entering the upstream side of the filter.
Note: This is also referred to as “Influent”, “Upstream Pressure” or “Line Pressure”.

Outlet Pressure
The pressure exiting the downstream side of the filter.
Note: This is also referred to as “Effluent Pressure” or “Downstream Pressure”.

Redundant Filtration
A type of serial filtration where a second sterilizing filter is used as a backup in the event of an integrity failure of the primary sterilizing filter.

Serial Filtration
Filtration through two or more filters of the same or decreasing pore size, one after the other.

Sterilization
Validated process used to render a product free of viable microorganisms.
Note: In a sterilization process, microbiological death or reduction is described by an exponential function. Therefore, the number of microorganisms that survive a sterilization process can be expressed in terms of probability. While the probability may be reduced to a very low number, it can never be reduced to zero.

Surface Tension
The tendency of the surface of a liquid to contract to the smallest area possible under defined conditions. It is expressed as dynes per centimeter.

Surfactant
A soluble compound that reduces the surface tension of a liquid or reduces interfacial tension between two liquids (causing formation of micelles), or between a liquid and a solid.

Throughput
The amount of solution that passes through a filter. It is described as volume through the membrane area.
Note: This is also referred to as “Capacity”.

Upstream
The influent, or inlet side of the filter.

Validation
A documented program that provides a high degree of assurance that a specific process, method or system will consistently produce a result meeting predetermined acceptance criteria.

X-ray Irradiation
Ionizing radiation, generated by an electron beam accelerator coupled with a metal target, that can be used to sterilize a material.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts: The Pressure Terminology System

Pressure terms in filtration can be confusing. Let us build a clear framework:

  • Inlet Pressure: The pressure on the upstream side entering the filter, the "driving force" pushing liquid through the membrane
  • Outlet Pressure: The pressure on the downstream side leaving the filter
  • Back Pressure: Resistance caused by restrictions in the downstream piping
  • Differential Pressure, ΔP: Inlet Pressure - Outlet Pressure = the effective pressure that actually drives filtration

Remember the formula: ΔP = Pinlet - Poutlet

The larger the ΔP, the faster the liquid passes through the membrane, but the greater the mechanical stress that may be placed on the membrane. The maximum allowable ΔP for most sterilizing-grade membranes is between 3 and 5 bar.

Key Concepts: The Probabilistic Nature of Sterilization

The glossary definition of Sterilization carries an extremely important note: microbial death follows an exponential function; the probability of survival can be reduced to a very low level, but it never equals zero.

This is why the pharmacopoeias use the concept of SAL (Sterility Assurance Level):

  • Terminal Sterilization: SAL = 10-6 (one in a million products may be non-sterile)
  • Sterilizing Filtration: Because the mechanism is different (retention rather than kill), the SAL concept is not used directly; instead, the Bacterial Retention Study together with the Integrity Test is used to ensure the retention capability of the membrane

Analogy

A filtration pressure system is like tap water plumbing:

Imagine the water system in your home. The height of the water tower creates the "inlet pressure", and the valve before the faucet and the bends in the pipe create the "back pressure". The speed at which water actually flows from the faucet depends on the "differential pressure" between the two.

If you open several faucets at once (increasing downstream resistance = increasing back pressure), the flow at each faucet becomes smaller. Sterilizing filtration is the same: if there are too many restrictions in the downstream piping (such as small-bore tubing or closed valves), ΔP will change and affect filtration performance.

Key Notes

The strategic value of Redundant Filtration:

For a CDMO, Redundant Filtration is a risk management strategy. When a batch has very high product value (such as a cell therapy product or an expensive biologic), if the only sterilizing-grade membrane fails the post-use integrity test, the entire batch may have to be scrapped.

The cost of adding a second, redundant filter may be only a few thousand US dollars, yet it can protect a batch worth hundreds of thousands or even millions of dollars.

The practical impact of Surface Tension: If your drug product contains a surfactant, it lowers the surface tension of the liquid, which may affect the integrity test result, because both the bubble point and the diffusive flow values depend on the surface tension of the wetting fluid. This is why integrity test validation must be performed using product-wetted filters.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: batch disposition and integrity test failure:

Suppose you are manufacturing a batch of monoclonal antibody injectable worth US$2 million for a client. After filtration, the post-use integrity test of the sterilizing-grade membrane fails.

  • If you have only a single filter: The batch may have to be scrapped or subjected to a deviation investigation, with heavy losses
  • If you have Redundant Filtration: The second filter passes the integrity test, so the batch can still be released, while a deviation investigation is started to understand why the first filter failed

This is the practical meaning of "high degree of assurance" in the definition of Validation: not an absolute 100% guarantee, but reducing risk to an acceptable level through a systematic strategy.

Practice Questions

  1. The definition of Sterilization states that "the probability of survival can be reduced to a very low level but never equals zero". Then why do we still say "sterile product"? Is this expression scientifically accurate?
  2. Your process drug solution contains 0.05% Polysorbate 80 (a commonly used surfactant). What impact would this have on the integrity test, and how would you handle it?
  3. What are the inclusion relationships among Serial Filtration, Double Filtration, and Redundant Filtration? Try drawing a logic diagram.
2.1 Abbreviations

Original Text

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts: In-Depth Reading of Key Abbreviations

These abbreviations run throughout the entire technical report, so let us look closely at a few of the most important ones:

PUPSIT (Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Testing): This has been one of the most hotly debated topics among regulators in recent years. EU GMP Annex 1 (effective 2023) requires integrity testing of sterilizing-grade filters after sterilization and before use. The abbreviation itself tells you the timing of the test: Pre-Use + Post-Sterilization.

CCS (Contamination Control Strategy): This is a core concept introduced by EU GMP Annex 1. Sterilizing filtration does not exist in isolation; it is one element of the overall CCS. Your filtration strategy must be consistent with the larger contamination control framework.

SUS (Single-Use Systems): More and more CDMOs are adopting single-use systems, including single-use filters. This changes the need for SIP (single-use systems are usually pre-sterilized by gamma irradiation or X-ray irradiation and do not require SIP).

Analogy

PUPSIT is like checking the gas before you leave home:

Every time you leave home, you check whether the gas is turned off. It is not because you distrust the last time you turned it off, but because something you are unaware of (a gas leak, a loose valve, and so on) may have happened in between. PUPSIT follows exactly the same logic: after the membrane has been sterilized by SIP, its integrity is tested again just before actual use, to confirm that the sterilization process did not damage the membrane.

The controversy over this test is that it adds operational complexity and sterility risk (the test itself requires manipulation of the piping), but regulators consider that the confirmation value it brings outweighs the additional risk.

Key Notes

The validation significance of NOR (Normal Operating Range):

NOR defines the range over which process parameters fluctuate during normal production. In sterilizing filtration validation, you need to demonstrate that the membrane still achieves sterilization at the boundary conditions of the NOR (and even under more extreme worst-case conditions).

For example: if your normal filtration differential pressure (ΔP) range is 0.5-2.0 bar, your Bacterial Retention Study should be performed at a differential pressure of at least 2.0 bar or higher to cover the worst case.

TOC (Total Organic Carbon): Used to assess residues on equipment surfaces after cleaning. When using reusable stainless steel filter housings, TOC testing is a key indicator for cleaning validation. If you switch to SUS, TOC cleaning validation is not required, which is a major advantage of single-use systems.

Practice Questions

  1. PUPSIT is required by EU GMP Annex 1, but what is its position under the FDA regulatory framework today? If your CDMO supplies both the European and US markets, how would you develop a PUPSIT strategy?
  2. If your site is evaluating a move from a conventional stainless steel system to SUS (single-use systems), how would this affect the need for SIP, TOC cleaning validation, and PUPSIT, respectively?
  3. Where does sterilizing filtration sit within the CCS (Contamination Control Strategy)? How does it relate to other control measures such as environmental monitoring, personnel controls, and equipment cleaning?

Section Summary

  • Terminological precision is the foundation of quality: The TR 26 glossary ensures a consistent definition of every concept throughout the document. In day-to-day CDMO work, the precise use of terminology directly affects communication efficiency and documentation quality
  • Core distinction: Double Filtration (both filters must pass the integrity test) vs Redundant Filtration (the second filter is a backup) — this difference directly affects batch disposition decisions
  • Extractable vs Leachable: The former is the "worst case" under extreme conditions, while the latter is the "actual case" under normal use conditions. E&L studies are a necessary step in membrane selection
  • The pressure terminology system: ΔP = Pinlet - Poutlet is the effective pressure driving filtration. Monitoring changes in ΔP can indicate the degree of fouling
  • Integrity Test: A non-destructive physical test correlated with bacterial retention capability. PUPSIT is a new requirement of EU GMP Annex 1 and stands for "pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing"
  • Hydrophilic vs Hydrophobic: Hydrophilic membranes are used for aqueous solutions (most injectables), and hydrophobic membranes for gas filtration and non-aqueous solvents. Choosing the correct type is the first step in filtration design
3

Section 3: How Filters Work

p11-p16

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2008) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p7 - p10

Learning Objectives of This Chapter

  • Understand the two retention mechanisms in liquid filtration, Size Exclusion and Adsorptive Sequestration, and why both operate at the same time
  • Recognize the true pore structure of sterilizing-grade membranes (a sponge-like maze rather than simple cylindrical pores) and how it affects retention performance
  • Grasp the definition and meaning of the ASTM F838 standard test, and distinguish the essential differences among the three key terms "0.2 µm Rated Filter", "Sterilizing-Grade Filter" and "Sterilizing Filter"
  • Understand how modern sterilizing-grade membranes have evolved, and how the full list of quality requirements shapes a CDMO's filter selection strategy
  • Build the core principle that "passing a standard test does not equal a guarantee of a sterile process", and understand why every application must be validated independently
3.0 How Filters Work

Original Text

Liquid filters work through a combination of size-exclusion (involving surface screening as well as entrapment in the filter matrix depth) and adsorptive sequestration. The filtration mechanisms are well described in the literature (references 3, 4, 6, 7, 9, 11, 21, 22).

Sterilizing-grade filters have a sponge-like pore structure; they are not simple screens with cylindrical pores of a defined size. As a consequence, their retention characteristics are dependent upon a number of factors, including differential pressure, flow rate, the number of particles or microorganisms to be retained (bioburden), binding-site saturation, surface tension, pH, and ionic strength.

For very small microorganisms that can grow, shrink, deform, and divide, retention becomes probabilistic rather than absolute. Accordingly, each application of a sterilizing filter must be validated to demonstrate its suitability for the intended use.

"Sterilizing-grade filters have a sponge-like pore structure; they are not simple screens with cylindrical pores of a defined size."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Size Exclusion: This is the most intuitive retention mechanism. A particle or microorganism that is larger than the pore size of the membrane is physically stopped. It has two forms:

  • Surface Screening: The particle is stopped at the membrane surface and cannot enter the pores
  • Entrapment in Filter Matrix Depth: The particle enters the multilayer structure of the membrane and becomes lodged in the tortuous pathways

Adsorptive Sequestration: This is a less intuitive but equally important mechanism. Microorganisms or particles are "stuck" to the internal surfaces of the membrane by surface forces such as electrostatic attraction and van der Waals forces, even when they are small enough to pass through the pores in theory.

Key point: the success of sterilizing filtration does not rely on "small enough pores" alone, but on the combined action of the two mechanisms.

Analogy

Security gate vs plainclothes police:

Picture an airport security system. Size Exclusion works like the security gate: anything above a certain size is stopped outright and cannot pass. It is passive screening based on physical size.

Adsorptive Sequestration, on the other hand, works like the plainclothes police: even if a suspicious person is small enough to get through the gate, the officers will stop that person by "recognizing telltale features". It is active retention based on surface characteristics.

A truly safe airport needs both. In the same way, truly effective sterilizing filtration needs the double protection of size exclusion and adsorptive sequestration.

Analogy: The Sponge Maze

Sponge maze vs simple sieve:

Many people instinctively think of a filter membrane as a window screen at home: neat rows of round holes that keep large insects out while small air molecules pass through. But TR26 tells us clearly that the structure of a sterilizing-grade membrane is more like a sponge.

Imagine cutting a sponge open: inside are countless interconnected, irregular cavities and winding channels. For a microorganism to "get through", it would have to find a path in this three-dimensional maze that is wide enough from start to finish. That is almost impossible, because it will always be stuck at some bend or adsorbed onto the pore wall at some narrow point.

This is why we say the "0.2 µm" of a membrane is not an exact pore dimension but a functional rating.

Key Notes

"Probabilistic retention" is the most important concept!

TR26 uses the word "probabilistic" rather than "absolute". This means:

  • No filter can guarantee 100% retention of all microorganisms
  • Microorganisms are alive: they grow, shrink, change shape and divide, all of which affect retention
  • Retention performance is affected by many process conditions, such as differential pressure, flow rate, bioburden, pH and ionic strength

Implication for a CDMO: You cannot simply buy a filter labeled "0.2 µm" and assume everything is fine. You must validate with your own product and under your own process conditions. This is why filter validation is a hard regulatory requirement.

The Seven Factors Affecting Retention

TR26 lists the key factors that influence retention characteristics. Let us go through why each one matters:

  • Differential Pressure: The higher the differential pressure, the faster the liquid passes through and the shorter the contact time between microorganisms and the membrane, so adsorptive retention may decrease
  • Flow Rate: Similar to differential pressure, a high flow rate reduces contact time
  • Bioburden: The more microorganisms present, the faster the binding sites become saturated, and the easier it is for later microorganisms to "slip through"
  • Binding-Site Saturation: The "plainclothes police" of adsorptive retention are limited in number; once all of them are busy, new suspicious individuals may get through
  • Surface Tension: Affects the wetting behavior between the liquid and the membrane, and therefore retention
  • pH: Affects the surface charge of the microorganism and the membrane, changing the effect of electrostatic adsorption
  • Ionic Strength: Ions in the solution shield electrostatic interactions, so high ionic strength may reduce adsorptive retention

Practice Questions

  1. If the pH of your drug formulation is adjusted from 7.0 to 4.0, how might this affect the filter validation results? Why does revalidation become necessary?
  2. Why does a drug substance solution with high bioburden need more careful evaluation of filtration performance than one with low bioburden? Analyze it from the standpoint of binding-site saturation.
  3. A CDMO client says: "We use a 0.2 µm filter, so filter validation is not needed." How would you respond? Which concepts from this section would you use to persuade them?
  4. If the filter were a simple sieve structure like a window screen (rather than a sponge maze), do you think the "adsorptive sequestration" mechanism would still exist? Why?
3.1 Pore Size Rating and Filter Nomenclature

Original Text

Currently, sterilizing-grade filters are defined not by a physical pore size, but by the ability to retain a minimum challenge of 107 CFU/cm2 of Brevundimonas diminuta (ATCC 19146) when tested per ASTM F838.

Table 3.1-1🔍 below defines three related but distinct terms that are commonly used — and commonly confused — in the context of sterilizing filtration.

Table 3.1-1🔍: Filter Nomenclature

The relationships among these three terms can be visualized as a set of concentric circles (Figure 3.1-1🔍), where the outermost circle represents the broadest category (“0.2 µm Rated Filters”) and the innermost circle represents the most restrictive claim (“Sterilizing Filter”).

Figure 3.1-1🔍: Relationship Among Filter Terms (Venn Diagram)

0.2 µm Rated Filter
Sterilizing-Grade Filter
Sterilizing Filter
Broadest (outer) → Most restrictive (inner)

Figure 3.1-1🔍: Concentric relationship among the three filter terms. The outermost circle (blue) represents all 0.2 µm rated filters — the broadest and most ambiguous category. The middle circle (green) represents sterilizing-grade filters that have passed ASTM F838. The innermost circle (red) represents sterilizing filters validated for a specific application.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

ASTM F838 standard test: This is the "gold standard" that defines a sterilizing-grade filter. The test challenges the filter with Brevundimonas diminuta (B. diminuta), one of the smallest known culturable bacteria, at a concentration of at least 107 CFU (colony forming units) per square centimeter. If the filtrate is completely sterile, the filter can be called a Sterilizing-Grade Filter.

Why B. diminuta? Because it is one of the smallest known culturable bacteria (about 0.3 µm in diameter) and is regarded as the "most stringent challenge organism". If a filter can retain even this organism, there is more confidence that it will retain larger microorganisms.

The essential differences among the three terms:

  • 0.2 µm Rated Filter: The broadest and vaguest term. It may have passed ASTM F838, or it may simply meet a standard that the manufacturer defined itself. Do not assume it is sterilizing grade!
  • Sterilizing-Grade Filter: A filter that has explicitly passed ASTM F838. This is a statement about the filter itself, verified by the filter manufacturer under standard test conditions.
  • Sterilizing Filter: A filter that has been validated in a specific application to produce a sterile filtrate. This is a statement about function and requires process validation by the end user.

Analogy: The Driving Test

ASTM F838 = the driving test:

The ASTM F838 test is like a driving test. Passing it shows that you have "basic driving ability", which corresponds to the concept of a Sterilizing-Grade Filter.

But holding a license does not mean you can safely drive any vehicle under any road conditions. You might run into trouble driving on snow, or be unable to handle a large truck. Truly safe driving has to be demonstrated in a specific context, which corresponds to a Sterilizing Filter: it must be validated with your specific drug product and under your specific process conditions.

Analogy: The University Degree

0.2 µm rating = a university degree:

"0.2 µm Rated" is like a university degree: it tells you the person "graduated", but not which university they graduated from (a top school or a diploma mill?), and it does not guarantee they can do a particular job well.

Likewise, the figure "0.2 µm" is only a rating label, and the test standard behind it may be the rigorous ASTM F838 or a lenient standard set by the manufacturer. This is why TR26 specifically reminds us: seeing "0.2 µm" does not allow you to automatically assume it is a sterilizing-grade filter.

Key Notes

The "0.2 µm Rated" trap: a CDMO must stay alert!

This is one of the most important warnings in TR26. In everyday communication, many people (including suppliers and manufacturing staff) use these three terms interchangeably. In a regulated environment, however, the three have a strict hierarchy:

  • Not every "0.2 µm Rated Filter" is a "Sterilizing-Grade Filter"
  • Not every "Sterilizing-Grade Filter" is a "Sterilizing Filter" (even if it passed ASTM F838, it may not achieve sterilizing performance with your specific product)

During a regulatory inspection: if an inspector asks "How do you know this filter is sterilizing grade?", the correct answer is not "because it is labeled 0.2 µm", but "because the filter supplier provided ASTM F838 test data, and we have completed filter validation for our specific product and process conditions."

Figure Analysis: Venn Diagram

Reading the Venn diagram (concentric circles) in Figure 3.1-1🔍:

The figure uses concentric circles to show how the three terms are nested:

  • Outer circle (blue), 0.2 µm Rated Filter: The largest and vaguest set. Every filter labeled "0.2 µm" is here, but quality and validation standards vary widely.
  • Middle circle (green), Sterilizing-Grade Filter: Only filters that have passed the ASTM F838 test can enter this circle. This is the filter manufacturer's commitment on "product quality".
  • Inner circle (red), Sterilizing Filter: The most stringent claim. Only filters that have undergone full process validation in a specific application belong here. This is the user's demonstration of "process performance".

Core message: From the outer circle to the inner circle, the requirements become stricter and the scope smaller. Your goal is to make sure that every filter you use in CDMO production sits in the innermost circle.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your company has taken on a biologics filling project for a new client. The client supplied a document specifying a "0.2 µm membrane filter" from a particular brand for sterilizing filtration.

What you should do:

  1. Confirm whether the filter is sterilizing grade: Request the ASTM F838 B. diminuta challenge test report from the filter supplier
  2. Perform filter validation: Conduct bacterial retention validation with the client's actual product under your actual process conditions (differential pressure, temperature, filtration time, bioburden level)
  3. Establish the filter integrity test criteria: Set the acceptance criteria for your bubble point test or diffusion test, and correlate them with bacterial retention capability

Practice Questions

  1. In the definition of "0.2 µm Rated Filter" in Table 3.1-1🔍, the phrase "or other criteria established by the filter manufacturer" appears. Why is this phrase a major risk signal?
  2. Could a filter that passed the ASTM F838 test (a Sterilizing-Grade Filter) be "downgraded" to a failing status when filtering a drug solution containing a high concentration of surfactant? Why?
  3. In the Venn diagram, who must make the step from "Sterilizing-Grade Filter" to "Sterilizing Filter": the manufacturer or the user? Why?
  4. If a filter passes its integrity test, but the filter validation used water instead of the actual drug product, would you consider this a valid validation?
3.2 Evolution of the Sterilizing Filter

Original Text

While the basic function of a sterilizing filter — the removal of microorganisms from a liquid — has remained unchanged, the performance requirements and quality expectations placed on modern sterilizing-grade filters have evolved considerably. Today's sterilizing-grade filters are expected to meet a comprehensive set of requirements that go well beyond simple bacterial retention.

Modern sterilizing-grade filters have evolved to include the following attributes and requirements:

  • Steam sterilization resistance — the ability to withstand repeated steam sterilization cycles (typically 121°C for 30 minutes or more) without loss of integrity or retention performance
  • Irradiation resistance — compatibility with gamma irradiation sterilization, particularly important for single-use (disposable) filter assemblies
  • Minimal extractables — low levels of extractable substances to minimize the risk of product contamination and ensure patient safety
  • Mechanical resistance — structural integrity under the mechanical stresses of installation, pressurization, and operation
  • Low unspecific adsorption — minimal non-specific binding of product components (particularly proteins) to the filter membrane, to maintain product yield and quality
  • Optimal throughputs — the ability to process the required volume of product within an acceptable timeframe
  • Flow rates — consistent and predictable flow performance under defined operating conditions
  • Robust integrity testing — reliable and reproducible integrity test methods (e.g., Bubble Point, Diffusion Test) that correlate with bacterial retention
  • Full qualification documentation — comprehensive documentation packages including validation guides, extractables data, bacterial retention data, and regulatory support files
  • Individual process validation — support for end-user validation of the filter in the specific application, with the actual product and process conditions
  • 100% integrity tested by manufacturer — every filter unit is integrity tested before shipment to ensure no defective units reach the end user
  • Transportation and shelf life validation — demonstrated stability of filter performance throughout storage and shipping conditions
  • Membrane casting batch consistency — manufacturing controls to ensure lot-to-lot consistency of the filter membrane

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

"Sterilizing" is only the minimum threshold:

In this subsection TR26 conveys a very important message: the role of a modern sterilizing-grade filter goes far beyond "simply retaining microorganisms". It must meet thirteen quality and performance requirements at the same time. These fall into four broad categories:

  • Resistance: Resistance to steam sterilization, resistance to radiation exposure, mechanical strength
  • Product Compatibility: Low extractables, low unspecific adsorption
  • Performance: Flow rate, throughput, reliability of integrity testing
  • Quality System: Complete documentation, process validation support, 100% testing at release, shipping and shelf-life validation, batch consistency

This means that choosing a filter is not just about "whether it can filter", but a comprehensive decision involving quality, regulatory, process and commercial factors.

Analogy

Early filters vs modern filters = early mobile phones vs modern smartphones:

Early sterilizing filters were like early mobile phones: all they had to do was "make calls" (retain bacteria). A modern sterilizing-grade filter is like a smartphone: besides basic calling, it needs a camera (integrity testing), GPS (document traceability), water resistance (steam sterilization resistance), strengthened glass (mechanical strength), long battery life (shelf-life validation), stable operating system updates (batch consistency)......

If you were buying a phone today, would you decide based only on "can it make calls"? Of course not. In the same way, when choosing a sterilizing-grade filter you should not look only at "can it retain B. diminuta".

Key Notes: CDMO Filter Selection Strategy

Requirements of particular importance to a CDMO:

  • Low Unspecific Adsorption: For biologics (protein drugs, antibody drugs), if the filter "eats" the active ingredient in the product, your yield drops. This directly affects cost and customer satisfaction. Choosing a low-protein-binding membrane material (such as PES, polyethersulfone) is key.
  • Minimal Extractables: Chemicals released by the filter can become impurities in the drug product. Regulations require you to know the type and amount of extractables and to demonstrate that they are harmless to the product and to patient safety.
  • 100% Integrity Tested by Manufacturer: This means every filter shipped has been integrity tested, with no "escapees". This is the most basic supplier quality expectation for a CDMO.
  • Membrane Casting Batch Consistency: If filter quality varies from batch to batch, your filtration process parameters need constant adjustment, which adds validation burden and risk.

Decision Hierarchy

When selecting a filter, keep the PDA order of priorities in mind:

1. Sterility Assurance
↓
2. Product CQAs
↓
3. Business & Flexibility

For example: a filter has a very high flow rate (good for commercial efficiency) but also high protein adsorption (affecting the product CQA); you must sacrifice flow rate to protect product quality. And if a filter is cheap but its integrity test is unreliable, sterility assurance (the highest priority) is threatened, and that filter must never be used.

Formula: Estimating Protein Adsorption Loss

When evaluating low unspecific adsorption, a simple estimate:

Protein loss = adsorption density (µg/cm²) × effective filtration area (cm²)

Example:
  Adsorption density: 5 µg/cm² (typical PVDF membrane)
  Filtration area: 13,000 cm² (10-inch cartridge filter)
  Loss = 5 × 13,000 = 65,000 µg = 65 mg

If the total amount of your protein drug is only 500 mg:
  Loss rate = 65 / 500 = 13%

If you switch to a low-adsorption PES membrane (adsorption density: 0.5 µg/cm²):
  Loss = 0.5 × 13,000 = 6,500 µg = 6.5 mg
  Loss rate = 6.5 / 500 = 1.3%

Conclusion: the choice of membrane material can reduce protein loss from 13% to 1.3%

Practical Application

CDMO supplier evaluation scenario:

You are evaluating two filter suppliers (A and B). Both claim to supply Sterilizing-Grade Filters, and both passed ASTM F838. You should use the thirteen requirements of Section 3.2 as a "scorecard" for comparison:

  • Supplier A provides a complete Extractables report and Validation Guide, but has radiation exposure resistance data only for its main product line
  • Supplier B is 15% cheaper, but provides no shipping validation data, and its integrity test values vary considerably between batches

According to the decision hierarchy, supplier B's batch consistency problem may affect sterility assurance (the highest priority), so it should be evaluated with caution even though the price is more attractive. Supplier A's gap in radiation exposure data may not be a fatal flaw if your process does not use gamma-irradiated single-use systems.

Practice Questions

  1. Of the thirteen requirements listed in Section 3.2, which three do you think have the most direct financial impact on CDMO operations? Why?
  2. "100% Integrity Tested by Manufacturer" means the manufacturer integrity tested every filter. Why, then, does the user (the CDMO) still need to perform a Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Test (PUPSIT) before use?
  3. If a new filter supplier provides only an ASTM F838 test report but lacks Extractables data and Transportation Validation data, what action would you recommend to your quality unit?
  4. If "Membrane Casting Batch Consistency" is poorly controlled, how would it have a knock-on effect on the CDMO's process validation strategy and routine operations? List at least three possible impacts.

Section Summary

  • Dual retention mechanisms: Sterilizing filtration relies on both Size Exclusion (surface screening plus entrapment in filter matrix depth) and Adsorptive Sequestration. Like the security gate working with plainclothes police, neither can be left out.
  • Sponge maze structure: The pore structure of a sterilizing-grade membrane is a sponge-like three-dimensional maze, not simple cylindrical sieve pores. This is why "0.2 µm" is a functional rating and not an exact physical pore dimension.
  • Retention is probabilistic: Because microorganisms grow, shrink, change shape and divide, and because retention is affected by differential pressure, flow rate, bioburden, pH, ionic strength and other factors, it is probabilistic rather than absolute.
  • Always distinguish the three terms: "0.2 µm Rated Filter" (the broadest and potentially vague), "Sterilizing-Grade Filter" (a statement about the filter itself that has passed ASTM F838), "Sterilizing Filter" (a functional statement validated in a specific application). From the outside in, the requirements increase layer by layer.
  • Passing a standard test does not equal guaranteed sterility: ASTM F838 is the "driving test"; passing it shows basic competence, but whether you can drive safely on your specific "road" (product, process conditions) requires independent process validation.
  • The thirteen all-round requirements of modern filters: From steam sterilization resistance, low extractables and low protein binding to 100% release testing, batch consistency and shipping validation, selecting a modern sterilizing-grade filter is a comprehensive decision involving quality, regulatory, process and commercial factors.
  • Core takeaway for CDMOs: Never make a decision based on the "0.2 µm" label alone. Complete supplier evaluation, rigorous filter validation, and adherence to the decision hierarchy (sterility assurance > product CQA > business flexibility) are the right path to patient safety and regulatory compliance.
4

Section 4: Filter Qualification

p17-p22

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025): Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p11 - p22

Learning Objectives

  • Understand how responsibilities are divided between the filter manufacturer and the user for filter qualification and filter validation
  • Recognize the structural features and typical applications of the different filter formats (cartridge, capsule, disc)
  • Understand the definitions and sources of extractables and leachables, and their impact on drug product quality
  • Master the key considerations for chemical compatibility, biocompatibility, operational ranges, and shelf life
  • Learn the advantages and risk assessment considerations of integrating single-use systems into sterilizing filtration operations
4.0 Filter Qualification, Design, and Characterization

Original Text

Filter manufacturers typically provide the information discussed in this section, which should be considered by the end user for their risk assessment when selecting a sterilizing-grade filter. Since bacterial retention studies and filter integrity testing are important components of manufacturer-supplied information, those topics are covered separately in Section 6.0 and Section 7.0, respectively.

Sterilizing-grade filters are available in a variety of sizes, configurations (e.g., flat sheets, capsules, cartridges) and membrane chemistries so that the filter user can select the most appropriate filter for a particular application. Commonly used membrane chemistries include polyvinylidene fluoride (PVDF), polysulfone (PS), polyethersulfone (PES), nylon, cellulose esters, polytetrafluoroethylene (PTFE), polyester and polypropylene. Not only do different chemistries impart differences in flow characteristics and filtration performance, but they also affect the levels of filter extractables and leachables, the thermal and physical properties of the filter, and the interactions with the process stream (as determined via compatibility and adsorption-testing). Once the membrane is manufactured into its final format, it is evaluated by the filter manufacturer with respect to flow rate and pressure drop, operating limits of temperature and pressure, extractables, compatibility, cleanliness, sterilization conditions, integrity, and bacterial retention applying defined test conditions.

Since sterilizing-grade filters are a critical part of any aseptic process, a significant amount of testing and documentation is conducted by filter manufacturers to qualify their filters. Filter support documentation may include a validation or qualification guide, FDA Drug Master File (DMF), product literature, specification sheets, technical bulletins and application notes. Pharmaceutical-grade filters are also often accompanied by an individual certificate listing the filter part, lot numbers and release criteria. The supporting documentation from filter manufacturers can help the end users in their assessment for qualifying filters and supporting regulatory inquiries.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Sterilizing-Grade Filter: A membrane filter with a pore size of 0.2 micron (or smaller) that has been validated to completely retain microorganisms (in particular Brevundimonas diminuta, at a challenge level of at least 107 CFU/cm2). It is the most critical last line of defense in aseptic processing.

Filter Qualification: A series of standardized tests performed by the filter manufacturer to demonstrate that the filter is suitable for pharmaceutical applications. It includes bacterial retention, integrity testing, extractables, biocompatibility, and more.

Membrane Chemistry: Different polymers (PVDF, PES, nylon, etc.) each have their own flow rate characteristics, chemical compatibility, and extractables profile. The choice must take the process requirements into account.

Analogy

Choosing a filter membrane is like choosing a water purifier cartridge. There are different types on the market (activated carbon, reverse osmosis, hollow fiber), each with different filtration performance, flow rate, and service life. You would not buy on price alone -- you would consider the water quality (characteristics of the process fluid), the water volume (batch size), and whether the filter might release an odor afterwards (extractables). Likewise, the technical documents supplied by the filter manufacturer are like the test report for a water purifier, but you still have to test it on your own plumbing to confirm it works.

Key Notes

The division of responsibilities matters: The filter manufacturer is responsible for "qualification", demonstrating the quality and performance of the product itself; the filter user is responsible for "validation", demonstrating that the filter still achieves the intended sterilizing performance under the user's actual process conditions. The manufacturer's documentation is the foundation, but it cannot replace the user's own validation work.

FDA DMF (Drug Master File): A confidential document that the filter manufacturer submits to the FDA, containing complete manufacturing and quality information. A drug manufacturer can reference this DMF in an NDA/BLA submission and does not need to provide these details itself.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does this section stress that bacterial retention and integrity testing will be discussed separately in later sections (Section 6.0 and Section 7.0)? What does this imply about the standing of these two tests within overall filter validation?
  2. Suppose you are a quality professional at a CDMO and your client specifies a particular brand of PES filter. Which key documents would you request from the filter manufacturer to start your evaluation?
4.1 Filter Qualification and Validation

Original Text

Filter manufacturers typically publish results of tests performed according to applicable compendial methods to qualify the filter as suitable for pharmaceutical applications. This qualification documentation supports, but does not replace, performance qualification as a part of process validation conducted by the filter user. Table 4.1-1🔍 lists qualification and lot-release tests on membranes and devices commonly performed by manufacturers and validated by filter users.

Table 4.1-1🔍 Qualification and Validation Recommendations

L = Filter Manufacturer's Lot Release Criteria | Q = Filter Manufacturer's Qualification | V = Process Validation Testing (Rationale can be provided for scale-down testing) | E = Evaluate the Need for Additional Testing, Document Technical Assessment | * = Typically Using Water

Further details of the tests listed in Table 4.1-1🔍 are provided below:

  • Bacterial retention of a filter is initially qualified by the filter manufacturer in saline lactose broth (SLB), water or another suitable carrier fluid and then again by the filter user under process conditions for critical sterilizing-filtration applications. In each case, 100% retention is correlated to an integrity test using a standard reference fluid (e.g., water or alcohol). During the membrane development process, filter manufacturers define the correlation between integrity testing and bacterial retention following ASTM F838 methodology (6).
  • The chemical compatibility between the filter and product can be qualified by the filter manufacturer. Filter-user testing is often necessary to confirm compatibility of the process fluids with the filter and operation conditions. Tests such as product-based integrity and bacterial retention may be used to assess chemical compatibility.
  • Extractables may be qualified by the filter manufacturer using model solvents and specific laboratory conditions and may also be determined by the filter user for product-specific fluids under simulated processing conditions (e.g., temperature, duration). Based on the results obtained from the extractables study, the filter user should evaluate the need for testing of leachables. The leachables study should build upon the identified extractables and be tested with the product in scope or a representative surrogate.
  • Filter safety testing (i.e., biological reactivity) is conducted by the filter manufacturer and does not need to be repeated by the filter user (16).
  • The capability of a filter to be sterilized and the maximum sterilization conditions recommended for the filter are qualified by the filter manufacturer. The filter user is responsible for validating the sterilization method used in the process.*
  • Particulate matter, nonfiber release, oxidizable substances, total organic carbon (TOC), conductivity testing and flow rates may be qualified by the filter manufacturer. The filter user should determine if additional testing is required due to differences in processing conditions.
  • Filter selection done by the end user will look at filter sizing, filter type based on process scale and design, and impact on product critical quality attributes (CQAs) by evaluating compatibility (e.g., adsorption, reaction, degradation) through contact with the filter unit.
  • Since solvents could be used as wetting agents to determine if the integrity test failed due to wetting problems, it would be advisable to determine the appropriate amount of flush volumes needed.

*Note: Sterilization of filter capsules by gamma or x-ray irradiation is generally validated by the filter manufacturer.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The Q, L, V, E four-level responsibility framework:

  • Q (Qualification): Standardized tests performed by the filter manufacturer that establish the basic quality data for the product
  • L (Lot Release): The manufacturer's release testing of every lot shipped, ensuring lot-to-lot consistency
  • V (Validation): Process validation that the user must perform to demonstrate performance under actual process conditions
  • E (Evaluate): The user must evaluate whether additional testing is needed and document the technical rationale

ASTM F838: The standard method for bacterial retention testing. It uses Brevundimonas diminuta as the challenge organism and establishes the correlation between integrity testing and bacterial retention.

Analogy

The division of responsibilities in Table 4.1-1🔍 is like buying a car: the car manufacturer (the filter maker) is responsible for crash tests, emissions tests, and quality control before the car leaves the factory (Q, L). But after buying the car, the owner (the filter user) still has to do a test drive on real roads (V = process validation) and decide whether to add a dash cam or modify the exhaust (E = evaluating the need for additional testing). The manufacturer saying the car "can be driven" does not mean it "can be driven safely on your route" -- that is the owner's responsibility.

Key Notes

Key differences between extractables and leachables:

  • Extractables: Chemical substances that are "forcibly" extracted from the filter under extreme laboratory conditions (strong solvents, high temperature, long duration) -- a complete "worst-case" inventory
  • Leachables: Chemical substances that actually migrate into the product under normal process conditions -- typically a subset of the extractables

A leachables study should be designed based on the results of the extractables study rather than starting from scratch. This is a science-driven, stepwise approach.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: When your client asks you to change filter suppliers, Table 4.1-1🔍 is your checklist. You need to:

  1. Request from the new supplier all test data marked Q and L
  2. Repeat all process validation marked V (data from the old filter cannot be carried over)
  3. Perform a risk assessment on all items marked E to decide which need to be retested

This change may take 3-6 months and cost hundreds of thousands of dollars -- so supplier selection should be considered carefully early in process development.

Practice Questions

  1. In Table 4.1-1🔍, why is "Bacteria Retention in Product" performed only by the user (V) and not by the manufacturer (-)?
  2. If an extractables study shows that a compound is present at a very low level, can you skip the leachables study? What would your risk assessment logic be?
  3. Why does the user not need to repeat the "Biological reactivity" tests? How does this differ from the other test items?
4.2 Filter Design

Original Text
