Sterilizing-grade membranes are employed in a wide variety of sizes and filter formats, including cartridges, capsules and disc styles to suit many scales and types of applications. Membranes can also be pleated to increase the surface area, and this can be placed in either a cartridge or a capsule filter. Figure 4.2-1🔍 shows examples of filter cartridges, capsules and components.

The design of filter elements is important because design features like membrane pleat shape and density determine the EFA (Effective Filtration Area) which, in turn, influences the flow rate and total throughput (i.e., the larger the EFA, the higher the flow and/or throughput). If the pleat density is too high, the free flow path is restricted, and the flow is reduced; if the pleat density is too low, the EFA might be too low and the flow restricted. There may be a difference in the pleating geometry used in a scale-up study in process development (PD) and the pleating geometry of the process-scale device, since high-EFA pleating geometries are typically used in process-scale devices only. Potential differences in performance should be evaluated during PD (see Section 5.4.1 for additional information).

Depending on the filtration needs (e.g., product, process, equipment or facility constraints), different setups and filter types can be employed. Figure 4.2-2🔍 shows different filter types and configurations.

Figure 4.2-3🔍 and Figure 4.2-4🔍 are cutaways that reveal the internal components of a typical cartridge and capsule. Filter capsules, composed of filter elements preinstalled and sealed in plastic housings, are compact and relatively lightweight. Depending on the orientation and configuration of the capsule or filter-housing, the fluid may enter from the bottom or from the top of the capsule or housing and flow around the outside of the internal filter module, pass through the pleated filter membrane to the interior core, and exit the outlet.

Stainless-steel filter housings and disposable filter capsules are available in two configurations: T-style or in-line style (Figure 4.2-5🔍A and Figure 4.2-6🔍A, respectively). The T-style housings have the inlet and outlet pipe adjacent to each other at the bottom plate of the housing (Figure 4.2-5🔍B). The inlet pipe in the in-line style housing is positioned at the dome of the housing and the outlet pipe on the bottom plate (Figure 4.2-6🔍B). Most housings and capsules also have vent ports for removing air.

In addition to housing styles, the filter elements themselves have multiple adapter options (Figure 4.2-7🔍) for fitting the filter into the housing. The most common filter-cartridge adapter is a double O-ring, double-bayonet adapter. This adapter type is twisted into place on the base plate to ensure a tight seal between the filter element and the housing base. As housing-base tolerances may vary, it is imperative that the specific filter and housing provide the proper seal throughout the filtration operation (e.g., from steam-in-place (SIP) through post-use integrity-testing).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

EFA (Effective Filtration Area): The membrane surface area that actually takes part in filtration. Pleating makes it possible to greatly increase the effective area within the limited space of the filter housing. EFA directly determines flow rate and total throughput.

The three main filter formats:

  • Cartridge: Installed in a stainless steel housing with a replaceable element; suited to large-scale production
  • Capsule: An integrated design with the membrane pre-installed in a plastic housing; the whole unit is discarded after use, so it is single-use
  • Disc: Used for laboratory or small-scale testing

T-Style vs. In-Line Style: Two housing inlet/outlet configurations. In the T-style, the inlet and outlet are on the same side at the bottom (convenient for draining); in the in-line style, the inlet is at the top and the outlet at the bottom (draining by gravity).

Analogy

Balancing pleat density is like folding a hand fan: if the fan is folded too tightly (pleat density too high), it cannot open and moves no air (restricted flow path); if folded too loosely (pleat density too low), the fan surface is too small and also moves little air. The best design finds the sweet spot of pleat density within the limited space, so that air (the liquid) can flow through freely while the effective area is maximized.

An adapter is like an electrical plug: no matter how good the appliance is, if the plug and socket do not match or make poor contact, the best case is a short (leakage) and the worst case is a fire (failure of sterility assurance). The double O-ring, double-lug design is a "mistake-proofing plus double safeguard" concept.

Key Notes

The scale-up trap: The small filters used in process development and the large filters used in commercial production may have different pleat geometries. High-EFA pleat designs are usually used only in production-scale equipment. This means that flow rate and throughput data from the PD stage may not be directly extrapolated to production scale -- they need to be re-evaluated on the production equipment.

Seal integrity throughout: Note that the source text stresses that the seal must be maintained throughout the entire operation -- from SIP (steam-in-place, with high temperature and pressure) all the way to post-use integrity testing. This spans extreme changes in temperature and pressure and is a major challenge for sealing materials and design.

Figure Analysis

Figures 4.2-1 to 4.2-7 (figures in the source text): This section cites many figures showing different filter constructions. The key points to understand are:

  • Figure 4.2-1🔍: Cartridge (requires a stainless steel housing), capsule (self-contained, integrated housing), and exploded views of the components
  • Figure 4.2-3🔍/4.2-4: Cross-sections showing the direction of liquid flow -- from the outside inward through the pleated membrane, into the central core, and out
  • Figure 4.2-5🔍/4.2-6: T-style (inlet and outlet on the same side at the bottom) vs. in-line style (inlet at the top, outlet at the bottom)
  • Figure 4.2-7🔍: Different adapter types -- choosing the wrong one can lead to a poor seal with serious consequences

Practice Questions

  1. Under what circumstances would you choose a capsule rather than a cartridge (with stainless steel housing)? Analyze from three perspectives: cost, validation, and operational convenience.
  2. Why is the vent port important in filter design? If air is not completely removed, what effect will it have on the filtration operation and integrity testing?
4.3 Filters in Single-Use Disposable Systems

Original Text

It is common to build transfer sets of filters integrated with tubing, sensors, and connectors that are presterilized (e.g., gamma, x-ray) typically used together with other single-use technology or in hybrid systems together with stainless steel equipment. Furthermore, the assembling of multiple filters in ready-to-use assemblies has also become common. These systems offer some advantages over conventional stainless-steel equipment including the:

  • Ability to obtain presterilized
  • Reduction in setup time
  • Elimination of cleaning
  • Elimination of cleaning validation
  • Elimination of risk of cross-contamination
  • Elimination of filter housing maintenance
  • Reduction in operator exposure due to closed systems
  • Reduction of drug product contamination from the external environment
  • Reduction of the operator's exposure to the drug substances

Implementation of SUS should follow a risk-based approach that considers product and process compatibility and demonstrates robustness, suitability, and consistent performance under intended use conditions. (Refer to PDA's Technical Report No. 66: Application of Single-Use Systems in Pharmaceutical Manufacturing for additional information.)

Depending on the processing considerations and setup requirements, the filter user chooses the best configuration (capsule, cartridge in stainless-steel housing, or hybrid) for the filtration operation. Use of disposable capsules obviates the need for cleaning validation and equipment amortization, which may reduce operating costs. Another advantage is that operator contact with potentially hazardous products is minimized. The use of stainless steel must be balanced against the use of capsule filters, which can be single use and can reduce utilities and validation efforts. Different optimization can be performed based on environment (waste), energy use, or financial analysis.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Single-Use System (SUS): Pre-assembled, pre-sterilized (usually by gamma or x-ray irradiation) filter and tubing assemblies. They are discarded after one use, with no cleaning and no CIP/SIP required.

Hybrid System: A system that combines stainless steel equipment with single-use components, for example a stainless steel formulation tank used with single-use tubing and capsule filters. This is currently the most common configuration at many CDMOs.

Ready-to-Use Assembly: A complete filtration system pre-assembled by the supplier, including filters, tubing, connectors, sensors, and so on, shipped after gamma irradiation sterilization and ready to use once unpacked on site.

Analogy

A stainless steel filter system is like ceramic tableware at home: good quality and reusable, but after every use it has to be washed, disinfected, and checked for damage, and the washing itself (cleaning validation) is a major undertaking. A single-use system is like disposable tableware: open it and use it, throw it away afterwards, with no dishwashing and no worry about leftover food from the previous guest (cross-contamination). But disposable tableware has an environmental cost (waste), and you need to confirm that the disposable tableware itself does not release harmful substances (extractables/leachables).

Key Notes

The hidden costs of SUS: Although SUS eliminates cleaning validation and equipment depreciation, there are other costs to consider:

  • Waste disposal cost: Handling large amounts of plastic waste and environmental compliance
  • Supply chain risk: Greater dependence on specific suppliers; a supply interruption could affect production
  • Extractables/leachables assessment: Plastic components require additional E&L studies
  • Storage space: Pre-sterilized components need clean, temperature-controlled warehouse space

PDA TR-66 provides complete guidance on implementing SUS and should be consulted in detail before introducing SUS.

Practical Application

Decision-making for CDMO multi-product manufacturing: A CDMO produces many different drug products at the same time, so the risk of cross-contamination is one of the biggest challenges. Using SUS can eliminate cross-contamination risk at its root (brand-new components for every batch) and also greatly shorten changeover time between batches (no CIP/SIP). For highly potent drugs (such as ADCs and highly potent APIs), SUS is almost a necessity. For large-volume, low-value products, however, a stainless steel system may be more economical in the long run.

Practice Questions

  1. Your CDMO is evaluating whether to convert a traditional stainless steel filling line to a hybrid system. List the top three risk factors and the top three benefits you would consider.
  2. The source text mentions that different optimizations can be made based on "environment (waste), energy use, or financial analysis". Now that ESG (environmental, social, and governance) is receiving ever more attention, what does this mean for a CDMO's SUS procurement strategy?
4.4 Filter Toxicity and Biocompatibility

Original Text

One important aspect of selecting a filter is assessing the safety of the filter medium and/or device. Filter manufacturers should provide information about the origin and toxicity of the filter components, including their origin of animal-derived components.

4.4.1 Toxicity

Filters must not impart toxic materials to the fluid stream. Filter manufacturers generally qualify filters through performance of standardized testing in compliance with compendial methods (16). These tests involve the introduction of filter extracts or actual filter samples into cell-culture systems. Reactivity of the cell culture to the test article is assessed visually, scored and compared against predefined acceptance criteria for toxicological safety. The results of these tests are commonly included as part of the filter validation guide.

Over the years there has been a shift away from animal-based testing, to better align with animal-welfare principles.

4.4.2 Animal-Derived Materials

The filter manufacturer typically includes information on biocompatibility of animal-derived materials used in filter manufacture. The use of bovine tallow-derived stearates poses essentially no risk for transmission of transmissible spongiform encephalopathies (TSE) and other diseases. Typically, the manufacturing processes used to produce bovine tallow and tallow derivatives are very rigorous and meet or exceed safety requirements (17, 18).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Biocompatibility: The ability of the filter material to avoid causing harmful reactions when in contact with biological systems. This is a basic requirement for drug product safety -- the filter itself must not become a source of toxic substances.

Toxicity Testing: Following pharmacopoeial methods (such as USP <87> in vitro cytotoxicity test and USP <88> in vivo biological reactivity test), filter extracts or samples are exposed to cell culture systems and the cellular response is observed to assess toxicity.

TSE (Transmissible Spongiform Encephalopathies): For example bovine spongiform encephalopathy (BSE, commonly known as mad cow disease). Any product that uses animal-derived materials must be assessed for the risk of TSE transmission.

Analogy

Biocompatibility testing is like safety testing for food containers: the plastic container used in your microwave must pass food-grade safety tests to confirm that it will not release harmful substances into the food when heated. Likewise, when a filter is in contact with a high-value drug product stream, it must not release anything toxic to patients. Traceability of animal-derived components is like food traceability labeling -- you need to know the source of the raw materials to make sure there is no risk of infectivity.

Key Notes

Regulatory trends for animal-derived materials:

  • EU GMP Annex 1 and ICH Q5A set strict traceability and risk assessment requirements for animal-derived materials
  • The industry is moving from animal testing to alternative methods such as cell culture, in line with the global 3Rs principles (Replacement, Reduction, Refinement)
  • Although the TSE risk of bovine tallow-derived stearates is extremely low, the risk assessment must still be documented

Practical impact for CDMOs: Some clients (particularly vaccine or biologics manufacturers) may require filters that are completely free of animal-derived components (animal-free). Confirm that the supplier can provide the relevant declaration documents.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the user not need to repeat toxicity testing (biological reactivity) once the manufacturer has completed it? Compare with the responsibility allocation of the other test items in Table 4.1-1🔍 and explain the reason.
  2. If your client requires that all process-contact materials be "Animal-Free", which filter components and documents do you need to confirm?
4.5 Filter Cleanliness

Original Text

Particulate contamination from the filter, filter hardware, filter installation and the process should be evaluated and considered since each source may contribute to particle burden in the product. Filter flush effluent samples are examined for particles greater than 10 microns and greater than 25 microns. Filter flush effluent is considered compliant upon meeting compendial guidelines for particulate matter in injections. Using these criteria, filters are also qualified as nonfiber-releasing (10-12).

In addition to particles, filters may be a source of other contaminants, such as endotoxin, organic carbon, or oxidizable substances. Potential sources may include surfactants, wetting agents, additives in the plastic component manufacture, manufacturing debris and materials of construction (MoC). Preflushing filters may reduce levels of particulates and contaminants and may be performed as part of the wetting process prior to integrity-testing. A filter can be preflushed based on the extractable studies, taking into account the sterilization method and process step.

Note: Filter flushing and preflushing are performed by the filter user.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Particulate Matter in Injections: Under USP <788> / Ph. Eur. 2.9.19, injections must contain no more than 6000 particles ≥10 μm per container and no more than 600 particles ≥25 μm per container (large-volume injections). Filter flush effluent must meet these limits.

Nonfiber-Releasing: Confirms that the filter does not release fibers into the filtrate during filtration. This is a basic pharmacopoeial requirement.

Preflushing: Flushing the filter with water or a suitable solvent before actual filtration to remove residual particles, manufacturing residues, surfactants, and so on. The flush volume should be determined based on extractables studies.

Analogy

Preflushing is like running a few kettles of water through a new kettle and pouring them away before first use: the inside wall of a new kettle may carry residues from manufacturing (a plastic smell, metal dust, etc.), and the first few kettles wash these away. Likewise, a new filter may carry additives, surfactants, or particles left over from manufacturing, and preflushing removes these "factory residues" so that the product is not contaminated during actual filtration. The flush volume must be decided from experimental data, just as you have to test for yourself how many kettles of water it takes before the smell is gone.

Key Notes

A comprehensive view of contamination sources: The source text specifically points out that particulate contamination comes not only from the filter itself, but also from:

  • Filter hardware: Stainless steel housings, O-rings, adapters, and other hardware components
  • Filter installation: Particles introduced during installation (operators, tools, environment)
  • Process: Particles generated by the process itself

This is a reminder that a particulate investigation must not focus on the filter alone -- the whole system has to be evaluated. Endotoxin and total organic carbon (TOC) are also cleanliness indicators that need attention.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is preflushing usually combined with the wetting step and performed before integrity testing? What is the logic behind this sequence of operations?
  2. If the extractables from a filter after gamma irradiation sterilization are higher than after steam sterilization, how does this affect the setting of the preflush volume?
4.6 Filter Extractables

Original Text

Since sterilizing-filters are often used in the final manufacturing steps of a pharmaceutical production process, the effect of the filter on the final product should be evaluated. An extractable is any chemical component that is removed from a material by the application of an artificial or exaggerated condition (e.g., solvent, temperature, time). A leachable is a chemical component that migrates from a contact surface into a drug product or process fluid during storage or normal use conditions. These are typically a subset of extractables (19, 20).

Potential sources of extractables and leachables include, but are not limited to, membrane components (e.g., plasticizers, surfactants, antioxidants, residual solvents, device support layers), and device components (e.g., end caps, housings, cages, O-rings). Factors affecting leachables may include the chemical properties of the process stream, sterilization process, contact time, temperature, and volume-to-area ratios (21). Filtration of organic solutions may lead to higher levels of leachables than aqueous solutions.

Extractable data may be obtained from the filter manufacturer or may be generated by the filter user (see Section 6.5 for filter user-generated data). Filter manufacturers can use model streams to determine the extractable profiles under worst case conditions (22). Although an important component of filter validation, the level of extractables and leachables from most sterilizing-grade filters that can potentially enter the process stream is relatively low. Flushing the filters prior to use can further reduce these levels as demonstrated by flush curves measured by TOC or other tests for specific leachables (21).

Data should ideally include a comprehensive list of the compounds that could be leachables when the filter is used in the actual formulation under process conditions. The necessity to perform leachable study should be evaluated based on risk determined from extractable studies and risk to patient safety.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Extractables: Chemical substances extracted from a material under deliberately intensified conditions (strong solvents, high temperature, long duration). This is a comprehensive "worst-case" inventory.

Leachables: Chemical substances that migrate from a contact surface into the drug product under normal use or storage conditions. They are typically a subset of the extractables -- that is, the amounts that actually come out under normal conditions are much lower than under extreme conditions.

Volume-to-Area Ratio: The ratio of the volume of process fluid to the membrane contact area. The smaller the ratio (a small volume of liquid in contact with a large membrane area), the higher the concentration of leachables per unit volume may be.

Flush Curve: A plot of the extractables concentration in the flush effluent against flush volume, monitored by TOC or a specific analytical method. When the curve falls to an acceptable level, preflushing can be stopped.

Analogy

The relationship between extractables and leachables is like washing a car: if you blast it with a high-pressure water jet and detergent (extreme conditions = extractables), everything that can come off the car will come off -- dust, bird droppings, the wax layer, even a little paint. But if it is just an ordinary light rain (normal use conditions = leachables), perhaps only the surface dust is washed off. So leachables are typically a subset of extractables. But the purpose of extractables testing is to first know the "worst case" and then assess the risk under "normal conditions".

Key Notes

The special risk of organic solvents: The source text notes that filtering organic solutions may lead to higher levels of leachables. This is especially important for processes that use organic solvents (such as the final filtration step of some small-molecule drugs). Both the choice of membrane material and the design of the E&L study need special consideration.

Patient safety is the ultimate consideration: Whether a leachables study is needed ultimately depends on a risk assessment of patient safety. Even if the extractables levels appear low, if they include known carcinogens or genotoxic substances, a detailed leachables study is still required. This again confirms that "sterility assurance/patient safety" always ranks first in the decision hierarchy.

Formula

Key variables for E&L risk assessment:

Leachables risk = f(extractable species, contact time, temperature,
                solvent strength, volume-to-area ratio, sterilization method)

Actual leachables concentration (estimate) =
  total extractables x extraction efficiency factor x (contact time / extraction time)
  ÷ drug product volume

Where: the extraction efficiency factor for organic solvents > the extraction efficiency factor for aqueous solutions

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the source text say "leachables are typically a subset of extractables"? In what rare situations might leachables appear that are not on the extractables list?
  2. You are designing the sterilizing filtration step for a small-batch biologic. The batch is only 5 L, but a standard 10-inch cartridge is used. From the volume-to-area ratio perspective, what should you be concerned about, and how would you mitigate the risk?
4.6.1 Chemical Compatibility

Original Text

The chemical compatibility of the filter device must be evaluated to avoid potential filter damage or alteration and to avoid fluid contamination by either leachables or particulates. Filter manufacturers will have general chemical-compatibility guides based on the MoC and a range of standard chemicals. Since there may be numerous chemical interactions between filter device components and the process fluid or solvents, the typical chemical compatibility table provided by the filter manufacturer is commonly used as a starting point for further testing. More extensive testing should be performed by the end user to determine chemical compatibility of the filter device and process fluid under process conditions (see Section 6.0 for more details).

Chemical-compatibility testing should encompass the entire device and depends on the fluid, filtration temperature, and contact time. Additional studies should be performed by the filter user. Additional product-specific studies may be conducted by the filter vendor on request from the filter user to ensure there is no impact to product CQAs. Common chemical-compatibility tests include integrity test, tensile strength, nonvolatile residue (NVR), extractables, particulates, flow rate, optical or scanning electron microscopy (SEM), burst pressure and membrane/O-ring thickness (23). Use of a combination of tests is recommended since a single test might not be able to detect subtle incompatibilities.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Chemical Compatibility: The ability of the filter and all of its components (membrane, housing, O-rings, support layers, etc.) to avoid harmful chemical reactions with the process fluid under actual operating conditions. Incompatibility can lead to:

  • Damage to the filter structure → failure of bacterial retention
  • Increased extractables/particles → product contamination
  • Integrity test failure → batch rejection

Entire Device: Emphasizes that it is not enough to test the membrane alone; all contact parts, including O-rings, housing, and end caps, must be included. It is often the non-membrane components (such as O-rings) that are most prone to compatibility problems.

Analogy

Chemical compatibility testing is like buying a new food storage container for acidic foods (such as ketchup or vinegar): you need to know not only whether the container body resists acid, but also whether the silicone gasket in the lid will be corroded and whether the latches will become brittle and snap. The manufacturer's compatibility chart is like the "suitable foods" list on the container's packaging -- it is only a starting point. If what you want to store is a special chili sauce (a proprietary drug formulation), you may need to run your own actual tests to be confident.

Key Notes

The need for a combination of test methods: The source text explicitly recommends using a "combination of tests", because a single test may not detect subtle incompatibilities. For example:

  • The integrity test may pass, yet SEM shows microscopic damage on the membrane surface
  • The flow rate may be unchanged, yet nonvolatile residue (NVR) increases significantly, indicating that material is being released
  • A decrease in tensile strength indicates that the material is degrading, even when other indicators look normal

This is why chemical compatibility assessment is a comprehensive, multi-dimensional process.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must chemical compatibility testing be performed under actual process conditions (temperature, contact time, flow rate) rather than only static soaking at room temperature?
  2. A client's drug formulation contains 20% ethanol. From a chemical compatibility standpoint, which components of the filter would you pay particular attention to, and why?
4.7 Operational Ranges

Original Text

Manufacturers typically specify the maximum operational temperature, pressure, and sterilization temperature limits and provide water flow-rate data for their filters. Manufacturers provide maximum forward and reverse differential pressure limits at specific temperature conditions, incorporating adequate safety margins. This information may also reference limits at different temperatures, facilitating the selection of process flow rates, temperatures, and sterilization options compatible with the filter. Section 8.0 addresses selection and validation of sterilization options by the filter user.

Filter-system flow rate at a given differential pressure is a function of membrane polymer and structure type, pore size, housing inlet and outlet diameter, effective filter area, fluid temperature and viscosity. During the design of a process filtration system, these variables should be evaluated for compatibility with the operating limits of the filters and the filter area selected. Filter suppliers also test pressure-pulsation cycles to determine the mechanical stability of the filter device. However, care should be taken to avoid pressures exceeding the manufacturer's stated specifications of maximum allowable differential pressure at specified temperature ranges.

Note: There may be a distinction between the operational ranges with which a filter is generally suitable, and the "validated filtration parameters" for a sterilizing filtration unit operation, as defined in process-specific bacterial retention study validation in order to cover scale-down worst-case process conditions (see Section 6.0).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Differential Pressure: The pressure difference between the upstream and downstream sides of the filter. Too high a differential pressure may damage the membrane structure and cause integrity failure; too low a differential pressure gives insufficient flow. The manufacturer provides the maximum allowable differential pressure at different temperatures.

Operational Range vs. Validated Parameters: This is a key distinction --

  • Operational range: The manufacturer says "this filter can be used at X temperature and Y pressure" -- this is a general hardware limit
  • Validated parameters: The actual usable parameter range that you have confirmed in your own process through bacterial retention validation -- usually narrower than the operational range

Pressure-Pulsation Cycles: Simulate the pressure fluctuations seen in actual operation (such as pulsation caused by pumps) to test the mechanical stability of the filter under repeated pressure changes.

Analogy

The difference between the operational range and validated parameters is like a car's design speed versus the safe speed on a particular road: the manufacturer says the car can reach 250 km/h (operational range), but on the mountain road near your home, after actual testing, the safe speed may be only 80 km/h (validated parameters). Exceeding that speed does not necessarily mean a crash, but you cannot guarantee safety -- because you have only validated up to 80 km/h. Likewise, the filter manufacturer's operational range is the limit at which the "hardware will not fail", but your "sterility assurance" is valid only within the parameter range you have validated.

Key Notes

Interaction of temperature and pressure: This is an often overlooked point. The maximum allowable differential pressure usually decreases as temperature rises. For example, a filter may have a maximum differential pressure of 5 bar at 25°C but only 2.5 bar at 80°C. If you filter at high temperature but apply the room-temperature pressure limit, you may damage the filter.

Extreme conditions of SIP (steam-in-place): The high temperature (usually 121-134°C) and steam pressure during SIP are the most severe test for the filter. The sterilization temperature limit and the operating temperature limit may differ -- be sure to distinguish them.

Formula

Factors affecting flow rate:

Flow Rate = f(ΔP, A_eff, μ, T, d_pore, d_inlet/outlet)

Where:
  ΔP    = Differential Pressure
  A_eff = Effective Filtration Area (EFA)
  μ     = liquid viscosity (varies with temperature)
  T     = temperature (affects viscosity and maximum differential pressure)
  d_pore = pore size
  d_inlet/outlet = housing inlet/outlet diameter

Note: temperature rises → viscosity falls → flow rate increases
      but at the same time → maximum allowable differential pressure falls → operating window narrows

Practice Questions

  1. Your process filters a high-viscosity protein solution at 60°C. In choosing the operating pressure, which conflicting factors do you need to consider? How would you find the optimal operating point?
  2. Why does the source text specifically mention "forward and reverse differential pressure limits"? Under what circumstances can reverse differential pressure occur? What risk does it pose to filter integrity?
4.8 Shelf Life and Shipping Studies

Original Text

Filter manufacturers will validate packaging and shipping of the filters according to industry standards to ensure that the products can withstand common transport hazards (24). In addition, filter manufacturers will establish shelf-life recommendations based on product stability under defined conditions, which will be valid if the filter is stored and handled properly. These recommendations can be used by the filter user to establish expiration dates or use-by dates.

Filters that are sterilized via gamma or x-ray irradiation will have expiration dates due to the sterility claim associated with the sterilization method.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Shelf Life: The period during which the filter, under the manufacturer's recommended storage conditions, maintains its quality attributes and performance specifications. Filters beyond their shelf life should not be used in drug manufacturing.

Sterility Claim: Filters sterilized by gamma or x-ray irradiation carry a "pre-sterilized" claim. This claim is time-limited because the packaging materials age over time and may lose their sterile barrier function. These filters therefore always have a defined expiry period.

Shipping Validation: Based on standards such as ISTA or ASTM D4169, risks such as vibration, drop, and compression during transport are simulated to ensure that the filter is still intact when it reaches the user.

Analogy

Managing filter shelf life is like managing expiry dates on supermarket food: milk (an irradiation-sterilized capsule filter) has a definite expiry date and cannot be drunk after it passes; canned goods (non-pre-sterilized stainless steel cartridges) keep longer, but are still limited. Shipping validation is like packaging tests for fragile items bought online -- the supplier must make sure the product can withstand the knocks and squeezing of courier transport and reach you safely.

Key Notes

Inventory management considerations for CDMOs:

  • First in, first out (FIFO): Enforce strict material rotation to avoid scrapping expired stock
  • Storage condition monitoring: Temperature or humidity excursions may accelerate material aging and shorten the actual shelf life
  • Receiving inspection: Check packaging integrity on receipt to confirm that nothing was damaged in transit
  • Special management of irradiation-sterilized filters: These filters usually have a shorter expiry than non-sterilized filters and require more active inventory management

In a multi-product CDMO, different clients may specify different filter brands/models, so there are many inventory items -- scrapping of expired stock is a real and common source of wasted cost.

Practical Application

Supply chain strategy: During the COVID-19 pandemic, the global supply chain for single-use components was severely disrupted, and many drug manufacturers faced filter shortages. CDMOs are advised to:

  1. Establish safety stock (while balancing the risk of expiry)
  2. Validate alternative filters from at least two suppliers
  3. Establish long-term contracts and priority supply agreements with suppliers
  4. Consider a consignment stock model to reduce tied-up capital

Practice Questions

  1. A lot of gamma-sterilized capsule filters is found in the warehouse to have been stored at a temperature that exceeded the recommended upper limit (but is still within its shelf life). How would you handle it? Can it be used directly?
  2. From the perspective of supply chain resilience, what additional risks does a single-use filter system carry compared with a traditional stainless steel system? How can they be mitigated?

Section Summary

  • Clear division of responsibilities: The filter manufacturer is responsible for qualification (Q) and lot release (L); the user is responsible for process validation (V) and additional evaluation (E). Table 4.1-1🔍 is the most important reference table for the division of responsibilities.
  • Filter design affects performance: Pleat density determines EFA (effective filtration area), which in turn affects flow rate and throughput. During scale-up, pay attention to differences in filter geometry between the PD stage and the production stage.
  • The two faces of SUS: Single-use systems eliminate cleaning validation and cross-contamination risk, but bring new challenges in supply chain dependence, waste management, and E&L assessment.
  • E&L is a layered, progressive process: First perform an extractables study to establish a worst-case baseline, then decide through risk assessment whether a leachables study is needed. Preflushing can effectively reduce the levels of both.
  • Chemical compatibility requires a comprehensive assessment: The entire device must be tested (not just the membrane), using a combination of test methods, under actual process conditions.
  • Operational range ≠ validated parameters: The manufacturer's operational range is a hardware limit; the user's validated parameters (from bacterial retention validation) are the basis for sterility assurance.
  • Shelf life management cannot be ignored: In particular, gamma/x-ray sterilized filters have a defined expiry period, so FIFO and storage condition monitoring must be strictly enforced.
  • The decision hierarchy reconfirmed: All choices (material, format, supplier) ultimately come back to the order of priority: sterility assurance > product CQAs > commercial flexibility.
5

Section 5: Process Design

p23-p38

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p23 - p31

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the overall framework of sterilizing filtration process design, including the three stages of FDA process validation and the ICH Q8 design space concept
  • Learn how to evaluate product properties and filter compatibility, including chemical compatibility, physical properties, and adsorption effects
  • Master how the design space is defined and the key factors that set the operating range of filtration (temperature, volume, flow rate, pressure, time, etc.)
  • Understand how flow characteristics, inlet/differential pressure, temperature, and filtration time relate to one another and affect the process
  • Master the methods for evaluating filter throughput and maximum volume, and the factors that influence them
  • Understand the principles, methods, and scale-up considerations of scale-down systems and small scale device testing
5.0 Filtration Process Design, Usage, and Handling Considerations

Original Text

According to the FDA's Guidance for Industry: Process Validation: General Principles and Practices (Revision 1), quality, efficacy, and safety must be built into the product and cannot be tested at the end of the manufacturing process. This concept is especially important for many sterilizing filtration operations, as the filter often represents the final microbial control barrier prior to patient dosing (25). This guidance document outlines three stages of process validation: Stage 1-Process Design, Stage 2-Process Qualification, and Stage 3-Continued Process Verification. Section 5.0 focuses on the process design aspects, and Section 6.0 focuses on the process-specific validation requirements for a sterilizing filtration process. Continued process verification is generally out of scope in this document and is covered in other guidance documents. The process design methodology discussed here is consistent with the principles of the International Council for Harmonization (ICH) Quality Guideline (Q8): Pharmaceutical Development, which is to first evaluate the design space, then develop a control strategy (8).

Membrane filters are used for multiple applications within the pharmaceutical industry and therefore, membrane filter types, designs and configurations are as varied as their applications. This chapter will focus on the process design and usage of sterilizing filters for the removal of microorganisms in pharmaceutical processing. Many of these design principles will also apply to the use of sterilizing-grade filters in locations other than the final sterilizing barrier for the control of bioburden, although those use cases have significantly reduced risk to product quality and may not require the same level of validation and controls to be applied (26).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three Stages of Process Validation:

  • Stage 1 - Process Design: Through development activities, the commercial manufacturing conditions of the process are defined. This chapter (5.0) focuses on this stage.
  • Stage 2 - Process Qualification: Demonstrates, in the actual manufacturing environment, that the designed process performs reproducibly. Section 6.0 covers this.
  • Stage 3 - Continued Process Verification: Ongoing monitoring to ensure the process remains in a state of control.

ICH Q8 Design Space: First define the boundaries of the operating space, then establish a control strategy to ensure that any operating condition within that space consistently yields acceptable product.

Analogy

Imagine you are a head chef opening a new restaurant. You would not wait until the food is on the table to check whether it tastes good (that is "after-the-fact testing"). The right approach is:

  • Stage 1 (Design): Develop the menu in a test kitchen first and establish the best ranges for ingredients, temperature, and time
  • Stage 2 (Qualification): Cook to the recipe in the real kitchen and confirm that the output is consistent
  • Stage 3 (Monitoring): Keep up quality control after opening so that every dish stays true to standard

Sterilizing filtration is like the restaurant's last food safety checkpoint -- if the ingredients themselves are already a problem, even the best filtration cannot fully fix it. The concept of "quality built in" means controlling quality from the source.

Key Notes

The filter is the last microbial control barrier: In aseptic filling processes, the sterilizing filter is often the last line of defense before the product reaches the patient. This means a filtration failure = a direct impact on patient safety, with no downstream remedy.

Non-final barrier applications: Sterilizing-grade filters may also be used for in-process bioburden control. These applications carry lower risk and the validation requirements can be moderately simplified. But "can be simplified" does not mean "can be ignored".

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: As a CDMO, you may manufacture different products for several clients at the same time, and each product's sterilizing filtration process needs its own independent Stage 1 design. Even when the same filter model is used, the chemical properties, particle load, and temperature requirements differ from product to product, so the design space must be established individually. This is a basic compliance requirement and a focus area in client audits.

Practice Questions

  1. Among the three stages of FDA process validation, why must Stage 1 (Process Design) be completed before Stage 2 (Process Qualification)? What are the consequences of skipping it?
  2. Why does TR26 emphasize that the sterilizing filter is the "final microbial control barrier"? In your facility, are there other microbial control measures that can serve as a backup?
5.1 Process Design: Design Space Evaluation

Original Text

An initial evaluation of the design space for a sterilizing-filtration operation will generally encompass a survey of the product properties, compatibility and required operational ranges used in the process.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Design Space: As defined in ICH Q8, the design space is "the multidimensional combination and interaction of input variables that have been demonstrated to provide assurance of quality". For sterilizing filtration, this means you must define safe operating ranges for parameters such as temperature, pressure, flow rate, volume, and time.

The three elements of the design space, in order of evaluation:

  1. Product Properties: understand what you are filtering
  2. Compatibility: confirm that the product and the filter system can coexist
  3. Operational Ranges: define the upper and lower limits of each parameter

Analogy

The design space is like the safe driving range of a car: it has a speed limit (maximum pressure), it needs a minimum oil temperature to run properly (minimum temperature), the tires have a maximum load (maximum volume), and the fuel tank has a capacity limit (filtration time). You reach your destination safely only when all of these parameters are within their safe ranges at the same time.

Key Notes

Note that the text speaks of an "initial evaluation", which implies that establishing the design space is an iterative process -- as you gain more process knowledge, the space is continually refined. Do not expect to define every range perfectly the first time.

Practice Questions

  1. How does the ICH Q8 "design space" concept apply to sterilizing filtration? How does it differ from traditional validation at a single operating condition?
  2. Why is the evaluation order product properties → compatibility → operational ranges? Could the order be reversed?
5.1.1 Product Properties and Filter Compatibility

Original Text

A good first step in the design of a sterilizing-filtration process is to evaluate the solution that needs to be sterilized and how it will interact with the filtration system. A thorough evaluation of the properties of the filtrate will guide in the selection of a filtration membrane and housing system that can withstand chemical stress such as solvents, strongly basic or acidic media, and high-ionic strengths. After performing this type of analysis, it can allow the user to select filtration systems that are appropriate for the application.

For the second step of the compatibility analysis, an evaluation of the physical properties of the process fluid and intended processing parameters should be carried out. Characteristics such as the fluid temperature range, particulate load, and viscosity can also affect the filter membrane and filtration system selection. The system must be able to handle the mechanical stresses placed on it and remain integral for the duration of the filtration process. Since there may be numerous chemical interactions between filter device/SUS components and the process fluid or solvents, the typical chemical compatibility table provided by the filter manufacturer is commonly used as a starting point for filter selection.

Another dimension of filter compatibility to consider is adsorption of components from the process stream. Adsorption is a mechanism of product binding to the membrane or product contacting materials, which may affect the product composition and concentration. Potential adsorption of product components to the filter and other components of the filtration system (e.g., bags, connectors, tubing) must be considered independently. Considerations should include membrane type, MoC used throughout the filtration system, hardware, and support materials. Flow rate, product concentration, contact time, process fluid components, temperature and pH can also affect the level of adsorption.

In instances where elevated adsorption occurs in the beginning of the filtration process but levels off during filtration, the filtered volume could be pooled to achieve the specified product composition. During PD, adsorption tests are typically performed at small scale and confirmed at large scale. These tests can also be used to establish potential pretreatment options (e.g., buffer flush, soaking), necessary process parameter options or membrane polymer choices. Elevated levels of nonspecific product adsorption on the filter membrane can result in accelerated filter-fouling and performance degradation that would need to be studied as part of filter-sizing studies.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Two steps of compatibility analysis:

Step 1 -- Chemical Compatibility:

  • Evaluate the chemical properties of the solution (solvent, pH, ionic strength)
  • Confirm that the filter material and housing can withstand these chemical environments
  • Starting point: the filter manufacturer's chemical compatibility chart

Step 2 -- Physical Compatibility:

  • Evaluate the temperature range, particle load, and viscosity
  • Confirm that the system can withstand mechanical stress and maintain integrity throughout the entire filtration

Adsorption: The phenomenon in which product components bind to the filter or system materials. This is not the purpose of filtration but a side effect. Adsorption can lead to:

  • Reduced product concentration -- especially significant for high-value biologics
  • Altered product composition -- some components are selectively adsorbed
  • Accelerated filter plugging -- accumulation of adsorbed material causes fouling

Analogy

Imagine using a new kettle to brew tea. You would need to consider:

  • Chemical compatibility: Can the kettle material withstand hot water? Will it be corroded by the tannins in the tea? This is like confirming that the filter can withstand your process liquid
  • Physical compatibility: Is the kettle big enough? Can it withstand high temperature? This is like confirming that the filtration system can handle your temperature and volume
  • Adsorption: Will the new kettle absorb the aroma or color of the tea? This is like a filter adsorbing certain drug components. The first few pots taste off, but after a while it stabilizes -- this is the phenomenon of "high initial adsorption that levels off later"

Key Notes

MoC (Materials of Construction): Adsorption assessment cannot look at the filter alone! All product-contact surfaces of the filtration system (bags, connectors, tubing) can adsorb product components and must be evaluated individually.

Pretreatment options: A buffer flush or soaking can "saturate" the adsorption sites of the filter and reduce subsequent product loss during filtration. For high-value biologics, this is common practice.

Filter fouling: Non-specific adsorption not only affects product yield but also accelerates filter fouling, directly affecting filtration performance and filter sizing.

Formula

Concept for estimating the adsorption rate:

Adsorption Rate = (C_feed - C_filtrate) / C_feed x 100%

Where:
  C_feed = product concentration on the feed side
  C_filtrate = product concentration on the filtrate side

If Adsorption Rate > acceptable range:
  → Consider buffer flush pretreatment
  → Evaluate alternative filter materials
  → Adjust the filtration volume to dilute by pooling

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: A CDMO receives a request to manufacture a new client's monoclonal antibody (mAb) product. During sterilizing filtration process design, you find that the isoelectric point (pI) of the mAb is close to the process pH, causing protein adsorption on the PVDF membrane as high as 15%.

Response strategies: (1) switch to a PES membrane and test the adsorption rate; (2) perform a buffer flush pretreatment to reduce initial adsorption; (3) evaluate whether a pooling strategy is feasible; (4) calculate the cost of product loss due to adsorption and compare it with the cost of changing the filter.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should chemical compatibility be evaluated before physical compatibility? If the order were reversed, what problems might arise?
  2. For a high-concentration protein drug, how would you expect adsorption behavior to differ between the early and late stages of filtration? What does this mean for production operations?
  3. Besides the filter itself, which other components of the filtration system need to be evaluated for adsorption risk? List at least three.
5.1.2 Defining the Design Space and Operational Ranges

Original Text

Knowledge of the desired at-scale process prospectively allows setting ranges to be studied during the development of the filtration process. For example, a survey of the compounding process and the maximum and minimum volumes that will flow through the filter during the sterilizing filtration process can create an important set of operational ranges that the filter must be able to process. In addition, a subset of these parameters will define ranges that need to be validated later (see Section 6.3). While some parameters can be difficult to know during the initial PD exercise, it can be useful to evaluate the potential market demands for the product and maximum expected batch volume. This will potentially reduce the need for additional development and revalidation of the sterilizing-filtration process as the conditions change over the product lifecycle. Table 5.1.2-1🔍 shows a list of potential factors to consider during the process design phase that can help determine operational ranges to study.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Prospective Design: TR26 stresses anticipating commercial-scale needs at the design stage. Do not look only at the current clinical batch size; consider the maximum batch size that future market demand may bring. The benefit is avoiding revalidation later when the batch size is scaled up.

Table 5.1.2-1🔍 The eight design factors:

  • Temperature: from refrigerated to heated; affects viscosity, flow rate, and microbial growth
  • Total Product Volume: minimum to maximum; determines the range of the bacterial retention validation and the filter size
  • Flow Rate: zero to the maximum expected value, depending on the application
  • Differential Pressure: minimum to maximum; the relationship between pressure and flow rate needs to be studied
  • Filtration Time: maximum value, including process setup and interruption time
  • Product Contact Time: maximum value; affects compatibility and integrity
  • Driving Force: pulsatile or constant pressure; determines the pump type
  • Configuration: single or redundant/multiple filters; considers physical space and risk management

Analogy

Defining the design space is like planning a highway. You consider not only today's traffic but also forecast traffic demand 20 years ahead. If you design a two-lane road based on current traffic, you will have to rebuild and widen it (= revalidation) when traffic doubles five years later. But if you reserve room for four lanes from the start (= covering the future maximum batch size), even though it looks somewhat "excessive" at first, it saves a great deal of reconstruction cost in the long run.

Key Notes

The minimum differential pressure also matters: Note that the range for "Differential Pressure" in the Table includes a minimum value. Setting a reasonable minimum differential pressure can help detect filter installation errors -- if the pressure drop is abnormally low during filtration, the filter may not be installed correctly or there may be a bypass.

Product Contact Time vs Filtration Time: These are two different concepts. Filtration time is the duration of actual filtration; product contact time includes process waiting time, interruption time, and so on. The latter is usually longer and is the worst case for validation.

Configuration decision: Redundant filtration may be a requirement, not an option, in some regulatory jurisdictions. Include it in the design space early to avoid forced changes later.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your facility currently produces 100 L batches of an injectable for a client, but the client's commercialization plan expects batch sizes to grow to 500 L. If you validate the design space only up to 150 L, then when the client's demand grows you will have to repeat the bacterial retention validation and process validation, taking 3-6 months. But if you expand the design space to 600 L (including a safety margin) from the start, development costs are 20% higher initially, yet you avoid future revalidation and save the company a great deal of time and cost.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the "Flow Rate" range in Table 5.1.2-1🔍 start from "Zero"? In what situations is the flow rate zero?
  2. "Driving Force" mentions two modes, pulsatile and constant pressure. In what situations is each suitable? Why does this affect the design space?
  3. If you were a process development scientist at a CDMO, how would you persuade management to invest more resources up front to establish a wider design space? Describe the return on investment in business language.
5.2 Flow Characteristics

Original Text

Flow-performance criteria during a filtration operation can include parameters such as flow rate, total throughput, thermal and mechanical resistance, and nonspecific adsorption. A filter's EFA is used in calculations for scaling filter performance, determining extractables levels, reporting flow rates (i.e., flux), and assuring of bacterial retention. Since applications vary, desired filter-performance characteristics also vary. For example, since buffers widely utilized in purification processes are nonfouling, the optimum filter may be one with the highest flow rate; for solutions that may contain foulants, such as media, the optimum filter may be one with the highest filtration capacity for that solution. Optimizing the filter to the process requires determination of critical and key operational parameters and performance requirements.

Filter flow rate is affected by such parameters as the EFA, membrane porosity, pore size, thickness and area, differential pressure, flow path design, fluid viscosity, and temperature. Table 5.2-1🔍 shows the relationships between flow rate and a common list of process parameters.

Most of these parameters are governed by membrane and filter designs or particular fluid properties. The primary parameter is the differential pressure that is, the difference between inlet (feed side) and outlet (filtrate side) pressure. The differential pressure can be adjusted within the filter manufacturer supplied maximum temperature/pressure ranges to achieve the desired process flow rates. Studying the required pressure and flow rate ranges using scale-down membrane areas with the actual product stream is further elaborated in Section 5.4.1. It is important to understand this information thoroughly to determine the ranges that will be challenged during the validation activities (see Section 6.8).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

EFA (Effective Filtration Area): This is one of the most central parameters in filtration science. EFA is the membrane surface area that actually takes part in filtration and is the basis for scale-up and scale-down calculations.

Flux: The flow rate per unit area, usually expressed in L/m²/hr. It is a standardized metric for comparing the performance of filters of different sizes.

The logic of Table 5.2-1🔍: Flow rate is affected by eight factors, most of which are determined by the filter design and the liquid properties. In actual operation, the main factor you can control directly is the differential pressure -- which is why it is the "most critical adjustable parameter".

Analogy

Understanding flow rate is like understanding water flowing from a tap. Imagine the pipes in your home:

  • Pore Size = pipe diameter: the wider the pipe, the greater the water flow
  • Differential pressure (ΔP) = water pressure: open the pressure valve and the water flows more
  • Viscosity = water vs honey: honey flows slowly and needs more pressure to push it
  • Membrane thickness = pipe length: the longer the pipe, the greater the resistance
  • EFA = how many pipes are open at once: the more pipes open, the greater the total flow

Of all these factors, the one you can adjust most easily is the "water pressure" (differential pressure); the others are fixed once you have chosen the filter and the liquid.

Key Notes

Different applications have different optimization goals:

  • Non-fouling fluids such as buffers: optimization goal = maximum flow rate → choose a high-flux filter
  • Fluids containing contaminants such as culture media: optimization goal = maximum throughput (capacity) → choose a high-capacity filter

The same filter is not necessarily both the "fastest" and the one that "holds the most" -- a trade-off is needed based on the application.

Link to validation: The results of the flow rate range studies in this section will ultimately be used to define the challenge range of the validation activities in Section 6.8. The more clearly the design stage is defined, the more precise and efficient the validation activities will be.

Formula

Basic flow relationship (simplified Darcy's law):

Q = (ΔP x A x K) / (μ x L)

Where:
  Q = Flow Rate
  ΔP = Differential Pressure
  A = Effective Filtration Area (EFA)
  K = Membrane Permeability
  μ = Fluid Viscosity
  L = Membrane Thickness

Flux = Q / A = (ΔP x K) / (μ x L)

Basis for scale-up calculations:
  Q_large / Q_small = EFA_large / EFA_small
  (at the same ΔP and temperature)

Practice Questions

  1. Of the eight factors in Table 5.2-1🔍, which can be controlled by operators? Which are fixed once the filter has been selected?
  2. Why does a "Tortuous Flow Path" reduce the flow rate? What effect does it have on bacterial retention?
  3. A colleague suggests, "Since higher pressure means faster flow, just set the pressure to the maximum." What are the risks of this suggestion?
5.2.1 Inlet and Differential Pressure

Original Text

From the filtration curves it can be determined that increasing the pressure across the membrane will result in an increased flow rate under most processing conditions, which is an important variable to control during the sterilizing-filtration process. There are two important ways to specify the pressure: Differential pressure (ΔP) is the difference in pressure between the upstream (inlet) and downstream (effluent) sides of the filter, while inlet pressure is the pressure measured directly on the inlet side of the filter. To determine the differential pressure, the inlet pressure must be measured using a pressure gauge or sensor. When appropriate, outlet pressure may be assumed to be atmospheric (e.g., a vented downstream vessel). If the outlet pressure is higher than the atmospheric pressure (e.g., when the fluid is transferred into a pressurized or unvented system or if the flow rate causes a significant pressure drop downstream of the filter) either the outlet pressure must be measured, or assume that the inlet pressure represents a worst-case upper limit on the true differential pressure.

Depending on the flow regime and the safety factor to the validated maximum pressure it may be important to monitor the inlet or differential pressure of the filtration system (see Section 6.8). Increasing differential pressure at a constant flow rate is indicative of filter fouling, and detection may be important for certain types of process streams that are susceptible to fouling behavior.

Many modern filtration systems are driven by peristaltic pumps, especially in the case of SUS. As peristaltic pumps are a type of positive displacement pump, the flow rate can be easily controlled (correlated with pump revolutions per minute (RPM) and defined tubing diameter) without needing to worry about cleaning internal pump components or tubing systems; this adds a degree of safety and convenience to many filtration process designs. On the other hand, filtration can also be performed by pressurizing the system, managed by a pressure control valve, which avoids the need of a pump system. In most final sterilizing-filtration systems, the process stream is nonfouling and the filtration pressure remains relatively consistent over time. With proper system design and appropriate safety factors, constant flow rate or pressure filtration setups can operate in pressure regimes well below validated filter pressure limits. Some form of pressure control or monitoring may be an expectation of certain health authorities, even for pump-driven sterilization filtration processes that do not exhibit significant fouling.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Differential Pressure (ΔP) vs Inlet Pressure:

  • ΔP = P_inlet - P_outlet: The force that actually drives liquid through the filter. This is the core parameter affecting filtration performance.
  • Inlet Pressure: Measures only the pressure on the feed side. When the outlet is at atmospheric pressure (e.g., discharging into an open vessel), Inlet Pressure ≈ ΔP.
  • But if the outlet side is a pressurized system (e.g., a sealed tank), you must measure the outlet pressure as well to calculate the true ΔP.

Filter fouling indicator: Under constant flow rate operation, a gradual rise in differential pressure = the filter is fouling. This is an important process monitoring signal.

Two driving modes:

  • Pump-driven: a peristaltic pump controls the flow rate, and the pressure varies with resistance
  • Pressure-driven: a pressure control valve maintains constant pressure, and the flow rate varies with resistance

Analogy

The difference between differential pressure and inlet pressure is like a water tower supply:

  • Inlet pressure = the water pressure at the water tower: you can read it on the water meter
  • Outlet pressure = the water pressure at your floor: on the ground floor it is almost zero, while on the tenth floor there is noticeable back pressure
  • Differential pressure = the force that actually pushes the water: if the tower pressure is 5 bar and your back pressure is 2 bar, only 3 bar actually drives the flow

If you look only at the tower pressure and say "the water pressure is plenty" while ignoring that your back pressure is high, the actual water flow may be very small. Likewise, looking only at Inlet Pressure may overestimate the actual driving force for filtration.

Key Notes

Advantages of peristaltic pumps in single-use systems (SUS):

  • No internal parts to clean (the fluid contacts only the tubing, not the pump mechanism)
  • The flow rate is directly related to rotational speed (RPM) and tubing diameter, so it is easy to control
  • Very well suited to the single-use system concept

Regulatory expectation: Even for constant flow rate systems using peristaltic pumps, some health authorities may still require pressure monitoring. This means pressure sensors/gauges should be regarded as standard equipment rather than optional.

Safety margin principle: A properly designed system should ensure that the operating pressure is well below the validated maximum pressure limit, leaving an ample safety margin.

Formula

Differential pressure calculation:
  ΔP = P_inlet - P_outlet

Scenario 1: outlet discharges into an open vessel (vented vessel)
  P_outlet ≈ atmospheric pressure (1 atm)
  ΔP ≈ P_inlet - 1 atm

Scenario 2: outlet discharges into a pressurized system
  P_outlet = must be measured
  ΔP = P_inlet - P_outlet (measured value)

Scenario 3: outlet pressure cannot be measured
  Assume P_inlet = ΔP (worst-case upper limit)
  → This is a conservative estimate that ensures the validated pressure is not exceeded

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your aseptic filling line uses a single-use filtration system driven by a peristaltic pump. The pump is set at a fixed RPM, and normal operating pressure is about 1.2 bar. During one production run, the operator notices that the pressure has risen to 1.8 bar while the flow rate has remained unchanged.

What does this mean? -- The filter is fouling. Although the flow rate is still normal, if the pressure keeps rising toward the validated upper limit (for example, 2.5 bar), you must stop and replace the filter. This is the value of differential pressure monitoring: it gives early warning before the problem affects product quality.

Practice Questions

  1. Under what circumstances is Inlet Pressure not equal to Differential Pressure? Give an example from your facility.
  2. What are the advantages and disadvantages of peristaltic pump-driven and pressure-driven systems? As a CDMO, how would you choose?
  3. Why is a rise in differential pressure under constant flow rate operation an indicator of filter fouling? What would the fouling indicator be under constant pressure operation?
5.2.2 Filtration Process Temperature

Original Text

Process temperature has a variety of effects on the filter and fluid stream. Elevated temperatures reduce the maximum allowable differential pressure due to the strength of the mechanical structure of the filter. Pressure limits may vary between forward-and-reverse flow directions, based on filter structure (reverse flow often being considered worst-case). Pressure limits at specific process temperatures may be listed by the filter manufacturer in the qualification documentation of the particular filter type. Figure 5.2.2-1🔍 depicts an example of the relationship between process temperature and maximum differential pressure, that is, the higher the process temperature, the lower the pressure resistance of a filter. Under elevated temperature conditions, filter users should evaluate the risk factors, and if necessary, monitor process parameters carefully.

In addition to affecting differential-pressure capabilities, higher process temperatures could result in elevated leachables or chemical incompatibilities. Consideration should also be given to temperature, as some polymeric membranes are sensitive to hydrolytic and oxidative alterations by heat.

Higher temperatures reduce fluid viscosity and increase the flow rate through the filter for most Newtonian fluids. Higher viscosities could also result in the need for higher differential pressures to achieve a required flow rate. These elevated differential pressures correspondingly require increased monitoring of flow rate and/or differential pressure to avoid exceeding the rated pressure conditions.

Figure 5.2.2-1🔍 Example of Filter Pressure/Temperature Resistance
(Figure not available in text extraction — refer to original TR26 document p28)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three effects of temperature on the filtration system:

  1. Reduced mechanical strength: temperature rises → the filter material softens → the maximum allowable pressure decreases. This is an inverse relationship: the higher the temperature, the lower the allowable maximum differential pressure.
  2. Increased leachables: High temperature promotes the leaching of chemicals from the filter material, which may affect product quality and safety.
  3. Reduced fluid viscosity: temperature rises → viscosity decreases → flow rate increases. For Newtonian fluids, this is a positive effect.

Forward vs reverse flow: Pressure limits may differ between forward and reverse flow. Reverse flow is generally regarded as the worst case because the filter structure is usually optimized for forward flow.

Analogy

The effect of temperature on a filter is like the effect of weather on a rubber band:

  • Low temperature: the rubber band is stiff but strong and can withstand strong pulling (high pressure)
  • High temperature: the rubber band softens and is easily snapped (damage may occur at low pressure)
  • Fluid viscosity: like honey becoming thinner when heated -- it flows faster under the same pressure

But there is a contradiction here: high temperature makes the liquid flow more easily (good) but also makes the filter more fragile (bad). So the operating temperature needs to strike a balance between the two.

Key Notes

The inverse temperature-pressure relationship: The typical curve depicted in Figure 5.2.2-1🔍 trends downward -- as temperature rises, the maximum allowable differential pressure decreases step by step. Be sure to consult the filter manufacturer's qualification documentation to obtain the pressure limits at each temperature for the specific filter model you use.

The dual risk of leachables:

  • Product quality risk: leachables may interact with the active pharmaceutical ingredient
  • Patient safety risk: some leachables may be toxic

Leachables assessment is especially important for high-temperature operation, and it is directly related to the extractables/leachables studies in Section 4 (filter qualification).

Figure Analysis

Figure 5.2.2-1🔍 (temperature/pressure tolerance curve): Although the original figure cannot be shown here, the typical shape of the curve is as follows:

  • X axis = temperature (°C), from room temperature to the maximum use temperature
  • Y axis = maximum allowable differential pressure (bar or psi)
  • The curve slopes down to the right: the higher the temperature, the lower the maximum allowable pressure
  • There is usually a noticeable inflection point at a certain temperature, beyond which the pressure limit drops sharply

Practical significance: During process design, you must operate within the safe region of this curve, with an appropriate safety margin.

Practice Questions

  1. If you need to filter a product that must be processed at 60°C, what information do you need to confirm with the filter supplier?
  2. A rise in temperature both lowers viscosity (favorable) and lowers pressure tolerance (unfavorable). How do you balance these two factors in actual operation?
  3. Why is reverse flow usually regarded as the worst case for pressure limits? In which operating steps might reverse flow occur?
5.2.3 Filtration Time

Original Text

Filtration time (duration) is the amount of time required to filter a single batch (or campaign) of production fluid. The process duration will differ depending on if the process is a single bulk-sterilizing filtration operation or an intermittent in-line-filtration operation used to maintain the level in a break tank connected to dosing pumps during a sterile filling process. Increasing filtration times may increase the potential for bacterial penetration; therefore, the filter retentive performance should be validated to the maximum product/filter contact time for the process (see Section 6.8).

As part of the development of the process an assessment should be conducted to determine the longest potential product-contact time with the sterilizing filter. The worst-case process time should include maximum filling-process duration, including manufacturing interruption scenarios prior to and during filtration.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Time characteristics of the two filtration modes:

  • Single Bulk Filtration: The entire batch of drug solution is filtered once into a sterile holding tank. The filtration time is relatively fixed and short.
  • Intermittent In-line Filtration: Matched to the speed of the filling machine, the drug solution is filtered continuously into a break tank, from which the filling pump draws. The filtration time equals the duration of the whole filling process, which may last several hours or even a whole day.

Filtration time vs product contact time:

  • Filtration time: the duration of actual filtration
  • Product contact time: = filtration time + waiting time + interruption time. This is the stricter definition and is used for validation.

Analogy

Imagine filtering tap water with a water filter pitcher:

  • Batch filtration: fill the whole pitcher at once and wait for it to finish filtering -- the contact time is the time needed to complete filtration
  • In-line filtration: you add water while you drink, and the pitcher works from morning to night -- the contact time is the whole day

The longer the time, the higher the risk of bacteria penetrating the filter -- like a door left open longer gives mosquitoes more chances to fly in. Therefore the validation must cover the longest possible contact time.

Key Notes

The relationship between bacterial penetration risk and time: Time is a key worst-case parameter in bacterial retention validation. The reasons include:

  • Long contact gives bacteria more opportunity to find and penetrate the membrane pores
  • Some bacteria may change morphology (e.g., shrink in size) after contact with the filter
  • Prolonged soaking may affect the integrity of the filter material

Manufacturing Interruption Scenarios: The worst-case time must include unplanned interruptions. Examples: filling machine breakdowns, environmental monitoring alarms, and equipment cleaning, which may pause filtration while the filter remains in contact with the product.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your filling line usually fills a batch in 8 hours, but occasionally an equipment failure requires a 2-hour pause, and the production procedure allows a maximum of two pauses. Your worst-case product contact time is then 8 + 2 + 2 = 12 hours. Your bacterial retention validation must cover a contact time of at least 12 hours; otherwise, if actual production exceeds the validated range, that batch may face compliance challenges.

Practice Questions

  1. In your facility, what is the maximum product contact time for sterilizing filtration? Have all possible interruption scenarios been taken into account?
  2. How do the time-related risks of batch filtration and in-line filtration differ? Which mode requires stricter time validation?
5.3 Filter Throughput and Maximum Volume

Original Text

Filter throughput, or capacity, is the measurement of a total volume that can be filtered through the EFA under defined process conditions. Filter capacity depends on membrane material, porosity, pore size, nonspecific adsorption, pore shape, filtration area, pressure differential, contaminant load, and the physical properties of the product type being filtered. Table 5.3-1🔍 lists potential factors affecting throughput.

Many process fluids that undergo sterilizing filtration are in the final formulation and do not result in significant fouling or filter-flux degradation over any practical volume. In many cases, there is no practical limit to the throughput, while in other cases, there can be particulate matter or subvisible particles present in the filtrate that do limit the filter throughput. In some cases, the prior processing history of the filtrate can affect the capacity (e.g., high-shear mixing of monoclonal antibodies can generate agglomerates) (27). Appropriate scale-down studies can be used to determine these behaviors as outlined in Section 5.4. Even in the case where no defined maximum filter capacity can be determined, the maximum volume that will be filtered should be specified so it can be covered during the filter-validation studies (see Section 6.8).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts
