Filter Throughput: The total volume a filter can process under specific process conditions. This differs from flow rate -- flow rate is "how fast", throughput is "how much".

Key insights from Table 5.3-1🔍:

  • Asymmetric vs Isotropic Pore Shape: The pores of an asymmetric membrane narrow gradually from the feed side to the outlet side, forming progressive retention that can hold more particles → higher throughput
  • Deformable, Soft Contaminant: Soft particles (such as protein aggregates) are pressed into the membrane pores and cause more severe plugging → lower throughput. Hard particles stay on the surface and do not plug deep into the membrane
  • Shear-Stress History: High-shear operations upstream in the process (such as high-speed mixing) may generate protein aggregates, directly affecting filtration throughput

Analogy

Imagine brewing coffee with a paper filter:

  • Flow rate: how fast the water passes through the filter paper -- "how fast"
  • Throughput: how many cups this filter paper can brew in total before it clogs -- "how much"
  • Soft vs hard contaminants: Fine coffee grounds (soft, deformable) work their way into the paper fibers and clog it quickly. Coarse bean fragments (hard) stay on the surface, and the paper lasts longer
  • Upstream process effects: If you grind the beans too fine (= high shear), the filter paper clogs faster

Key Notes

Many final formulations are non-fouling: TR26 specifically points out that many drug solutions that undergo sterilizing filtration are final formulations that in practice do not cause noticeable filter fouling. In these cases throughput limitation is not a technical issue, but you still need to specify a maximum filtration volume for validation.

The effect of high-shear mixing: TR26 cites an important case -- high-shear mixing of a monoclonal antibody generates agglomerates, directly affecting filtration throughput. This reminds us that filtration performance depends not only on current operating conditions but also on the upstream process history.

Specify a maximum volume even when there is no throughput limitation: This is a validation requirement. Validation must have clearly defined boundary conditions.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your client provides a mAb product for sterilizing filtration. In small-scale testing, you find that the flow rate begins to drop noticeably at 500 L/m². Further investigation shows that the client's upstream process used a high-speed mixer for blending, which generated protein aggregates.

Solutions: (1) recommend that the client reduce the mixing speed or switch to low-shear mixing; (2) add a prefiltration step to remove the aggregates; (3) increase the EFA (use a larger filter) to ensure sufficient throughput; (4) include the upstream mixing conditions in the process design space.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can an Asymmetric Pore Shape provide higher throughput than an Isotropic Pore Shape? Explain from a structural perspective.
  2. If your process liquid has never caused filter fouling in actual operation, do you still need to perform throughput testing? Why?
  3. How does the shear history of the upstream process affect downstream sterilizing filtration performance? What does this teach a CDMO about cross-functional collaboration?
5.4 Scale-Down Systems and Testing

Original Text

PD activities are generally done at the bench-scale using scale-down systems to minimize material quantities and allow for faster and more efficient experimentation. In order for the scale-down systems to be representative of the larger scales used in manufacturing the available systems and scaling considerations are discussed in this section.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Scale-Down Systems: Simulate the filtration conditions of large-scale production at bench scale. The core purpose is to obtain representative process data from a small amount of material in order to predict commercial-scale performance.

Key requirement: the scale-down system must be "representative" of the filtration behavior of large-scale manufacturing. This means you cannot scale down arbitrarily -- key parameters (such as flux, ΔP, and temperature) must be kept consistent.

Analogy

Scale-down testing is like the architectural model an architect builds: you use a 1:100 scale model to test structural strength and airflow. The model must be scaled down precisely in proportion, not just any small house. Likewise, a filtration scale-down system must be scaled down in proportion to the EFA while maintaining the same flow rate (flux) and pressure conditions; otherwise the test results cannot be scaled up to actual production.

Key Notes

The "representativeness" of the scale-down system is the foundation of the entire process development. If the results of small-scale testing cannot accurately predict large-scale performance, all subsequent design decisions will be built on unreliable data. This is why this section discusses the available system types and scale-up considerations in depth.

Practice Questions

  1. Why are process development (PD) activities performed at small scale rather than large scale? Besides saving material, what other considerations are there?
  2. If small-scale test results are inconsistent with large-scale production results, which areas would you investigate to find the cause?
5.4.1 Small Scale Device Testing

Original Text

Filter-screening is generally performed using the process fluid in a constant flow or constant pressure experimental setup. Traditionally, 25mm or 47 mm discs or disc composites (Figure 5.4.1-1🔍) installed in holders (Figure 5.4.1-2🔍) were used for these screening studies. With modern filtration systems, however, most filter manufacturers have developed specialized encapsulated discs or small pleated capsules that better represent the flow dynamics of full-sized filters and allow closer estimates of scaling by EFA (see Figure 4.2-2🔍). The screening tests can also be helpful to determine potential prefilter combinations to optimize the total throughput and reduce fouling, where required. For yield-sensitive applications, filtrate samples can be taken at frequent intervals to determine any nonspecific adsorption effects. Small-scale device tests may not always accurately predict the actual process in regard to flow-path dynamics and pleat-density parameters, however, and therefore may not scale up in a linear fashion, so care must be taken in the experimental design to ensure that any throughput or flux limitations are well understood.

Figure 5.4.1-1🔍 Filter Disc / Figure 5.4.1-2🔍 Filter Disc Holder
(Figures not available in text extraction — refer to original TR26 document p30)

Most filterability tests are performed by keeping pressure constant and measuring the decline in flow rate as a function of filtered volume. The pressure set-point should mimic production pressure conditions. Nevertheless, there are some applications that should be evaluated using constant flow rate, such as the filtration of media. For constant-flow testing, the flow rate is controlled while the pressure gradually increases until a maximum pressure is reached. This mode of testing avoids compression of contaminants that may be captured on the membrane surface, leading to membrane fouling. Either constant-flow or constant-pressure filterability tests can be used to predict manufacturing-scale performance. Ideally, small-scale tests should be performed using flux and pressure conditions representative of the manufacturing-scale process or a representative worst-case scenario.

The main output of these small-scale filtration studies is to develop and understand the flow curves and evaluate filter flux at different pressure conditions, thus allowing selection of the optimal filter size and configuration to use in the at-scale process. If properly designed, the fouling propensity of the filter and process fluid combinations can also determine a maximum filter-throughput value. With this information under representative temperature and process conditions, the process can be scaled up using the ratio of EFA.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Evolution of filter screening:

  • Traditional method: test with 25 mm or 47 mm flat membrane discs mounted in holders
  • Modern method: use encapsulated discs or small pleated capsules, which better represent the flow dynamics of full-size filters in actual production

Two test modes:

  • Constant Pressure: The pressure is fixed and the decline in flow rate is measured as filtered volume increases. Suitable for most applications.
  • Constant Flow Rate: The flow rate is fixed and the rise in pressure is measured as filtered volume increases. Suitable for fluids containing contaminants (such as culture media), because constant pressure operation may compress contaminants on the membrane surface and cause more severe plugging.

Limits of linear scale-up: Because of differences in flow-path dynamics and pleat density, small-scale results do not necessarily scale up linearly. The experimental design must fully take these limitations into account.

Analogy

Small-scale testing is like trying on clothes. The traditional 47 mm disc test is like estimating from a size chart -- you only get a rough sense of size. The modern small pleated capsule test is like actually trying on a small size in the store -- you get a much better feel for how it fits and how comfortable it is.

But even actually trying it on does not guarantee that the larger size of the same style will fit exactly the same -- the pattern may differ subtly after scaling up. Likewise, small-scale filtration test results may show non-linear deviations when scaled up to production scale.

Key Notes

The logic for choosing between constant pressure and constant flow rate:

  • Constant pressure testing is more common and is the default method recommended by most vendors
  • But for fluids containing compressible contaminants (such as culture media), constant pressure forces contaminants into the membrane pores, causing non-representative, severe plugging
  • Constant flow rate testing avoids this problem because the pressure rises gradually

Prefilter Combinations: Small-scale testing can also be used to evaluate the effect of prefilters -- adding a prefilter with a larger pore size ahead of the sterilizing-grade filter removes large particles, extends the life of the sterilizing-grade filter, and lowers overall cost.

Yield-Sensitive Applications: For high-value biologics, samples must be taken frequently during filtration to measure product concentration and confirm that adsorption loss is within an acceptable range.

Formula

EFA proportional scale-up calculation:

Scale-up factor = EFA_production / EFA_small-scale

For example:
  Small-scale test: EFA = 3.5 cm² (47 mm disc)
  Production needs to process 500 L; the small scale can process 50 mL

  Required scale-up factor = 500,000 mL / 50 mL = 10,000
  EFA required at production scale = 3.5 cm² × 10,000 = 35,000 cm² = 3.5 m²

Note: This calculation assumes linear scale-up. In practice,
a safety factor (typically 1.2-2.0x) may need to be added to
compensate for non-linear effects and differences in pleat density.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: You need to select a sterilizing filter for a new mAb product. You have small test capsules from three candidate filter vendors.

Suggested screening procedure:

  1. Using the same process liquid, test the three filters under constant pressure conditions (simulating production pressure)
  2. Plot the flow rate vs filtered volume curve (Flow Curve)
  3. Compare the throughput (L/m²), fouling tendency, and adsorption performance of each filter
  4. After selecting the best candidate, use the EFA proportional calculation to size the filter required at production scale
  5. Add a 1.5-2.0x safety factor to compensate for uncertainty in the scale-up

Practice Questions

  1. Why are modern small pleated capsules more representative of production-scale performance than traditional 47 mm flat discs? Explain from the standpoint of flow dynamics.
  2. Under what circumstances would you choose constant flow rate testing over constant pressure testing? Answer based on your actual process experience.
  3. If small-scale testing shows that the filter plugs at 200 L/m² but your production requirement is 180 L/m², do you think the safety margin is sufficient? How would you decide?
PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p31 - p39
5.4.2 Filtration Process-Scaling Considerations

Original Text

Scaling up filtration performance from a laboratory-scale filter unit to a production-scale system, which might contain multiple cartridges or units, poses several challenges to the filter user. Challenges include, but are not limited to:

  • Product for filter trials might be in limited supply due to high production costs and limited production runs.
  • Surrogate fluids might not be representative of the actual process fluid for certain behaviors like fouling or non-Newtonian flow dynamics.
  • Small-scale filter studies may not represent the conditions under which filtration occurs in the manufacturing process.
  • Laboratory-scale filter elements used for testing may not be directly predictive of large-scale filter capacity.

To overcome these challenges, filter users may elect to work with the filter manufacturer(s) to design small-scale studies that either closely approximate production systems or, alternatively, rely on appropriate safety factors to account for unknown variables and ensure successful filtration operations at scale.

Careful attention should be paid to ensuring that the material used for the scale-down studies is representative of the materials used for commercial production. In the production of proteins, there have been several documented examples where shear stresses imparted on the molecule upstream in the process result in the formation of subvisible particulates that can cause filter-fouling (27). In many cases the same solution that has not been subjected to the same levels of shear stress will show no signs of fouling behavior; this represents a scale-up risk that can be difficult to characterize that can manifest itself as filter-clogging during production. In this instance, considerable study may be required to create appropriate scale-down models of this shear behavior, as not all protein solutions and mixer types react in the same fashion. This is just one example of the importance of understanding the full-scale process and the stress history of the filtrate and how they affect filtration performance. There are also instances where freeze-thaw or shipping conditions of solutions, such as lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) or lentivirus drug products can affect the filtration performance (e.g., reduced volume throughput). It is critical that the impact of these factors is understood to ensure small-scale studies provide representative data.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Scale-up vs scale-down: Filtration process development usually starts at small laboratory scale and is then scaled up to production scale. A scale-down study uses small-scale equipment to simulate production conditions, generating the most data from the least material.

Shear stress: The mechanical force a protein solution experiences as it passes through pumps, mixers, piping and other upstream process equipment. High shear can cause protein aggregation and the formation of subvisible particulates, which can then block the sterilizing-grade filter.

Surrogate fluid: When actual product supply is limited or too costly, a simulated fluid is used in place of the product for preliminary testing. However, a surrogate may not fully reproduce the blocking behavior of the real product.

Analogy

Imagine you try a recipe in a small pot in your home kitchen (the laboratory) and it works well. But when you cook the same dish in a large pot in a wedding-banquet kitchen (production scale), uneven heating, different stirring force and ingredient batch differences can produce a completely different result. A scale-down study is like deliberately recreating the conditions of the big pot in a small pot, so that you can truly predict the quality of what the big pot will produce.

Key Notes

The "stress history" of the protein matters greatly: The same protein solution, after processing with different upstream equipment (different mixers, different pumps), can show completely different filtration behavior. This is why TR26 stresses that the stress history of the filtrate must be understood.

Special risks for LNP and lentiviral products: Freeze-thaw or shipping conditions can change the physical properties of these novel modalities and sharply reduce filtration flux. Small-scale studies must use material that has gone through the same handling history.

Practical Application

In a CDMO setting, clients often provide a limited quantity of clinical-batch material for process development. If you run filtration tests only with WFI and skip testing with real product, discovering filter blockage during GMP manufacturing is extremely costly: you lose an entire batch and may delay clinical timelines. Recommended practice: run small-scale filtration studies with at least some real product, and apply a safety factor (typically 1.5-2x) to cover unknown variables.

Practice Questions

  1. Why might the same protein solution not block the filter in the laboratory but block it in the plant? What are the possible causes?
  2. If your client can supply only 500 mL of drug product for filtration development studies, how would you design the experiments to maximize the value of the data?
  3. Why do the shipping conditions of an LNP drug product affect filtration performance? What does this mean for a CDMO's material receipt procedure?
5.5 Filtration System Design and Integration

Original Text

A user requirement specification (URS) is a helpful tool in designing a SUS or stainless-steel setup. The filter user should have detailed knowledge of all process operational parameters to define the requirements of a particular assembly. The suitability of particular components depends on the requirements of the product and process. URSs typically include:

  • Flow rate
  • Pressure conditions
  • Temperature conditions
  • MoC
  • Filter (pore size, area)
  • Product volumes
  • Instrumentation
  • Sampling requirements
  • System configuration
  • Fluid properties
  • Integrity-test methods and needs
  • Sterilization method
  • Documentation requirements

In addition to the integrity-test considerations stated in Section 7.0, the following points should be considered for filtration systems:

  • Pressurization of the upstream side of the filter with gas after wetting
  • Need for a downstream vent filter to accommodate the gas flow
  • Mechanism to aseptically remove the wetting fluid from the system, if necessary
  • Use of a hydrophilic drain filter and a separate hydrophobic vent filter or other appropriate valves and drains for the system
  • Use of a combination hydrophilic/hydrophobic vent filter, where applicable
  • Appropriate placement of process controls, such as pressure sensors or flowmeters, and required sampling locations
"Note: Use of separate filters enables both to be integrity-tested in situ with water, whereas a combination hydrophilic/hydrophobic filter can only be tested with alcohol, off-line."

The qualification tests listed in Section 4.1 are performed within the manufacturer's defined conditions. Assemblies of components require validation within the filter/single-use user's process and environment. Validation should be performed to cover actual or worst-case process conditions, preferably with the product or a model (surrogate) solution. The single-use component or assembly should meet process and regulatory requirements. The validation effort should ensure that the components/assembly functions as intended and does not negatively influence product attributes, neither the product and process conditions, nor the single-use components or assembly.

Furthermore, functionality tests verify that desired process conditions, (e.g., filtration time) can be met. Documented evidence should be established that operational parameter specifications can be met repeatedly.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

User Requirement Specification (URS): Before designing the filtration system, all process parameters, quality requirements and regulatory requirements must first be defined in writing. The URS is the starting point of the design and the baseline for later validation.

Hydrophilic vs hydrophobic filters: Hydrophilic filters are used for liquid filtration (including drain lines), and hydrophobic filters for gas filtration (vents). For ease of integrity testing, choosing separate filters versus a combined filter leads to different testing strategies.

Worst-case process conditions: Validation must cover actual or worst-case process conditions. This is the key principle for ensuring that the filtration system performs properly in commercial production whatever conditions are encountered.

Analogy

A URS is like the building brief you prepare before building a house: how many rooms, how large a living room, what piping is needed, what load the structure must bear. Assembling a filtration system without a clear URS is like starting construction without blueprints: by the time you discover that the piping does not connect or the load capacity is insufficient, rebuilding costs far more than planning properly at the start.

Key Notes

Integrity-testing differences between separate and combined filters: This is an easily overlooked decision with a large practical impact. If a combined hydrophilic/hydrophobic filter is used, only off-line alcohol testing is possible, which means extra handling steps and added risk. Separate filters can be tested in situ with water, which is simpler to operate and better aligned with regulatory expectations.

Manufacturer qualification is not user validation: The qualification tests a filter manufacturer performs under its own defined conditions cannot directly replace the validation the user performs in the user's own process environment. A CDMO must demonstrate that the system works under its own actual process conditions.

Practice Questions

  1. List the five most critical parameters in the URS when you design a new sterilizing filtration system, and explain why they matter most.
  2. Under what circumstances would you choose a combined hydrophilic/hydrophobic filter rather than separate filters? What factors must this decision take into account?
  3. Why can the filter manufacturer's qualification data not directly replace the user's process validation?
5.5.1 Multiple Filters in Series

Original Text

Manufacturing processes are frequently designed with trains of multiple filters in a series (sequential flow through one filter after another), but the purpose of these extra filters varies from process to process, and the design and purpose of each filter must be clearly documented.

5.5.1.1 Two-Filter Sterilization (Double Filtration)

In uncommon processes, where two filters together in series are both required to achieve sterility and the train has been validated to achieve the sterilization of a specific product only when passing through both filters, the filter train can be considered a single sterilizing unit, and both filters must successfully pass the integrity test.

5.5.1.2 Pre-Filtration

One of the most common purposes for additional filters upstream of the final sterilizing filter is for prefiltration functions, as opposed to sterilization. An example is bioburden reduction that may be performed with a sterilizing-grade filter, but the filter is not performing a strict sterilizing function. Rather, it is assuring that a very low bioburden level is present before entering the final sterilizing filter.

Other prefilters may be performing functions unrelated to microbial control, such as aggregate or particle removal prior to the final sterilizing filtration, that may not use sterilizing-grade filters.

5.5.1.3 Redundant Filtration

Processes where an additional (redundant) sterilizing filter is placed in the filter train to ensure against loss of product due to potential-integrity test failure of the primary sterilizing filter. The additional "secondary" filter is typically identical to the primary sterilizing filter, is fully validated to sterilize the fluid on its own and undergoes post-use integrity-testing as a contingency in the event the primary filter fails. Generally, the filter closest to filling (farthest downstream) should be the primary filter (10). The pressure and flow-rate controls are generally designed to apply across both sterilizing filters.

Figure 5.5-1🔍, Figure 5.5-2🔍, Figure 5.5-3🔍, and 5.5-4 illustrate different filter trains that can be used for the filtration operation (see Section 7.4.3 for additional information).

Note: DP/DS stands for drug product/drug substance.

Figure 5.5-1🔍 Single Filter Train Configuration

Figure 5.5-2🔍 Pre-Filter Train Configuration

Figure 5.5-3🔍 Redundant Sterilizing Filter Train Configuration

Figure 5.5-4🔍 "Double" Filtration Train Configuration (uncommon)

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three series filtration configurations:

  • Double filtration: Two filters jointly perform the sterilizing function; neither can be omitted, and together they are treated as a single sterilizing unit. This is a rare configuration, normally used only for special products.
  • Pre-filtration: The upstream filter reduces bioburden or removes particulates to protect the downstream final sterilizing filter, but it does not itself perform the sterilizing function.
  • Redundant filtration: A backup sterilizing filter, each filter independently capable of full sterilization. If the primary filter fails its integrity test, the backup filter can save the whole batch.

Position of the primary filter: In general, the filter closest to the filling point (the most downstream) should be designated the primary filter.

Analogy

Think of airport security screening:

  • Pre-filtration = the baggage X-ray machine (initial screening that removes obviously prohibited items)
  • Redundant filtration = two security gates in a row: even if the first gate fails, the second still catches everything
  • Double filtration = one security check that needs two devices operating together to complete the inspection (a very rare, special design)

Key Notes

The economics of redundant filtration: Adding one more sterilizing filter may cost only a few thousand US dollars, but if the primary filter fails its integrity test and there is no backup, the entire batch (potentially worth hundreds of thousands to millions of US dollars) must be scrapped. This is a very clear risk-management decision.

The purpose of each filter must be clearly documented: Regulators review the role of each filter in the filter train. If the documentation is unclear (for example, a pre-filter described as having a "sterilizing" function), additional validation requirements may be triggered.

Practical Application

In a CDMO setting, different clients' products may need different filter train configurations. It is advisable to establish a standard platform configuration (for example, pre-filter plus redundant sterilizing filter as the baseline) and then adjust it to product characteristics and client needs. This reduces repeated work in system design and validation while ensuring that every project has clear documentation of the role of each filter.

Practice Questions

  1. If both your redundant filter and your primary filter pass the post-use integrity test, do you still need to keep the redundant filter configuration? Why?
  2. For what kind of product characteristics might you consider double filtration instead of redundant filtration?
  3. Why is the primary filter usually placed at the most downstream position (closest to filling)?
5.6 Pre-Filtration Bioburden Monitoring

Original Text

An important aspect of filtration process design is pre-filtration bioburden monitoring: a common regulatory expectation. The inlet to the final sterilizing filter should have well-controlled bioburden levels, despite the extreme bacterial removal efficiency typically validated in retention studies (see Section 6.6) (28). The difference between the low bioburden levels routinely entering the sterilizing filter, and the high levels validated to be removed by retention studies provides a large safety factor which is an important principle in sterilization by filtration. Factors to consider when designing a pre-filtration bioburden monitoring program include:

  • Relevant regulatory expectations for allowable bioburden limit (i.e., the acceptance criteria of the test)
  • Representative sampling time:
    • If bioburden levels could rise over the course of the filtration operation, sampling at or near the end of the filtration could be warranted. For short filtration durations or bacteriostatic/bactericidal fluids, sampling at the beginning of the filtration may be appropriate.
  • Location of the sample: the pre-filtration bioburden sample should be taken immediately upstream of the sterilizing filter (or, in cases of redundant filtration, upstream of the first sterilizing filter), and downstream of any bioburden reduction filters.
  • Impact of preservatives on the bioburden assay.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Pre-filtration bioburden monitoring: Measuring the microbial content of the liquid before it enters the final sterilizing filter. This is a general regulatory expectation, intended to confirm that the bioburden challenging the sterilizing filter is within a controlled range.

The concept of safety factor: Bacterial retention validation studies (Section 6.6) typically use a very high challenge concentration (for example, 107 CFU/cm2), whereas the bioburden reaching the filter in actual production is far lower. The gap between the two is the safety factor: the larger the gap, the greater the confidence in sterility assurance.

Analogy

This is like the baggage X-ray check before airport security. Although the security gate (the sterilizing filter) has already been validated to stop weapons, you still want to scan the baggage by X-ray before it reaches the gate, to confirm that most problem items have already been detected. That way, even if the gate occasionally makes a small error, overall safety is still assured. Pre-filtration bioburden monitoring is that baggage X-ray.

Key Notes

The choice of sampling time point is critical:

  • If the product has no bacteriostatic component and the filtration time is long, microorganisms may multiply during filtration; sampling should then be done at the "end" of filtration, representing the worst case.
  • If the product itself is bacteriostatic (for example, it contains a preservative), microorganisms are unlikely to grow during filtration, and sampling can be done at the "start" of filtration.

Sampling location: Samples must be taken "immediately upstream" of the sterilizing filter. If a bioburden reduction filter is further upstream, the sampling point should be downstream of it but upstream of the sterilizing filter.

Interference from preservatives with testing: Some preservatives inhibit or kill the microorganisms in the test culture, producing false-negative results. This must be considered in method validation.

Practice Questions

  1. If your product contains 0.02% polysorbate 80 (a non-bacteriostatic concentration) and the filtration time is 4 hours, at what time point would you take the bioburden sample? Why?
  2. Even though bacterial retention validation has shown that the filter can retain extremely high bacterial concentrations, why do regulators still require pre-filtration bioburden monitoring?
  3. If the pre-filtration bioburden exceeds the acceptance criterion, what actions would you take? Would this affect batch release?
5.7 Flushing Conditions/Filter Priming

Original Text

Sterilizing-grade filters generally must be flushed and primed prior to use, either in the process of integrity-testing or as a separate step, and filter manufacturers typically have recommendations for minimum flush volumes. Pre-use flushing with water for injection (WFI), buffer, product, or sequential combinations as necessary may be used to reduce leachables and can be important to reduce the level of nonspecific adsorption and ensure that the filtrate is homogeneous during the process. Filter-priming is also necessary to remove air from the filters and ensure that the filter membrane is fully wet thus making use of all of the filter EFA during the process. For specific flushing considerations related to pre-use post-sterilization integrity test (PUPSIT) refer to Section 7.0.

Pre-use flush volumes are typically determined empirically at scale for each installation based on the needs of the process. Depending on the propensity of the filter to adsorb the product or excipients contained in the product stream, there may also be a specific flushing solution used. For example, polysorbate or poloxamer surfactants are common excipients in the final formulation of many protein-based pharmaceutical products. Depending on the filter chemistry and the formulation composition, the saturation of adsorption sites on the filter material with these excipients can occur considerably slower than the saturation of adsorption sites that chemically bind the protein active ingredient, leading to reduced excipient concentrations at the beginning of the filtration. In these instances, a surfactant containing flush buffer can considerably reduce the required product flush volume, significantly increasing the product yield.

The flush steps can be estimated using a scale down system, although due to the flow dynamics and different surface area-to-volume ratios between small-and-large scales, they require confirmation at full scale. In these instances, a larger flush volume, calculated as a multiple of the EFA-ratio scale-down volume required, is used, the flush is done in a stepwise fashion, and the material is collected after the filter. This fractionated material can then be subjected to characterization testing, such as TOC, excipient concentration, or protein-specific assays that can be used to generate a filter-flush curve to determine when the leachables are removed, the filter reaches saturation equilibrium and the filtrate is completely homogeneous. Nonproduct flushes may need to be chased with a product flush to avoid diluting effects and return the filtrate to a homogeneous state.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Filter flushing: Flushing the filter with WFI, buffer or product serves three purposes: (1) reducing leachables, (2) saturating the adsorption sites on the filter to reduce non-specific adsorption, and (3) ensuring a homogeneous filtrate.

Filter priming: Removing air from the filter and ensuring that the membrane is fully wetted, so that all of the effective filtration area (EFA) takes part in filtration. Areas that are not fully wetted mean that you have paid for filtration area you are not using.

Filter-flush curve: By collecting the flush fluid in fractions and analyzing it (TOC, excipient concentration, etc.), you plot a curve showing how leachables fall and the filtrate becomes homogeneous as flush volume increases, and use it to determine the optimal flush volume.

Analogy

Think of brewing tea. The first infusion releases the most off-flavors (like leachables), and the teapot itself absorbs some tea components (like non-specific adsorption). An experienced tea master first warms the pot with hot water and discards the first infusion, so that the tea you actually serve is clean and even. Filter flushing works on exactly the same principle: flush away impurities and saturate the adsorption sites first, so that the subsequent product filtrate has stable quality.

Key Notes

The time lag in surfactant adsorption: This is a very practical problem in protein drug manufacturing. Surfactants such as polysorbate 80 may take much longer than the protein active ingredient to saturate their adsorption on the filter, so the surfactant concentration in the early filtrate is too low. If this is not addressed, the initial product may fail the formulation specification because of insufficient surfactant.

Solution: Pre-flush the filter with a surfactant-containing buffer to saturate the adsorption sites first. This greatly reduces the volume of product that must be discarded as flush and directly improves product recovery.

Small-scale vs full-scale confirmation: Flush volume can be estimated from small-scale experiments, but because of differences in fluid dynamics and in the surface-area-to-volume ratio, it must ultimately be confirmed at full scale.

Formula

Flush volume scale-up estimate:
Full-scale flush volume = small-scale flush volume × (full-scale EFA / small-scale EFA) × safety factor

Example: small scale (EFA = 13 cm²) requires a 50 mL flush
    Full scale (EFA = 13,000 cm²):
    = 50 mL × (13,000/13) × 1.5
    = 50 mL × 1,000 × 1.5
    = 75,000 mL = 75 L

The safety factor is usually 1.2-2.0, depending on the application

Practical Application

For a high-value protein drug (for example, a monoclonal antibody), each milliliter of product may be worth hundreds of US dollars. Discarding 5 L of product flush could mean a loss of hundreds of thousands of US dollars. By developing a surfactant-containing pre-flush step that cuts the product flush from 5 L to 1 L, substantial cost can be saved on every batch. This kind of process optimization is an important value-added service in a CDMO.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does polysorbate reach adsorption saturation on the filter more slowly than protein? How does this relate to the molecular properties of the two?
  2. How would you design a filter-flush curve experiment to determine the optimal flush volume? Which indicators would you measure?
  3. Why is a further flush with product (a product chase) needed after the non-product flush?
5.8 Filter Handling and Installation

Original Text

An important part of the development of a sterilizing filtration process involves the installation and handling of the sterilizing filters in the manufacturing environment. Filter cartridges must be installed into a filter housing. Filters encapsulated in a polymeric housing may be part of a pre-assembled SUS assembly. Most commonly, the filter cartridge will be installed into the filter housing and heat sterilized via in-line steam sterilization or autoclaving. Capsule filters are commonly supplied presterilized via irradiation, or they can be autoclaved by the end user.

5.8.1 Cartridge Handling and Installation Considerations

By following these guidelines for cartridge-handling during the development of the process, users can ensure the proper installation, maintenance, and performance of the filtration system.

1. Identification and Receipt

  • Cartridges should be identified by checking the part number, lot number, and description.
  • Enclosed documentation such as the quality certificate or certificate of conformance should be reviewed to verify the correct filter type and conformance with the specifications.
  • The outer and inner packaging material should be checked for damage with processes put in place to segregate and evaluate any instances of damage.

2. Installation

  • Cartridges should be installed in the classified area as defined within the approved contamination control strategy (CCS) to mitigate contamination.
  • Cartridges should be handled with care and protected by the packaging during installation to avoid contamination or unnecessary operator contact.
  • Cartridges should be inserted into the filter-housing recess or adapter without twisting and ensuring a secure fit without forcing it in place. If the cartridge includes locking tabs, the cartridge should be rotated into position by holding it near the filter's base rather than at the closed end.
  • If needed, the manufacturer's recommendations for lubricating O-rings and using a product-compatible liquid should be followed.
  • Proper alignment and secure installation to prevent leaks or bypass.
  • Cartridges must be handled carefully to avoid damage to the filter media. Ensure proper procedures or instructions are in place to note any dropping or rough handling of the filtration system during installation.

3. Wetting

  • Some filter cartridges are required to be wet with water before steam sterilization or autoclave cycles. In that case care should be taken to achieve saturated steam conditions on the porous membrane structure during the sterilization phase and to ensure the differential pressure stays within the required conditions during the different sterilization phases.
  • Filter manufacturers typically have process recommendations on how to wet the filter to ensure the entire membrane matrix is properly wet.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Filter cartridge vs capsule filter:

  • Cartridge: Must be installed in a stainless steel or plastic housing, and can be sterilized by steam-in-place or by autoclave. Suitable for fixed equipment that is used repeatedly.
  • Capsule: The membrane is already encapsulated in a polymer shell, and is normally presterilized (by gamma or X-ray irradiation) as part of a single-use system (SUS).

Contamination control strategy (CCS): Defines the grade of environment in which the cartridge should be installed, to reduce contamination risk. This is one of the core requirements of EU GMP Annex 1.

Analogy

Installing a cartridge is like a surgeon preparing surgical instruments. You must: (1) first confirm that the instrument model and lot number are correct (identification), (2) open the packaging in a sterile environment (classified area), (3) handle it gently without touching critical surfaces (aseptic technique), and (4) confirm that it is correctly seated and not loose (secure fit). An error at any one step can cause the whole aseptic operation to fail.

Key Notes

Do not force the cartridge into place: TR26 states explicitly that the cartridge must not be installed by "forcing it in place." Forced installation can damage the O-ring seal or the membrane itself, causing bypass: liquid passes around the membrane, the sterilizing function is lost entirely, and no one notices.

Drop events must be recorded: Even if the cartridge looks undamaged, a drop or rough handling must be recorded and assessed. This is a basic GMP requirement and one of the last lines of defense protecting product safety.

Timing of wetting and sterilization: Some cartridges must be wetted with water before steam sterilization. This ensures that steam penetrates the porous membrane structure evenly during sterilization. Dry hydrophobic areas can obstruct steam penetration and create cold spots.

Practice Questions

  1. If an operator accidentally drops a cartridge on the floor (grade B environment) while installing it, how would you handle it? What is the decision process for replacing it with a new cartridge?
  2. Why should a cartridge be held at the bottom rather than the top during installation? How does this relate to the structural design of the cartridge?
  3. Why can a capsule filter usually not be steam-sterilized in place (in-line steaming)? What are the material and design limitations?
5.8.2 In-Line Steam Sterilization Considerations

Original Text

In-line steam sterilization is one of the main sources of potential damage to a filter. Well-defined standard operating procedures (SOPs), a proper training program, and close qualification and monitoring of pressure conditions are essential to prevent damage during steam sterilization. In addition to the sterilization considerations outlined in Section 8.0, the following items should be considered during PD to ensure the filter is not subjected to undue stress during steam sterilization.

1. Positioning

  • During steam sterilization, the cartridge should be positioned to prevent the accumulation of condensate, allowing drainage from the housing.

2. Temperature and Pressure Monitoring

  • Pressure gauges should be installed as appropriate to monitor pressure conditions during the steam sterilization process or during qualification activities.
  • Excessive pressure conditions during steam sterilization can potentially damage the membrane of the filter cartridge.

3. Potential Damage

  • Reverse-steaming of the filter at elevated differential pressure conditions can cause damage to the filter, leading to issues like pleat-pack movement, cage imprinting, or cartridge deformation.

4. Precautions

  • Care should be taken to avoid condensation vacuum by pressurizing the system or venting through appropriate sterilizing-vent filters.

By following these guidelines for steam sterilization of filter cartridges, end users can ensure the integrity of the filtration system and ensure the effective sterilization of the cartridges for safe and reliable filtration processes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Reverse-steaming: Steam flows from the filtrate side (downstream) of the filter toward the feed side (upstream). Under high differential pressure, this can severely damage the membrane structure, including pleat-pack movement, cage imprinting and cartridge deformation.

Condensation vacuum: When steam condenses, its volume shrinks sharply; if the system is sealed, a vacuum develops. This vacuum creates a large differential pressure across the filter and can damage the membrane structure.

Analogy

Imagine filling a plastic bottle with hot water and capping it tightly right away. As the water cools, the steam inside the bottle condenses and shrinks, and outside atmospheric pressure crushes the bottle. Condensation vacuum affects a filter in a similar way: if the system is not properly vented or pressurized, the vacuum from condensation can "crush" the filter and cause irreversible damage.

Key Notes

In-line steam sterilization is the leading cause of filter damage: TR26 directly names in-line steam sterilization as "one of the main sources of potential damage." This is not a theoretical risk; it happens frequently in actual production. A sound SOP, operator training and continuous monitoring of pressure conditions are the three pillars of prevention.

The importance of drain position: The cartridge must be installed in an orientation that lets condensate drain naturally. Accumulated condensate obstructs steam penetration and creates cold spots, leading to incomplete sterilization. Standing water also produces a stronger vacuum effect on cooling.

Practical Application

In a CDMO, different clients' products may use cartridges and housings of different specifications. Whenever the cartridge model is changed or the steam sterilization procedure is modified, sterilization qualification must be repeated, including a temperature distribution study and differential pressure monitoring. It is advisable to establish a standard range of steam sterilization parameters during the process development (PD) phase and include it in the change control procedure, to reduce repeated validation work.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is reverse-steaming more damaging to the filter than forward-steaming? Explain from the standpoint of the structural design of the filter.
  2. Where would you install pressure sensors in a steam sterilization system? Why is it necessary to monitor both upstream and downstream pressure?
  3. How can condensation vacuum be prevented? What engineering controls are available?
5.8.3 Autoclaving Considerations

Original Text

While the goals of steam sterilization using an autoclave are similar to those of in-line steaming, the process of placing a filtration device into an autoclave chamber is distinct in several aspects. Similar to sterilizing any item in an autoclave, the preparation of the filter must be considered, including appropriate wetting if necessary and how to wrap it to safeguard the sterilized equipment from contamination when removed after the sterilization cycle. The wrapping material must strike a balance; it should be porous enough to allow the penetration of steam and air while effectively blocking any bacterial ingress. Autoclave cycles typically run a pre- and post-vacuum cycle to evacuate any residual air and to dry the load. These vacuum cycles can cause high differential pressure when the wrapping materials are affixed too tight on the inlet, outlet and vent connections. Sterilizing-grade filters are vulnerable to damage from excessive differential pressure across the membrane at elevated temperatures; therefore the preparation of the filter requires attention during the development phase.

In addition, the placement of the filter within the autoclave is of importance as it should not have any contact with other parts within the autoclave especially the autoclave walls as this could result in temperature gradients which can result in additional stress on the filter.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Autoclave wrapping: When a filtration device is wrapped and placed in an autoclave, the wrapping material must balance two conflicting needs: (1) porous enough to allow steam penetration and air removal, and (2) dense enough to prevent microorganisms from entering after sterilization.

Pre/post-vacuum cycles: An autoclave typically draws a vacuum before sterilization to remove residual air (ensuring steam penetration) and after sterilization to dry the load. These vacuum cycles create a differential pressure across the filter.

Analogy

Autoclaving a filter is like vacuum-packing food: if the bag is sealed too tightly, the food is crushed and deformed when the vacuum is drawn. Likewise, if the wrapping material is wound too tightly around the cartridge inlet and outlet, the autoclave vacuum cycles create a large differential pressure across the membrane and may crush it. An appropriate "air space" is essential.

Key Notes

Wrapping tightness is the key: This is an operating detail that looks simple but often goes wrong. Wrapping too tight leads to excessive differential pressure during vacuum cycles and filter damage. Wrapping too loose fails to maintain a microbial barrier after sterilization and leads to a sterility failure. An appropriate wrapping method must be developed and validated in the PD phase.

Avoid contact with the autoclave wall: The wall temperature may be uneven, and contact with the wall creates local temperature gradients and thermal stress on the filter. Use suitable racks or supports to keep the filter off the wall.

Practice Questions

  1. If you find that filters occasionally fail the integrity test after autoclaving, which areas would you investigate to find the cause?
  2. How do you select a suitable autoclave wrapping material? What characteristics must be considered?
5.8.4 Filter-Capsule Handling

Original Text

The filter-capsule handling can be compared with the filter-cartridge activities, except the filter capsule is a filter unit that does not need to be installed into a filter-housing; it is already encapsulated and just needs to be connected into the process stream. More detailed information on the handling of single-use capsule filters can be found in PDA TR 66.

Most filter capsules cannot be in-line steam-sterilized and either require to be autoclaved or are supplied presterilized using gamma or x-ray irradiation. The autoclaving cycle for filter capsules requires the same attention as the filter cartridge to prevent damage to the filtration system.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Capsule filter: The membrane is integrated in a polymer shell and no external metal housing is needed. It is ready to use once connected to the tubing, which greatly simplifies installation. Common in single-use systems (SUS).

Presterilized filter: Delivered by the supplier already sterilized by gamma or X-ray irradiation, so the user does not need to sterilize it. This simplifies operation but adds supply chain management requirements.

Analogy

A cartridge filter is like buying a lens that you must mount on the camera yourself (the housing), whereas a capsule filter is like a disposable camera: it comes assembled from the factory and is ready to use once connected. It is fast and convenient, but if something goes wrong it cannot be taken apart and repaired.

Key Notes

Most capsule filters cannot be steam-sterilized in place: Their polymer shells cannot withstand the high temperature and pressure of in-line steam. This limits the choice of sterilization method to autoclaving or presterilized products, and the limitation must be considered early when designing the process.

Cross-reference to PDA TR 66: TR26 specifically cites PDA TR 66 as detailed guidance on the handling of single-use capsule filters. In practice, the two technical reports should be used together.

Practice Questions

  1. Under what circumstances would you choose a presterilized capsule filter over a cartridge filter (sterilized by steam-in-place)? What are the advantages and disadvantages of each?
  2. What should be considered in supply chain management when using presterilized capsule filters? (Hint: consider sterilization expiry, storage conditions and the effect of gamma irradiation on the materials.)
5.9 Impact of Filter Sterilization

Original Text

As shown in Table 4.1-1🔍, the effect of the sterilization method and conditions on filter bacterial retention for both the membrane and the assembled filter unit should be a part of the filter manufacturer's qualification package. These studies are generally performed to demonstrate that stresses that occur during the sterilization process do not affect the ability of the filtration system to retain microbes. The work that the filter manufacturers perform demonstrates, that after sterilization stress, the filter performs as designed as long as the integrity test passes. Within the sterilization stress ranges studied, this allows nondestructive integrity-testing of the filters by the end user to demonstrate that sterilizing filters are functioning as designed. It should be noted that these sterilization-method qualification studies are different than those performed for product-specific bacterial retention validation studies, as outlined in Section 6.0. In the absence of any filtration surface modifications caused by the sterilization method, no additional product specific studies related to the filter sterilization conditions are generally required.

As long as the sterilization processes performed by the end user are within the ranges (e.g., temperature, time, ionizing radiation exposure) qualified by the filter manufacturer, performing an integrity test is an appropriate control strategy that connects the filter performance back to the microbial-retention testing performed during the filter qualifications.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Sterilization-method qualification: The filter manufacturer must demonstrate that, within its specified sterilization conditions (temperature, time, radiation dose, etc.), the filter retains its full bacterial retention capability after sterilization. This is part of the filter qualification in Table 4.1-1🔍.

Integrity test as a bridging tool: The manufacturer's qualification establishes the correlation "integrity test pass → bacterial retention capability intact." The user therefore only needs to perform a non-destructive integrity test to demonstrate indirectly that the filter's sterilizing function is intact. This is the core logic chain of the entire quality control system for sterilizing filtration.

Analogy

This is like the factory testing of a car seat belt. The seat belt manufacturer has already tested its strength under all kinds of extreme conditions (high temperature, low temperature, repeated pulling). As long as your conditions of use fall within the range the manufacturer tested, the "click" you hear each time you buckle up (= the integrity test) is enough to confirm that the seat belt will work; you do not need to run a crash test before every drive.

Key Notes

Key prerequisite: the user's sterilization conditions must fall within the range confirmed by the manufacturer. If your steam sterilization temperature or time exceeds the qualification range provided by the manufacturer, the integrity test is no longer an adequate control strategy. In that case, you may need to perform additional product-specific bacterial retention studies.

Sterilization-method qualification is not product-specific validation: TR26 distinguishes these two kinds of study. The former demonstrates that "sterilization does not damage the filter"; the latter demonstrates that "a specific product can be effectively filtered under specific conditions" (see Section 6.0). Both are required, but they answer different questions.

Practical Application

In a CDMO setting, the practical significance of this concept is that as long as your sterilization conditions are within the filter supplier's qualification range, you do not need to repeat bacterial retention studies for each client's different sterilization parameters. This greatly reduces validation workload and cost. But you must carefully document and compare your actual sterilization conditions against the supplier's qualification range; anything outside the range requires additional risk assessment and possibly supplementary studies.

Practice Questions

  1. In your own words, explain the logic chain of "integrity test as a bridging tool." From the manufacturer's qualification to the user's integrity test, what is each link in between?
  2. If your steam sterilization cycle occasionally shows a temperature overshoot of 2°C above the manufacturer's specified maximum, what actions do you need to take?
  3. Why does TR26 say that no additional product-specific study is needed "in the absence of filter surface modification caused by the sterilization method"? Under what circumstances might sterilization cause surface modification?

Section 5.0 Complete Section Summary

  • Design before validation: The core spirit of Section 5.0 is Stage 1, Process Design, of the FDA's three stages of process validation. Quality must be "designed in" rather than "tested in." As the final microbial control barrier, the rigor of the sterilizing filtration process design directly affects patient safety.
  • Comprehensive design space evaluation: Starting from product characteristics and filter compatibility (5.1.1), define the operating range (5.1.2), covering seven factors: temperature, volume, flow rate, differential pressure, filtration time, driving force and configuration (Table 5.1.2-1🔍).
  • Flow characteristics are central to process control: Differential pressure (5.2.1), temperature (5.2.2) and time (5.2.3) interact in affecting filtration performance. Understanding the relationships among these parameters is the foundation for correct filtration process design.
  • Throughput and capacity determine filter selection: Table 5.3-1🔍 summarizes the eight factors that affect throughput. Small-scale screening trials (5.4.1) combined with EFA-proportional scale-up are standard practice, but the non-linearity risks of scale-up (5.4.2) must be watched, especially the effect of shear stress history on protein products.
  • System integration requires a complete URS: Start from the URS (5.5) and clearly define all process parameters and system requirements. Series filtration configurations (5.5.1) must clearly distinguish the different roles and validation requirements of pre-filtration, redundant filtration and double filtration.
  • Pre-filtration bioburden monitoring provides a safety factor: Pre-filtration bioburden monitoring (5.6) is a general regulatory expectation, and the choice of sampling time and location must be based on product characteristics and process risk assessment.
  • Flushing strategy affects product recovery: Filter flushing (5.7) is not only a quality requirement for reducing leachables but also an economic opportunity to improve product recovery, especially for surfactant-containing protein products. The filter-flush curve is the scientific tool for determining the optimal flush volume.
  • Handling and installation details determine success or failure: Cartridge installation (5.8.1), in-line steam sterilization (5.8.2), autoclaving (5.8.3) and capsule filter handling (5.8.4) can each lead to filter damage or contamination. SOPs, training and monitoring are the three pillars of prevention.
  • The integrity test is the bridge linking qualification to routine operation: As long as the user's sterilization conditions are within the manufacturer's qualification range (5.9), a non-destructive integrity test can indirectly demonstrate the filter's sterilizing function. This is the core control strategy of the entire sterilizing filtration quality system.
  • The decision hierarchy always applies: sterile filtrate assurance → product quality/CQA → process robustness → commercial and efficiency considerations. When design decisions conflict, the higher-ranked principle always prevails.
6

Section 6: Validation

p39-p55

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) - Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p39 - p43

Learning Objectives for This Chapter (Complete Section 6.0)

  • Understand the purpose, timing, and scope of process-specific filter validation, and its role in the marketing authorization application
  • Understand how Quality by Design (QbD) principles apply to selecting worst-case test parameters for sterilizing filtration
  • Understand the design considerations for adsorption, extractables/leachables, and chemical compatibility validation tests
  • Understand the methodology, challenge conditions, and acceptance criteria of the bacterial retention study
  • Recognize that the product wet integrity test value is optional, and how it relates to bacterial retention
  • Learn to link filter validation decisions to the contamination control strategy (CCS) and to the overriding priority of sterile filtrate assurance
6.0 Product and Process-Specific Validation Testing for Sterilizing Filtration

Original Text

Process-specific filter validation studies, to provide assurance that the filter will perform as expected under worst-case processing conditions, are required prior to submission for marketing authorization. However, filter validation studies may be conducted earlier based on risk assessment. A risk assessment can be based on prior knowledge from how the product, process and filter interact, leveraging successful filter validations of similar molecules. This risk assessment can be performed to evaluate whether a product operates within the design space. Bacterial retention studies, with the final sterilizing-grade filter, are required when a sterile claim is made and the product is not terminally sterilized (29). This section describes factors to consider when performing process-specific filter validation studies.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Process-Specific Filter Validation: This is validation of one specific combination: "your product + your process + the filter you selected". The filter supplier provides generic qualification data, but the regulations expect the end user to confirm filter performance under the end user's own product conditions.

Design Space: The validated multidimensional combination of parameter ranges within which operation is considered to assure product quality. It is central to the QbD concept and includes the normal operating range (NOR) and the proven acceptable range (PAR).

Marketing Authorization: The filter validation report is part of the CMC submission package. Without completed validation, the product cannot obtain marketing authorization.

Analogy

Think of the filter supplier's qualification data as an employee's university diploma: it proves the person completed basic education and has general competence. Your company still needs a probationary-period assessment (process-specific validation) to confirm the person can perform in "your team, your project, and your work environment". The diploma is necessary, but it cannot replace the probation period.

Key Notes

Timing is critical: The regulatory requirement is that validation be completed before the marketing authorization application, but TR26 suggests it may be performed earlier based on a risk assessment. Particularly in expedited clinical development, obtaining process-specific data early can avoid late-stage surprises.

The value of prior knowledge: If your company has already successfully validated sterilizing filtration for similar molecules, that experience can serve as a basis for the risk assessment. This is especially advantageous for a CDMO: experience across many products is your competitive edge.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: A CDMO receives a client's accelerated development project (FDA Breakthrough Therapy Designation) and must begin preparing the commercial process as early as Phase II. Based on a risk assessment, the CDMO should start preliminary filter validation studies early rather than waiting until Phase III is complete. That way the validation report is ready by the time the BLA is submitted.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can the filter supplier's generic qualification data not replace the end user's process-specific validation? What does each of the two cover?
  2. Under what circumstances would you advise a client to perform filter validation early in clinical development? What are the risks and benefits of doing so?
6.1 Performance of Process-Specific Filter Validation

Original Text

The rationale for selection of a suitable filter for final sterilizing filtration should be documented in an end user's CCS. As described in Section 5.0, during PD, end users will review generic information available from their supplier (i.e., qualification data) to assess suitability of components for the intended process and identify any potential risks associated with compatibility, adsorption, and extractables as part of their initial risk assessment. Consideration should also be given if the final sterilizing filter will be part of a SUS, which could include tubing and bags, as this may impact the maximum processing parameters and potentially the design of the final process-specific filter validation studies. While it is recognized that process-specific filter validation studies must be performed prior to commercial manufacture or release of product, there is no specific guidance that states precisely when such studies should be performed in the development/validation lifecycle. Consequently, the decision when to perform filter validation is determined largely by the end user. This usually coincides when the drug product is in late phase development, as it is important that the proposed critical process parameters (CPPs) and full-scale manufacturing conditions are well understood to ensure the testing performed validates the intended filtration design space. If a specific risk is identified, it may be appropriate to conduct process-specific testing earlier during PD (e.g., if the drug is subject to expedited clinical development). Generation of early process-specific data can also be considered for the purposes of risk assessment and not the final filter validation.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

CCS (Contamination Control Strategy): This is a core requirement of EU GMP Annex 1 (2022). The rationale for filter selection must be documented in the CCS, explaining why this brand and model of filter was chosen and how it supports the overall sterility assurance strategy.

SUS (Single-Use System): When the sterilizing filter is integrated into a SUS (for example, an assembly with tubing and bags), validation becomes more complex, because the maximum operating parameters (temperature, pressure, time) of the SUS may be more restrictive than those of the filter alone.

Filtration Design Space: The multidimensional range of CPPs that defines acceptable conditions for the filtration operation. Validation must cover the boundaries of this design space (worst-case conditions).

Analogy

Think of the timing of filter validation as the relationship between building a house and furnishing it. You would not order custom furniture (final validation) before the floor plan is settled (CPPs not yet defined). But if you already roughly know the size of the living room (basic process parameters are known), you can browse the furniture market first (preliminary feasibility testing). Once the layout is final (late-phase development), place the order, that is, perform the final validation.

Key Notes

The importance of CCS documentation: During a regulatory inspection, the auditor will review your CCS to confirm that filter selection was based on a systematic risk assessment rather than an arbitrary decision. This means you need to document: (1) which alternatives were evaluated, (2) the conclusions of the risk assessment, and (3) the data sources supporting the selection.

The SUS integration pitfall: Many end users overlook the overall impact of the SUS assembly. For example, the way tubing is connected may change the filtration pressure, and the bag material may contribute additional extractables. The validation design must consider the "system", not just the "filter".

Decision Hierarchy

1. Sterile Filtrate Assurance
▼
2. Product Quality / CQAs
▼
3. Process Robustness / Commercial Efficiency

The priority of validation always follows this hierarchy: first ensure bacterial retention capability (sterility assurance), then confirm that product quality is not affected (CQAs), and only then consider process efficiency.

Practice Questions

  1. If your sterilizing filter is part of a SUS, what additional factors must be considered when designing the validation plan? List at least three.
  2. A CDMO serves multiple clients, and each product uses the same brand of sterilizing filter. Can one validation report cover all products? Why or why not?
  3. Why does TR26 recommend performing final validation only in late-phase development? What risks arise if it is done too early?
6.2 Purpose of Process-Specific Filter Validation Testing

Original Text

The purpose of performing process-specific validation testing for final sterilizing filtration is twofold:

  1. To assess any impact the filter may have on the quality of the final drug product.
  2. To assess any impact the drug product may have on the performance of the filter.

Table 6.2-1🔍 provides a summary of typical testing that is in scope for process-specific filter validation.

Table 6.2-1🔍 Test Types for Process-Specific Filter Validation

*Any impact of the filter on the product CQAs should be conducted by the end-user during PD. Refer to Table 4.1.1.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Core logic of the two-way impact assessment:

  • Effect of the filter on the product: Will the filter "eat" your active ingredient (adsorption)? Will it "release" things that should not be there (extractables/leachables)?
  • Effect of the product on the filter: Will your product "kill" the challenge organism so that the test cannot be conducted? Will it "corrode" the filter material and compromise integrity? Will it "clog" the filter so that filtration cannot be completed?

Bacterial Retention Study: The filter is challenged with B. diminuta (or an appropriate process isolate) at a minimum challenge of ≥ 1 × 107 CFU/cm2, under simulated worst-case conditions, to demonstrate the filter's retention capability.

Analogy

Imagine you are bringing a new water purification unit (the sterilizing filter) into a restaurant:

  • Effect of the unit on the water: Will the new unit change the taste of the water (adsorption)? Will the piping leach plasticizers (extractables)?
  • Effect of the water on the unit: Will your water source (possibly high in minerals or at an extreme pH) corrode the unit's piping (chemical compatibility)? Will it clog the filter cartridge (is bacterial retention capability affected)?

You need validation in both directions to ensure that "the unit does not harm the water, and the water does not harm the unit".

Key Notes

The product wet integrity test value is optional: The table explicitly marks this as optional; it is not a regulatory requirement. In practice, however, if your product affects standard integrity test results (for example, protein can lower the bubble point), establishing a product-wet test value can prevent false failures.

Extractables testing need not be repeated: If the supplier's documentation is sufficient (see Section 4.6), extractables testing does not need to be repeated by the end user. However, you must assess whether the supplier's test conditions cover your operating conditions.

Table Analysis

Table 6.2-1🔍 is the roadmap for all of Section 6.0. It divides the validation tests into two broad categories, and each test under each category is explained in detail in the subsequent subsections 6.4-6.10. When studying, use this table as a "map" to check at any time which broad direction the subsection you are reading belongs to.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does Table 6.2-1🔍 divide validation tests into two directions, "effect of the filter on the product" and "effect of the product on the filter"? What risks might be missed if only one direction were assessed?
  2. Under what circumstances would you advise a client to establish a Product Wet Integrity Test Value, even though it is not a regulatory requirement?
  3. If the supplier's extractables data were generated at a temperature lower than your actual process temperature, can you accept the data without retesting? Why or why not?
6.3 Selection of Worst-Case Test Parameters Following Quality-by-Design Principles

Original Text

The principles of quality by design (QbD) should be applied when considering the worst-case parameters and conditions in which the sterilizing-grade filter should be validated. This includes a rationale for selection of worst-case parameters that must be based on a thorough understanding of the intended design space, including the normal operating range (NOR) and proven acceptable range (PAR) (8, 31). While it is expected that an end user will apply a safety factor to validate the use of the final sterilizing-grade filter in the given application, this should represent reasonable control of a process (based on manufacturing control limits) and defines the PAR for use of the sterilizing-grade filter in the application as outlined in Section 5.0.

A summary of worst-case parameters is included under the description of factors to consider for each filter validation test type.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Quality by Design (QbD): The core philosophy of ICH Q8: not "testing quality" but "designing quality". Applied to filter validation, it means you must first understand your process parameter space, and then deliberately select worst-case conditions for validation.

NOR (Normal Operating Range): The range over which a parameter actually varies during routine manufacturing. For example, the filtration temperature is typically 20-25°C.

PAR (Proven Acceptable Range): The parameter range that has been demonstrated to give acceptable product quality, usually wider than the NOR. For example, a validated temperature range of 15-30°C.

Safety Factor: An additional margin beyond the PAR, to ensure the filter still performs properly even under extreme excursions. TR26 emphasizes, however, that the safety factor must be "reasonable" and cannot be expanded without limit.

Analogy

Imagine you are designing a bridge:

  • NOR = everyday traffic (for example, 5,000 vehicles per day)
  • PAR = the maximum load established by stress testing (for example, 8,000 vehicles/day)
  • Safety Factor = an additional safety margin added at the design stage (for example, a design load of 10,000 vehicles/day)

But you would not design a bridge to carry one million vehicles; that is an unreasonable safety factor, wasteful and impractical. The same applies to the safety factor in filter validation: it must reasonably reflect the process control capability.

Key Notes

Worst case is not the same as an extreme case: This is a concept many people confuse. Worst case is based on the boundary of your design space plus a reasonable safety factor, not an arbitrary extreme condition. If your process has never operated at 50°C, you should not validate at 50°C; that is not a worst case, it is an irrelevant case.

The need to document the rationale: For every worst-case parameter selected, you must provide a written rationale explaining why that value represents the worst case. The auditor will ask, "How do you know this is the worst case?" Your answer must trace back to the definition of the design space.

Formula and Calculation

Design space hierarchy:

  Set Point
     ↕
  NOR (Normal Operating Range)
     ↕
  PAR (Proven Acceptable Range)
     ↕
  Worst-Case Test Condition = PAR + Safety Factor
     ↕
  Design Space Boundary

Example (filtration temperature):
  Set Point = 22°C
  NOR = 20-25°C
  PAR = 15-30°C
  Worst-Case = 10-35°C (PAR ± 5°C safety factor)

Practical Application

CDMO practical advice: When writing the validation protocol, prepare a "Parameter Justification Table": the left column lists each validation parameter, the middle column lists the selected worst-case value, and the right column gives the rationale (linked to NOR/PAR data). This lets you demonstrate your QbD thinking at a glance during a regulatory inspection.

Practice Questions

  1. What is the relationship among the NOR, the PAR, and the worst-case test condition? Illustrate with a specific process parameter (such as filtration differential pressure).
  2. Why does TR26 emphasize that the safety factor must be "reasonable"? What problems can an excessively conservative safety factor cause?
  3. If the CPPs are changed during process development (for example, the maximum filtration time is increased), what is the impact on a filter validation that has already been completed?
6.4 Adsorption

Original Text

While adsorption studies are typically performed during PD, as described in Section 5.0, some end users elect to have this performed as part of the filter validation package. Considerations for worst-case test conditions to assess potential adsorption are shown in Table 6.4-1🔍, but the parameters selected for testing should be based on risk assessment.

Table 6.4-1🔍 Considerations for Worst-Case Test Parameters to Assess Potential Adsorption

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Adsorption: The phenomenon in which product components (such as proteins, preservatives, surfactants, or the API) adhere to the filter surface. This is not "filtration"; the filter is "eating" your product. If adsorption is significant, the concentration of the active ingredient in the final product may fall below specification.

Key distinction, adsorption vs. retention:

  • Retention: The designed function of the filter; blocking bacteria = good
  • Adsorption: A side effect of the filter; binding the product = bad (you must assess whether it is within acceptable limits)

Analogy

Imagine filtering a homemade protein drink through a coffee filter:

  • The filter paper's function is to block the coffee grounds (= bacterial retention)
  • But the paper may also adsorb some protein and flavor components (= adsorption effect)
