  • The first cup filtered may taste weaker (because the paper is not yet saturated); only the later cups return to normal strength

In the same way, you need to confirm that the effect of filter adsorption on product quality is within acceptable limits.

Key Notes

Contact time has worst cases in two directions: Table 6.4-1🔍 lists both Shortest and Longest, because:

  • Shortest contact time: The filter is not yet saturated, so the proportion of product adsorbed from the early portion is highest
  • Longest contact time (static soak): Prolonged soaking may allow adsorption to reach a new equilibrium

Both scenarios need to be evaluated.

Temperature has an inverse relationship with adsorption: Most adsorption processes are more pronounced at low temperature. Therefore, if your product must be filtered under refrigeration (for example, 2-8°C), the adsorption risk may be higher.

Lower concentration is more hazardous: Counterintuitively, when the product concentration is low, the ratio of filter surface area to product amount is higher, so the proportional effect of adsorption is more pronounced.

Formula and Calculation

Estimating the adsorption rate:

Adsorption rate (%) = (C_before - C_after) / C_before × 100%

Where:
  C_before = product concentration before filtration
  C_after  = product concentration after filtration

Key judgments:
  - Adsorption rate < allowable range in the specification → acceptable
  - Collect samples at multiple time points (for example: first 10%, middle, and final portions)
  - Adsorption is usually highest in the early portion and gradually decreases as the filter becomes saturated

Practical Application

High-value biologic scenario: For a monoclonal antibody drug product at 10 mg/mL, if filter adsorption lowers the concentration of the early filtrate by 5% (0.5 mg/mL), the final product may fail the assay specification (typically 95-105%). Solutions include: (1) using pre-wetting to reduce adsorption, (2) discarding the initial filtrate, and (3) selecting a filter membrane material with lower adsorption. Each option has cost and process implications that must be weighed.

Practice Questions

  1. Why might a low flow rate increase adsorption? How does this relate to contact time with the filter surface?
  2. If your product contains a surfactant, why is adsorption testing especially important? What effect could surfactant adsorption have on product efficacy?
  3. In Table 6.4-1🔍, the worst case for pH is "Test Extremes". Explain why extreme pH values may affect adsorption behavior, and how this relates to the surface charge of the filter.
6.5 Extractables and Leachables

Original Text

During filter selection, extractables (and potentially leachables, based on a risk assessment of the extractables data) should be considered during early-PD. This is typically assessed based on the availability of supplier data using standardized conditions (e.g., model solvents under specified test conditions) (22, 33, 34). As defined in Section 4.6, an extractable is any chemical component that is removed from a material by the application of an artificial or exaggerated condition (e.g., solvent, temperature, time). A leachable is typically a chemical component that migrates from a contact surface into a drug product or process fluid during storage or normal-use conditions. However, on occasion, interaction with product components may produce leachables not seen as extractables (20).

Extractables testing may be repeated as part of the final filter validation. After selection of a suitable extraction solution(s) (model solvent, surrogate or combination of solutions), extraction studies should be designed to encompass the worst-case process conditions with regard to key variables, such as temperature, time, pH, and pretreatment steps (e.g., flushing, sterilization) (see Table 6.5-1🔍). Extraction studies are performed with the filter device using worst-case conditions of contact time and temperature with filters that have been sterilized by autoclaving or irradiation. Extraction studies may be conducted using methods such as static soak or recirculation/reciprocation/agitation. For static soak, the filter is submerged in the extraction solution at a given temperature for a defined amount of time. Extractables may also be generated by recirculating or reciprocating the extraction solution through the filter for a given amount of time under a defined temperature. The extract is collected and tested for the presence of filter extractables.

Table 6.5-1🔍 Worst-Case Parameters for Extractables/Leachables Testing Selection Factors

Once the filter extracts are obtained, a quantitative and qualitative assessment of the filter-derived materials are determined through selection of appropriate analytical methods. Additional information regarding these methods can be found in BioPhorum's Disposables: Extractables Testing of Polymeric Single-Use Components Used in Biopharmaceutical Manufacturing (30). In addition to determining identities and quantities of filter extractables, patient safety is evaluated through a toxicological assessment.

Based on a qualitative (e.g., extractable chemical name with CAS number) and quantitative assessment of extractables to evaluate the patient exposure to potential leachables, the end user may also elect to perform a leachables study. This involves using the actual process fluid with the same filter type that will be used in production. Due to the low levels of potential leachables, extractables testing using a model solvent approach is usually a prerequisite to having a leachables study performed. Circumstances, such as drug product interference with analytical methods or the prohibitive cost of the drug product, may necessitate the use of a surrogate solution. Surrogates should closely resemble the product to be filtered. Another option is to use several solutions to bracket the properties of pH, ionic strength, or level of organic components of the actual fluid. When a surrogate or bracketing approach is used, a clear rationale for the selection of solutions and extraction conditions should be documented.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Extractables: Chemical substances "extracted" from the filter materials under deliberately exaggerated conditions (high temperature, strong solvents, long duration). They represent the "maximum possible" total amount of released substances.

Leachables: Chemical substances that "leach" from the filter materials into the product under normal conditions of use. They represent the amount of substances that "actually" enter the product.

Relationship between the two: Extractables are generally a superset of leachables; leachables are generally a subset of extractables. However, TR26 specifically cautions that "occasionally, interactions between product components and the filter materials may produce leachables not seen in extractables testing". This means extractables testing cannot predict all leachables with 100% certainty.

Model Solvent Approach: Extractables testing is performed with standardized solvents (rather than the actual product). Commonly used solvents include water, acidic solutions, basic solutions, and organic solvents, chosen to simulate different product characteristics.

Analogy

Extractables vs. leachables is like brewing tea:

  • Extractables = soaking the tea leaves in 100°C water for 24 hours, extracting "everything that could possibly" be released from the leaves (including bitter substances you would never drink when brewing tea normally)
  • Leachables = brewing tea the normal way (80°C, 3 minutes), which gives what you would "actually" drink

Perform the extractables study first (to understand the worst case), then assess whether a leachables study is needed (to understand the actual case). If even the substances extracted under extreme conditions are within safe limits, the risk under normal conditions of use is lower still.

Key Notes

The sterilization method affects extractables: Table 6.5-1🔍 specifically lists filter sterilization as an influencing factor. Higher autoclave temperature or number of cycles, or a higher gamma irradiation dose, may accelerate degradation of the filter materials and generate more extractables. Validation must therefore use worst-case sterilization treatment.

Choice of extraction method: Static soak and recirculation each suit different scenarios. Static soak simulates long-term storage; recirculation simulates the actual filtration operation. The two methods may yield different extractables profiles.

Toxicological assessment is a required step: Knowing which extractables are present is not enough; a toxicology expert must also assess the patient safety risk of these substances at the expected dose. This usually involves calculating the AET (Analytical Evaluation Threshold).

Table Analysis

The logic of Table 6.5-1🔍: The worst case for each parameter is set in the direction that increases the likelihood of extraction: stronger solvent, more extreme pH, higher temperature, longer time, harsher sterilization. This follows the basic principle of E&L studies: evaluate under worst-case conditions first, and if they pass, normal conditions are safer still.

Surrogate Solution and Bracketing Approach

Surrogate Solution: A surrogate solution may be used when the actual drug product cannot be used for the following reasons:

  • Analytical interference: Drug product components interfere with the analytical method, so trace leachables cannot be detected
  • Excessive cost: The cost of leachables testing with the actual drug product is unreasonable

Bracketing Approach: Multiple solutions are used to "bracket" the characteristics of the actual product (pH, ionic strength, organic content), ensuring the testing covers all relevant product characteristics.

Documentation requirement: Whether a surrogate or bracketing approach is used, the rationale for the selection and for the chosen conditions must be documented.

Practical Application

E&L strategy for a CDMO: As a CDMO, you may serve multiple clients using the same filter model. An effective strategy is:

  1. Obtain extractables data under standardized conditions from the filter supplier (usually free or included in the technical documentation)
  2. Assess whether the supplier data cover your operating conditions (temperature, pH, contact time, sterilization method)
  3. If the supplier data are sufficient, repeat extractables testing can be avoided (see Section 4.6), saving cost and time
  4. Perform leachables testing only when the risk assessment for a specific product shows it is needed

This tiered strategy can substantially optimize validation time and cost while still ensuring safety.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR26 point out that "leachables sometimes include substances not detected in extractables testing"? What does this imply for E&L study strategy?
  2. Under what circumstances would you choose the static soak method? Under what circumstances would you choose recirculation? Which use scenario does each simulate?
  3. Your client manufactures a high-value gene therapy product (each batch worth $500,000), and leachables testing would require the actual drug product. Would you recommend a surrogate solution? If so, how would you design the surrogate approach and provide a reasonable justification?
PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p44 - p48
6.6 Bacterial Retention Study

Original Text

Bacterial retention studies are used to validate a filter to perform the sterilizing function (i.e., render the filtrate sterile) by showing complete microbial removal from the challenge fluid, product, or product family, using a representative challenge microorganism (1, 35, 36). Two primary elements are taken into consideration for bacterial retention study validation:

  1. Classification of the filter membranes as sterilizing-grade by means of bacterial retention studies, using standardized test methods (6). This is the responsibility of the filter supplier as part of the filter qualification to demonstrate that the filter retains bacteria using standardized methodology (i.e., proof that the filter retains B. diminuta when challenged at a level of ≥ 1 x 107 CFU/cm2 and can be classified as a sterilizing-grade filter in accordance with the principles of ASTM F838).
  2. Demonstration by the filter user or designated test facility (e.g., filter manufacturer or contract laboratory) of complete retention of a microbial challenge from each product or product family in the actual process conditions. Scientific rationale for each product group should be developed and may be reviewed with the appropriate regulatory agency before validation testing. It is the responsibility of the end user to confirm that the selected sterilizing-grade filter performs as expected for process-specific applications (i.e., under simulated worst-case processing conditions with the intended drug product).
"These two filter-testing concepts are not interchangeable and should be validated independently."

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Bacterial Retention Study: This is the most central test in validating sterilizing filtration. Its purpose is to challenge the filter membrane with live microorganisms and show that the membrane truly retains the bacteria completely, so that the filtrate is sterile.

TR26 clearly separates bacterial retention validation into two independent levels:

  • Level 1: Qualification of the membrane by the filter supplier - Using the ASTM F838 standard method, B. diminuta is applied at a challenge level of ≥ 107 CFU/cm2 to show that the membrane can be classified as "sterilizing-grade". This is like the membrane's "graduation certificate".
  • Level 2: Process validation by the user - For the actual drug product or product family, testing is performed under simulated worst-case conditions to confirm that the filter also removes bacteria effectively in your specific process. This is like an "on-the-job internship": it must be shown that the filter can do the job under the conditions in your own facility.

These two levels cannot substitute for each other and must be validated independently.

Analogy

Imagine hiring a security guard (the filter membrane). Level 1 is like his security licensing exam (ASTM F838): under standard exam conditions, he demonstrates basic ability to stop intruders. Level 2 is a trial period at your own company: in the real environment of your facility (your specific product, your specific conditions), you verify that he can really stop everything that should not get in. Passing the licensing exam does not guarantee he suits your facility; conversely, passing the trial period cannot replace the formal license. You need both.

Key Notes

Responsibilities are very clear:

  • Level 1 (standard ASTM F838 test) = responsibility of the filter supplier
  • Level 2 (process-specific validation) = responsibility of the end user

For a CDMO, your clients (the pharmaceutical MAH) will also ask you for the Level 2 validation data. If your process conditions change (for example, a change in drug product formulation, or in filtration temperature or pressure), process-specific bacterial retention validation may need to be repeated.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can't the supplier's ASTM F838 test results directly replace the user's process-specific validation? Give at least two factors that could lead to different results.
  2. If your CDMO manufactures three different injectable formulations, how many process-specific bacterial retention validations do you need? Can the "product family" concept be used to simplify this?
6.6.1 Factors Influencing Bacterial Retention

Original Text

Factors potentially affecting bacterial retention include, but are not limited to (2, 3):

  • Filter type (e.g., asymmetric, isometric)
    • Base polymer, surface modification chemistry, pore size distribution, and thickness
  • Fluid components
    • Formulation, surfactants, and additives
  • Fluid properties
    • pH, viscosity, osmolarity, ionic strength, and surface tension
  • Process conditions
    • Filter reuse, temperature, pressure differential, flow rate, filtration time, total filter/product contact time, and volume throughput.* The potential impact of intermittency should be considered.
  • Potential bioburden in the product (quantitative and qualitative)

*Note: As sterilization of the filter is part of the supplier qualification performed during development, exact simulation of the end user's sterilization method (e.g., irradiation, autoclave, SIP temperature, number of cycles) is not generally required for process-specific bacterial retention study.

In addition to these factors, process bioburden should be evaluated in order to establish B. diminuta as a relevant organism. Evaluation should be based on bioburden identification and a risk assessment to ensure the absence of potentially more penetrative organisms in the prefiltration bioburden (see Section 6.6.2). Consideration should be given to potential product formulations or process conditions affecting cell size or other physiological or morphological microorganism attributes that might allow organism passage.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The factors influencing bacterial retention can be divided into four main categories:

  • The membrane itself: Asymmetric or isotropic structure, base polymer, surface modification chemistry, pore size distribution, and thickness. These determine the membrane's "basic retention capability".
  • Fluid properties: pH, viscosity, osmolality, ionic strength, surface tension, and so on. These factors may affect the size and shape of microorganisms, and even the adsorptive capacity of the membrane surface.
  • Process conditions: Differential pressure, flow rate, filtration time, total contact time, volumetric throughput, and intermittency (that is, a stop-and-go filtration mode).
  • Bioburden: The types and numbers of microorganisms that may be present in the product.

Intermittency deserves particular attention - if filtration repeatedly stops and starts, microorganisms may have more time to interact with the membrane while stationary, and may even be pushed deeper into the pores.

Analogy

Imagine a sponge maze (the membrane structure) and you want to keep small balls (bacteria) out. Whether you succeed depends on how dense the maze is (membrane type), what liquid the balls are carried in (fluid properties; for example, an oily liquid may let balls slide through more easily), how hard and how long you push (process conditions), and how many balls rush in at once (bioburden). If the pushing force fluctuates (intermittent operation), some balls may be squeezed deep into the maze when you are not looking.

Key Notes

About the filter sterilization method: The source text specifically notes that the method used to sterilize the filter (such as irradiation, autoclaving, or SIP) falls within the supplier's qualification during development, and the user's process-specific bacterial retention study generally does not need to simulate the sterilization conditions precisely. This is a common misunderstanding: many people assume process validation must simulate every condition, but the sterilization part can be left to the supplier.

Bioburden risk assessment: Do not look at numbers alone. A qualitative assessment is also needed to confirm whether any small microorganisms could penetrate more easily than B. diminuta. If so, B. diminuta may not be a representative worst-case organism (see Section 6.6.2).

Practical Application

CDMO practical scenario: Suppose your facility manufactures an ophthalmic product containing a surfactant. Surfactants lower the surface tension of the liquid and may affect the membrane's adsorptive sequestration capability. In this case, your process-specific validation cannot use just pure water or a simple buffer; the challenge test must use the actual formulation (or a surrogate) containing the surfactant in order to reflect your real process risk.

Practice Questions

  1. If your filtration process is intermittent (for example, a 30-minute stoppage in the middle of each batch), how does this affect the design of the bacterial retention validation?
  2. Why might fluid pH and ionic strength affect bacterial retention? Hint: think about the adsorptive sequestration mechanism.
  3. The source text says the sterilization method "generally does not need" to be simulated in user validation. Under what circumstances might there be an exception?
6.6.2 Challenge Organism Selection Criteria

Original Text

Historically, Pseudomonas diminuta, reclassified as B. diminuta, has been the microorganism of choice for bacterial retention studies. Although a product may be bactericidal to B. diminuta under normal processing conditions, other microorganisms may survive under the same conditions. Another bacterial retention study method for bactericidal products is the use of "indigenous bioburden." Indigenous bioburden consists of bacterial isolates from the manufacturing environment or the product formulation that have demonstrated the ability to survive within the product formulation under actual production processing conditions.

In process bacteria should be propagated or equilibrated in the product in order to ensure that their morphological and physiological characteristics are representative of process isolates. The smallest, or worst-case, indigenous organism should be used based on a risk assessment against use of B. diminuta as the challenge organism. For additional discussion of preparation and use of challenge organisms, see Section 6.6.3.

If diminutive microorganisms are known to exist in the product, they or an appropriate model organism, may be used to challenge test filters. Typically, these challenges are conducted in addition to B. diminuta testing, and it is strongly recommended that appropriate measures be taken to remove these organisms as a potential contaminant.

If other bacteria are used, they should be small enough to challenge the retentivity of the sterilizing-grade filter and should simulate the smallest microorganism found in production (4). A risk assessment should determine the likelihood of any prefiltration bacteria potentially being able to penetrate a 0.2 μm sterilizing-grade filter (37).

The size of the challenge microorganism should be confirmed by demonstrating passage through a 0.45 μm-rated membrane as a positive control for each challenge performed. This confirms the challenge organism is appropriately sized and monodispersed. B. diminuta grown under standard culture conditions penetrates 0.45 μm-rated membranes in small numbers at high challenge levels, typically ≥ 107 CFU/cm2 (see Section 6.6.3). In some cases, B. diminuta may not be a representative worst-case model. Documented rationale should be provided if a different challenge organism is chosen.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

B. diminuta is the "standard opponent" in bacterial retention testing, but it is not always the worst case. The key logic for selecting a challenge organism is as follows:

  • Bactericidal product: If the product itself kills B. diminuta, but other microorganisms that are not affected by the product may be present in the process, then indigenous bioburden must be used - that is, organisms isolated from your manufacturing environment or product formulation and confirmed to survive in the product.
  • Diminutive microorganisms: If microorganisms smaller than B. diminuta are known to be present in the product, these small organisms (or a suitable model organism) must additionally be used to challenge the membrane.
  • Positive control: Every challenge test must include a 0.45 µm membrane as a positive control - the challenge organism must be able to pass through a 0.45 µm membrane, demonstrating that the cells are small enough and monodispersed, with no clumping.

Analogy

This is like the logic of setting an exam. B. diminuta is the standard exam question (the road-test route in a driving test). But if the "candidates" your facility actually meets are trickier (smaller than the standard question and better at squeezing through gaps), you cannot rely only on the standard question for your evaluation. You need to find the organism in your environment that is best at "slipping through holes" and use it for the challenge. The 0.45 µm positive control is like checking that the difficulty of the question is appropriate: it makes sure your challenge organism really is "small enough" and is not being "retained" merely because the cells have clumped into big balls.

Key Notes

Necessity of the positive control: The 0.45 µm positive control is a mandatory item in every challenge test, not an optional one. It confirms that:

  • The size of the challenge organism really falls within the 0.2-0.45 µm range (it passes a 0.45 µm membrane but is retained by a 0.2 µm membrane)
  • The cells are monodispersed and have not aggregated into clumps

If the cells clump, then even if individual cells are small, the "equivalent particle size" of the clumps becomes larger. Retention by a 0.2 µm membrane would then be due to clumping rather than the true retention capability of the membrane, and such a result is unreliable.

If a challenge organism other than B. diminuta is selected, a documented scientific justification must be provided.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Suppose your facility manufactures an injectable containing phenol as a preservative. Environmental monitoring shows that a small Gram-negative organism resistant to phenol occasionally appears in the process. In this case:

  1. You still use B. diminuta for the standard challenge test
  2. But you must additionally challenge with this environmental isolate (indigenous bioburden)
  3. This isolate must be cultured/equilibrated in the product before use, to ensure that its morphology is representative of actual process conditions
  4. A 0.45 µm positive control is required in every test

Practice Questions

  1. Why does bacterial retention validation remain important even if the product kills B. diminuta? What alternative strategies can be used?
  2. What does a failed 0.45 µm positive control test (the organisms cannot pass through the 0.45 µm membrane) mean? How should you respond?
  3. If your environmental monitoring has never found a microorganism smaller than B. diminuta, do you still need to consider using indigenous bioburden? Why?
6.6.3 Culture Maintenance and Challenge Preparation

Original Text

B. diminuta ATCC® 19146TM can be obtained in lyophilized form from bacterial culture collections such as the American Type Culture Collection (ATCC). After reconstituting the microorganism according to the manufacturer's instructions, stocks can be maintained (either refrigerated or frozen) on appropriate media per standard microbiological practice. Maintenance conditions for process isolates to be used for challenge studies should be established.

Two standard techniques have been recognized as suitable for preparation and maintenance of B. diminuta for bacterial retention studies. These are the SLB and the frozen cell paste (FCP) methods. Both methods have been found to be effective in producing suitable suspensions of B. diminuta of approximately 0.3 – 0.4 μm in diameter by 0.6 – 1.0 μm in length.

Alternate media and culture methods may be equally valid to prepare B. diminuta provided they produce single, monodispersed cells suitably sized to penetrate 0.45 μm-rated membrane filters. Alternate culture methods should be validated. Bacterial retention study stock cultures can be screened for aggregation by optical microscopy. In cases where aggregation is observed, immersion of the stock culture in an ultrasonic cleaning bath filled with cold water for a qualified duration is one means of dispersing the aggregate. The cavitation action of the bath is effective in disaggregating bacterial cells without the loss of viability. This effect should be confirmed by optical microscopy, viable count, and recovery downstream of the 0.45 μm-rated size control filter (38).

The bacterial challenge concentration should provide a uniform challenge over the duration in which viability of B. diminuta is assured to yield a final challenge level of at least 107 CFU/cm2 of filter surface area. Operational parameters (e.g., flow rate, time, and pressure) should be taken into consideration when calculating the bacterial retention concentration.

A bacterial challenge level of ≥ 1 x 107 CFU/cm2 is, therefore, the requirement for a sterilizing-grade filter (historically a filter rated at 0.2 μm). This level was derived from Bowman's observations that B. diminuta could penetrate a 0.45 μm-rated membrane at > 104–106 CFU/cm2 bacterial challenge level, and her suggestion for qualifying 0.2 μm-rated membranes as "sterilizing-grade" with B. diminuta at 107 CFU/cm2 to assure minimally sufficient sensitivity to detect oversized pores (39). Bacterial challenge concentration (CFU/mL) should not be confused with bacterial challenge level (CFU/cm2).

The viability and titer of the B. diminuta suspension should be confirmed using a suitable recovery medium such as soybean-casein digest agar (also known as tryptic soy agar), Mueller Hinton Agar, or other qualified growth medium. When performing filter challenges, the viable titer of the bacterial retention suspension should be determined immediately prior to and after the challenge; the bacterial titer should be determined using an accepted microbiological testing method. For situations when this is not possible (e.g., extremely short-duration bacterial retention studies), a rationale should be provided to justify the use of either a pre- or post-input viability sample. The same culture medium should also be used to determine any recovery of B. diminuta downstream of the test and control filters.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

B. diminuta ATCC 19146 - This is the globally recognized source of the standard strain. Key knowledge on culture and preparation:

  • Two standard preparation methods: the SLB method and the Frozen Cell Paste (FCP) method. Both produce cells of appropriate size, approximately 0.3-0.4 µm (diameter) × 0.6-1.0 µm (length).
  • Monodispersed: The cells must be separate individuals and must not clump (aggregation). Aggregated cells make the test results unreliable - they are in fact "falsely retained".
  • Ultrasonic dispersion: If aggregation is found, an ultrasonic cleaning bath (cold water) can be used to break up the clumps, taking care not to damage the viability of the cells. The effect of the treatment must be confirmed in three ways: optical microscopy, viable count, and the 0.45 µm membrane pass-through rate.

Analogy

Culturing the challenge organism is like preparing the "exam material". You need to ensure that every ball (cell) is the same size (0.3-0.4 µm) and rolls independently (monodispersed), not stuck together like a rice ball (aggregation) - otherwise it is like testing the holes of a window screen with basketballs instead of ping-pong balls, and the test result is meaningless. Ultrasonic dispersion is like shaking apart rice grains that are stuck together by vibration, but you must be careful not to shake them to pieces (maintain viability).

Formula

Challenge Concentration vs. Challenge Level - be sure to distinguish them!

Challenge Concentration = CFU/mL
  → colony-forming units per milliliter of the bacterial suspension

Challenge Level = CFU/cm²
  → total number of organisms applied per square centimeter of membrane area

Conversion between the two:
  Challenge Level = (Challenge Concentration × Total Volume) / Filter Area

Example:
  If filter area = 13.4 cm² (47 mm disc)
  Minimum required challenge level = 1 × 10⁷ CFU/cm²
  Total challenge organisms = 1 × 10⁷ × 13.4 = 1.34 × 10⁸ CFU
  If challenge suspension volume = 1,000 mL
  Minimum required concentration = 1.34 × 10⁸ / 1,000 = 1.34 × 10⁵ CFU/mL

Key Notes

Historical origin of 107 CFU/cm2: This value comes from the research findings of Dr. Bowman - B. diminuta can penetrate a 0.45 µm membrane at challenge levels of 104-106 CFU/cm2. She therefore recommended 107 CFU/cm2 as the qualification standard for 0.2 µm "sterilizing-grade" membranes, to ensure sufficient sensitivity to detect oversized pores.

Timing of viability confirmation: The viable titer of the challenge suspension must be confirmed both before the start and after the end of the challenge test. If this cannot be done for an extremely short test, a scientific justification must be provided for why using only one of the time points is reasonable.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must Bacterial Challenge Concentration (CFU/mL) and Bacterial Challenge Level (CFU/cm2) not be confused? What consequences would follow from confusing them?
  2. If you use an alternative culture method to prepare B. diminuta, which parameters must be validated to demonstrate its suitability?
  3. Why does the viable titer need to be determined both before "and" after the challenge? If only one is measured, which time point is more important, and why?
6.6.4 Viability/Filter Flush Qualification Testing Procedure and Protocol Development

Original Text

Prior to completing the bacterial retention study, the test organism's viability should be verified by direct inoculation into the carrier fluid (ideally, product or surrogate fluid, if justified) in order to determine the appropriate challenge test methodology (see Figure 6.9-1). Scientific justification should be provided if viability testing is not performed before finalizing the bacterial retention study design (e.g., nonaqueous process fluids). The microorganism should be grown in the same manner as that used for challenge-testing to preserve its morphological and physiological characteristics. The test exposure time chosen for the viability studies should equal or exceed the maximum filter/product contact time, unless viability of the test organism is demonstrated to decline by ≥ 1 log before the actual process filtration time is reached.

Note: It is not necessary to determine the exact point in time at which viability of the test organism declines by ≥1 log; however, the viability test time should be sufficient to allow meaningful measurements to be taken during the bacterial retention study.

The organism should be added to the process fluid (or surrogate solution) such that the inoculation volume does not cause significant dilution of the drug product and viability should be monitored by taking appropriate samples over the intended process duration to determine cell concentration in accordance with standard methods. A control (e.g., water, buffer) should be spiked at the same ratio and viability of the organism monitored over the same duration. If the temperature may be inhibitory to the viability of the challenge organism (typically, over 40°C for B. diminuta), consideration should be given to assessing viability at ambient temperature so the bacterial retention study could be conducted by inoculating the organism directly into the process fluid (40). In addition, for broad processing temperature ranges, consideration may be given to the impact of the extremes of the temperature range on viability of the challenge organism. If there is less than an approximately 1 log difference between the test and control over at least 30 minutes, direct inoculation of the organism into the process fluid may be an option for the bacterial retention study. Additional consideration should be given for situations where the viability of the challenge organism in the process fluid is reduced by close to 1 log at the desired time point. For such cases, it may be necessary to reduce the total exposure time of the challenge organism in the process fluid to reduce the likelihood of die-off, which could prevent the minimum challenge level being met during the bacterial retention study. A decision should be made between ensuring the minimum challenge level of ≥ 1 x 107 CFU/cm2 is met and ensuring the bacterial retention study duration is sufficiently long to allow adequate measurements of differential pressure, flow rate, volume throughput and temperature.

Filter flush schemes should also be considered prior to performing a process-specific bacterial retention study. If the drug product is bactericidal to the challenge organism, then adequate removal/neutralization of bactericidal effects resulting from residual process fluid should be ensured prior to introduction of the bacteria to the test system. If the process fluid is nonbactericidal and used as the challenge vehicle, the lack of potential inhibitory effects to the challenge organism should be ensured during the incubation period.

After the test organism's viability within the product has been established, the challenge methodology and protocol should be developed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Viability testing is a preliminary study that must be completed before the formal bacterial retention study. It answers one key question: how long can the challenge organism survive in your product?

  • Direct inoculation: The challenge organism is inoculated directly into the product (or a scientifically justified surrogate)
  • 1 log reduction threshold: If the survival of the organism in the product differs from that in the control (water or buffer) by less than about 1 log (over at least 30 minutes), the direct inoculation method can be used for the challenge
  • Filter flush: If the product is bactericidal, residual bactericidal product components must be flushed out before the challenge organism is introduced; otherwise the organisms are killed before they even reach the membrane

Analogy

This is like "getting used to the venue" before a physical fitness test. You cannot throw a candidate (B. diminuta) straight into a scorching desert (a bactericidal product) for the exam - he may collapse from heatstroke (die-off) before the exam even starts. So you first run a "venue adaptation test" (viability testing): put the candidate in the exam environment and observe. If he is still full of energy throughout the whole exam period (< 1 log reduction), the venue is fine. If he quickly gives out, you need to adjust the exam strategy - either shorten the exam time, or first clear the "poison" from the venue (filter flush).

Key Notes

The dilemma of challenge level vs. challenge time:

When the product is close to the bactericidal boundary (close to 1 log reduction), a contradiction arises:

  • Longer challenge time = better simulation of the actual process, but too many organisms may die, so ≥ 107 CFU/cm2 may not be reached
  • Shorter challenge time = enough viable organisms are maintained, but key data such as differential pressure, flow rate, and throughput may not be collected adequately

This calls for a balanced scientific decision between the two. There is no perfect answer, but the rationale for your decision must be documented.

Temperature note: The viability of B. diminuta may be inhibited above 40°C. If your process temperature is higher, consider performing the viability assessment at room temperature in order to design a workable challenge strategy.

Practical Application

CDMO decision process:

Suppose your viability test shows that B. diminuta in the product declined by 0.8 log after 15 minutes (close to 1 log), while your actual filtration process takes 45 minutes. You then have several options:

  1. Shorten the challenge time to within 15 minutes to ensure sufficient organism numbers, but you must show that this is still representative of your process
  2. Switch to the filter flush method: pre-wet the membrane with the product, flush out residual product, and then introduce the challenge organism
  3. Increase the initial inoculum concentration so that ≥ 107 CFU/cm2 can still be maintained despite the reduction

Whichever option is chosen, a scientific rationale and a documented risk assessment are required.

Practice Questions

  1. What is the "1 log difference" rule? How does it determine whether you can use the direct inoculation method?
  2. If your product is strongly acidic (pH 3.0) and B. diminuta dies rapidly in it, how should you design the challenge approach?
  3. Why is a filter flush more than just "rinsing"? What needs to be validated?
6.6.5 Bacterial Retention Validation Studies

Original Text

The goal of bacterial retention validation studies is to have documented evidence demonstrating that the filtration process will consistently remove a high level of a standard bacterium, or relevant bioburden isolate, suspended within the product or surrogate fluid under simulated process conditions.

The decision to test membrane discs or a full-size process filter depends upon the study goal. Ideally, the test filter should mimic the process filter unit as closely as possible, including the membrane type and pore size. If the study is to validate the bacterial retention efficiency of a particular membrane material, the use of a small test membrane disc is generally regarded as sufficient, but the filter selected should be justified. The test methodology used for determining physical integrity of process filters should yield results that are meaningful in terms of bacterial retention studies, and correlation data should be available from the filter supplier. If different test methods are used, a relationship between the two should be demonstrated.

The following should be considered when conducting process-specific bacterial retention validation studies on membrane filters:

  • A thorough assessment of the filtration process should be conducted, including the nature of the process fluid (e.g., aqueous, acid, base, organic), active filtration volume, total product/filter contact time, process pressure, process flow rate, process temperature, number and/or frequency of intermittent cycles ("on/off"), and process-filter design specifications.
  • Multiple filter membrane lots (typically three) should be included in process-specific bacterial retention validation studies. In cases where the product may be considered aggressive to the membrane, the exact number of filters and the test design will depend on the process.
  • At least one of the three filter membrane lots used for the process-specific bacterial retention validation study should have a pre-study or pre-use physical integrity-test value as close as possible to the filter manufacturer's test specification. The filter manufacturer is responsible for defining the criteria for the minimum specification filter. Due to improved process control during membrane manufacturing, it may not be possible to obtain membranes at or near the filter manufacturer's production limit. In this case, test membranes should be selected from a manufactured lot whose pre-use integrity test values are as low as practical but representative of the standard filter-manufacturing process.
  • Membrane physical-integrity test values from bacterial retention validation studies should be included in test reports. The physical integrity should be determined prior to and after challenge-testing using water, product, or other wetting fluid for which specifications exist. Use of a low-surface-tension wetting fluid for post-use integrity-testing may be required to remove residual product.
  • An investigation should be conducted if the test organism is recovered downstream of any filter after the product bacterial retention study. If the investigation confirms penetration of the filter by the test organism and the filter meets its integrity-test specification, then the applicability of this filter under these process conditions should be reconsidered.
  • Families of products with the same ingredients, varying only in concentration, may be validated by challenging the concentration extremes and accepting the intermediate concentrations by bracketing. If a single product is determined to be a worst-case representative, then rationale and data should accompany the model. A placebo (without the API but belonging to the same product family) may be considered for product bracketing with appropriate justification.
  • During bacterial retention studies, incorporation of pressure increases to simulate integrity-testing (e.g., PUPSIT) should be based on risk assessment performed by the end-user (e.g., if process fluid is used for PUPSIT that could potentially pose a risk to bacterial penetration). Examples of factors to consider for risk assessment include the potential bioburden in the wetting fluid (based on a qualitative and quantitative assessment), type of integrity-test performed, and maximum number of integrity-tests that the filter could be exposed to. Filter suppliers qualify the filter to be able to withstand the elevated pressures resulting from integrity-testing. Therefore, the risk assessment should be focused on the potential for any bioburden in the wetting fluid to penetrate the filter as a result of PUPSIT.
  • Under typical manufacturing conditions with controlled conditions, the risk of bacterial penetration due to PUPSIT is considered low. If PUPSIT simulation is deemed necessary, the test design should be accompanied by a technical rationale.
  • Filter reuse is typically not practical or recommended for pharmaceutical purposes. However, if a sterilizing-grade filter is reused, justification should be provided, and reuse parameters should be validated. Scale-down laboratory studies are unlikely to be able to simulate the conditions of filter reuse by an end user (e.g., multiple sterilization cycles, clean in place, excessive handling). Therefore, consideration should be given to testing process filters that have been exposed to the entire cycle of reuse by the end-user to supplement the small-scale study.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Bacterial retention validation studies are the final real-world test that brings together the knowledge from all the preceding sections. The key design points are:

  • Test scale: Either small membrane discs or full-scale process filters can be used. Discs are suitable for verifying the retention efficiency of the membrane material itself, but correlation data from the supplier are required to bridge small-scale and full-scale results.
  • Multiple membrane lots: Three different filter lots are generally needed, and at least one lot should have an integrity test value as close as possible to the lower limit of the supplier's specification (minimum specification filter), to simulate the worst case.
  • Integrity testing before and after: A physical integrity test of the filter must be performed both before and after the challenge and recorded in the report.
  • Bracketing: For the same product family (same ingredients, different concentrations), the extreme concentrations can be tested, and intermediate concentrations accepted by bracketing.

Analogy

The whole validation study is like a comprehensive stress test of a security system. You cannot test just once, or test just one door (one filter lot):

  • Three lots = testing three different doors, to confirm you did not just get lucky with an especially good door
  • Minimum specification filter = deliberately picking the "weakest" door to test, to ensure that even the worst door holds
  • Integrity tests before and after = checking before the test that the door is not broken, and after the test that the door was not damaged by the impact
  • Bracketing = if your security system must stop packages from "1 kg" to "100 kg", testing 1 kg and 100 kg is enough to accept everything in between

Key Notes

Seriousness of downstream recovery: If challenge organisms are recovered downstream of the filter after the challenge test, this is a major event. An investigation is required:

  • If the investigation confirms that organisms did penetrate the filter, and the filter's integrity test passed - this means the filter may not be suitable under these process conditions and needs to be re-evaluated
  • This is more serious than "leakage caused by a damaged filter" - because it implies that even a filter that passes the integrity test can fail

PUPSIT simulation: Under controlled, typical manufacturing conditions, the risk of PUPSIT causing bacterial penetration is considered low. However, if the user decides that PUPSIT needs to be simulated, this must be based on a risk assessment, focusing on whether potential bioburden in the wetting fluid could penetrate the filter under PUPSIT pressure.

Key Notes - Filter Reuse

Filter reuse is generally not recommended in the pharmaceutical industry. If reuse is truly necessary:

  • Adequate scientific justification must be provided
  • The reuse parameters must be validated
  • Small-scale laboratory studies may not adequately simulate actual reuse conditions (such as multiple sterilization cycles, CIP, and excessive processing)
  • Consideration should be given to supplementing small-scale studies with process filters taken after actual reuse cycles

This echoes the TR26 decision hierarchy: assurance of a sterile filtrate is always the first priority. The cost savings from reusing filters should never outweigh the risk to sterility assurance.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should at least one filter lot be selected close to the "minimum specification"? If all lots are of very good quality, what problem does that represent?
  2. If challenge organisms are recovered downstream of the filter after the challenge but the filter's integrity test passed, what should you do? Is this more serious or less serious than a filter that fails the integrity test? Why?
  3. Your CDMO client asks that PUPSIT be simulated in the bacterial retention validation. According to TR26, how should you respond? What additional risk assessments are needed?
  4. If your facility wants to reuse sterilizing-grade filters for cost reasons, what is TR26's attitude? What conditions must be met?

Practical Application

CDMO validation design example:

Suppose your CDMO manufactures a product family with three concentrations (10 mg/mL, 50 mg/mL, 100 mg/mL) under identical process conditions. You could design the validation as follows:

This design both meets regulatory requirements and makes effective use of resources.

6.6.6 Bacterial Retention Validation Studies: Considerations for Selection of Worst-Case Test Conditions

Original Text

There are different levels of risk associated with process-filtration parameters. Some are associated with microbial proliferation in the product prior to filtration, while others are associated with a higher risk of passage of bacteria through the filter. While some generalizations can be made with respect to selection of worst-case test parameters, it is important that worst-case parameters are determined on a case-by-case basis. Further, the worst-case parameters should be selected such that the intended manufacturing conditions are covered by the process-specific bacterial retention studies but should be reflective of the processing parameters established during PD (with an appropriate and justified safety factor). Bacterial challenge over the full process time assesses the potential for time-dependent factors, such as filter compatibility, maintenance of integrity and occurrence of time-dependent penetration.

The pressure differential across the test filters demonstrated during bacterial retention studies should meet or exceed the maximum pressure differential permitted during processing, within the filter manufacturer's design specifications. The intended process flow rates should be incorporated when designing the challenge conditions. It may not be possible to maintain both the target pressure and flux for the full duration of a retention study, while also achieving the desired total filter wet time and (scaled) batch size. In such cases, static wet hold times, recirculation, or intermittent filtration, can be incorporated into the study design when justified.

Bacterial retention study conditions should simulate the production process as closely as possible, but variations may be acceptable when they are well-justified and unlikely to affect the risk of bacterial penetration. Since bacterial retention studies are generally performed in a laboratory, the methodology should be scaled accordingly. The flux should be scaled to an equivalent flow rate per unit area, expressed for example in (ml/min)/cm2. If filtration is regulated by pressure, the challenge test pressure should represent the NOR with consideration to the maximum filtration pressure allowed within the process and the likelihood of operating the filtration process at the PAR. Product recovery by blowdown may be performed at a higher pressure than maximum pressure during normal filtration and should be simulated at the end of the bacterial retention study.

Table 6.6.6-1🔍 lists examples of factors to consider when selecting worst-case parameters for process-specific bacterial retention studies.

Table 6.6.6-1🔍 Considerations for Selection of Bacterial Retention Study Parameters

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Worst-Case Test Parameters: When designing a bacterial retention validation study, you must select worst-case parameters that "bracket" actual manufacturing conditions. These parameters are not arbitrarily inflated; they are based on data established during process development (PD), plus a reasonable and justified safety factor.

Time-Dependent Penetration: As filtration time increases, bacteria may gradually pass through the membrane. This is linked to factors such as deteriorating membrane compatibility and a reduced ability to maintain integrity, so the challenge test must cover the full process time.

Flux Scaling: Laboratory-scale bacterial retention studies need to scale the flow rate to flux per unit area, (ml/min)/cm2, to ensure equivalence with production scale.

Analogy

Worst-case design is like choosing the hardest evacuation route in a fire drill: you do not rehearse only the easiest exit, but simulate the scenario with the densest smoke, the longest distance and the most people. If people can evacuate safely under such extreme conditions, everyday conditions are naturally fine. Likewise, if a filter still fully retains bacteria under the combination of highest pressure, longest time and highest viscosity, you can have much more confidence in normal process conditions.

Key Notes

Table 6.6.6-1🔍 is a practical design "checklist":

  • Viscosity ↑ = pressure ↑ = penetration risk ↑
  • Surface tension ↓ = high-risk fluids such as liposomes let bacteria pass through more easily
  • Osmolality ↑ = bacterial cells may shrink and pass through more easily
  • Neutral pH = best supports microbial growth, and is therefore the worst case
  • Extremes of ionic strength = may affect the adsorptive sequestration mechanism

Pay particular attention to temperature, which is a "case-specific" three-way consideration: low temperature increases viscosity, room temperature supports microbial growth, and high temperature may expose compatibility problems.

Key Notes: Blowdown Simulation

Product recovery by blowdown (using gas pressure to push out residual liquid) often uses a higher pressure than normal filtration. This step must be simulated at the end of the bacterial retention study, because it represents the "final blow" the membrane experiences in the process. Testing only the normal filtration pressure and ignoring blowdown would miss a key element of the worst case.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your client has a liposome formulation that requires sterilizing filtration validation. According to Table 6.6.6-1🔍, you need to pay particular attention to:

  • Lower surface tension -- liposomes are themselves a high-risk fluid
  • The product may contain lipid components -- list them directly as a consideration
  • Temperature settings need a three-way consideration
  • Blowdown pressure may be higher than normal filtration pressure

As a CDMO, you need to explain in the validation protocol, parameter by parameter, why each worst-case condition was chosen. This is QbD (Quality by Design) thinking applied to filtration validation.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is neutral pH considered the worst case, rather than extreme acidic or alkaline pH?
  2. If the laboratory cannot maintain both the target pressure and the flux for the full process time, what alternative approaches can be built into the study design?
  3. Why should ionic strength consider "extreme values" rather than a single direction (high or low)? Which retention mechanism is this related to?
6.6.7 Factors to Consider for Bacterial Retention Study Design (incl. 6.6.7.1 – 6.6.7.5)

Original Text

6.6.7 Factors to Consider for Bacterial Retention Study Design

If questions arise during protocol development regarding the acceptability of a testing methodology, it may be advisable to contact the appropriate regulatory agency for guidance. Figure 6.6.7-1🔍 reflects key steps to be considered when selecting the appropriate validation strategy for a specific filter and product/process combination.

*Note: Concurrence of the appropriate regulatory agency should be sought prior to using this methodology.

Figure 6.6.7-1🔍 Sterilizing Filtration Process Validation Strategy

Irrespective of the test design used, it is important to include a 0.4/0.45 µm positive control filter in parallel with the test filters to assure organism size, monodispersion and viability. Other modifications for the 0.45 µm control filter may be appropriate if fully justified and still demonstrate the challenge organism is fit for purpose.

6.6.7.1 Non-bactericidal Processes and Fluids

Direct inoculation of the product with the challenge microorganism is the preferred method of validating microbial retention of a sterilizing-grade filter. This is possible with products and process fluids that have demonstrated no bactericidal effects from the product or processing conditions. For these processes, the product should be directly inoculated with the challenge organism at sufficient concentration and under simulated processing conditions, including time, pressure, flow rate, volume throughput and other critical variables (e.g., temperature) as indicated in Table 6.6.6-1. Dilution from bacterial inoculation should be minimized to prevent altering the product any more than necessary.

6.6.7.2 Bacteriostatic/Bactericidal/Nondispersive Challenge Fluids

Performing bacterial retention studies on bactericidal products makes it more difficult to answer questions relating to validation, such as What effect does the product have on the filter and what effect does the product have on flora within the product? Bacterial retention studies performed on a bactericidal formulation, or under challenge conditions adverse to microbial viability (e.g., elevated temperature), may not produce valid results.

To evaluate the potential effect of the product/process on the filter, the filter may be preconditioned with the product under simulated processing conditions, including flow rate, pressure, temperature, volume throughput and time. This preconditioning may be performed by recirculating the product through the test filter in a closed-loop system or by a single pass (which could include a static soak to meet required contact time) through the test filter, followed by the bacterial challenge. Section 6.6.7.3, Section 6.6.7.4, and Section 6.6.7.5 discuss examples of test-method modifications that may be used to accommodate this type of bacterial retention study. Other modification methods may be equally appropriate, however, if justified.

6.6.7.3 Reduced Exposure Time

In some instances, the challenge organism may not survive in the product for the total processing time. The product should be inoculated directly with the challenge organism at sufficient concentration near the end of the preconditioning phase of the challenge (see Figure 6.6.7-1🔍). The duration of the bacterial retention study should be supported by the viability data.

An alternative is to expose the challenge microorganisms in the challenge fluid under static conditions. After exposing the challenge bacteria in the product at process temperature and preconditioning the filter by recirculating the product (without the bacteria) under simulated processing conditions, the filter could be challenged under worst-case processing conditions (e.g., differential pressure and flow rate). The challenge duration should be minimized to ensure the challenge microorganisms remain viable during the actual exposure, challenge, and recovery periods.

6.6.7.4 Modify Test Method Parameters

Test-method parameter modification is useful for challenging bactericidal products, since it can involve changing only one process variable, such as temperature. Using this approach maintains the product/challenge organism interaction. This approach does not account for all possible process/product interactions but should allow the use of the standard challenge organism. After preconditioning the filter with the product under actual process conditions, the bactericidal component of the process (e.g., temperature) is modified and the bacterial retention study performed. The product should be inoculated directly with the bacterial retention study microorganism at a sufficient concentration (see Figure 6.6.7-1🔍).

6.6.7.5 Modifying Test Product Formulation

Another modified method is to remove the bactericidal component from the product for the bacterial retention study following preconditioning of the filter with the actual product. This may be as simple as adjusting the pH to a nonbactericidal range, removing or diluting the bactericidal component, or using a surrogate fluid. Whatever approach is used should be supported by a technical rationale.

The modified formulation should be inoculated directly with the challenge organism at a sufficient concentration under simulated product processing conditions (see Figure 6.6.7-1🔍). The bactericidal agent should be removed to a level that eliminates its interference with the challenge microorganism.

Another option is to precondition the test and control filters with the bactericidal process fluid under simulated worst-case processing conditions. After removing the bactericidal fluid from the test rig, the filters can then be challenged with the test organism suspended in surrogate fluid that has been justified to resemble the process fluid (e.g., chemical or physical properties). Other test modifications may also be acceptable if justified.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Positive Control Filter (0.45 µm): A 0.45 µm control filter must be run in parallel with the test filter in every bacterial retention study. This is not to test retention capability, but to confirm that the size, dispersion and viability of the challenge organism meet requirements. If the control filter does not recover the bacteria, the validity of the test results cannot be confirmed.

Direct Inoculation: The ideal approach -- the challenge microorganism is added directly to the product, and filtration is performed under simulated process conditions. This method best reflects the real situation, but it presupposes that the product itself does not kill or inhibit the challenge microorganism.

Preconditioning: When the product is bactericidal, the filter is first treated with the actual product under simulated conditions so that it is "exposed" to whatever effects the product may have on it, and only then is the bacterial challenge performed. This two-step design both evaluates the effect of the product on the filter and ensures the viability of the challenge microorganism.

Analogy

The three strategies for bacterial retention studies are like a coach designing tests for different playing fields:

  • 6.6.7.1 Direct inoculation = playing the match directly on a standard field (the product is not bactericidal; conditions are ideal)
  • 6.6.7.2 Preconditioning for bactericidal products = the field has irritating substances, so the players first acclimatize on the field and then start the match
  • 6.6.7.3-5 Modified methods = the field is too dangerous, so you either shorten the match, change one field condition, or test on a similar but safe field

Whichever method is used, you need a "referee" (the positive control filter) to confirm that the rules of the match are valid.

Key Notes: Three Modification Strategies for Bactericidal Products

When the product kills the challenge microorganism, TR26 provides three progressive modification strategies:

  • 6.6.7.3 Shorten the exposure time: Add the bacteria just before the end of the preconditioning phase, shortening the contact time between the bacteria and the bactericidal product. Viability data are needed to support the chosen duration.
  • 6.6.7.4 Modify test method parameters: Change only one process variable (such as temperature) while maintaining the product/microorganism interaction. For example, if the product is bactericidal at 60°C, lower the temperature to 25°C after preconditioning and then perform the challenge.
  • 6.6.7.5 Modify the product formulation: Remove the bactericidal component (for example, by adjusting pH or diluting the bactericidal agent) or use a surrogate fluid. A technical rationale must support the justification for the modification.

The common prerequisite of all three approaches is that the filter must first be preconditioned with the actual product under actual conditions.

Key Notes: Regulatory Communication

TR26 specifically notes that if there are questions about the acceptability of the test method during protocol development, it is recommended to contact the appropriate regulatory authority for guidance. This is especially important when using the modified methods (6.6.7.3-5), because they depart from the "gold standard" of direct inoculation and require thorough scientific justification.

Practical Application

CDMO decision tree: When you receive a client's new product for sterilizing filtration validation, the first step is a bactericidal/bacteriostatic test:

  • Result: not bactericidal → go directly to 6.6.7.1 and use direct inoculation, which is the simplest and clearest route
  • Result: bactericidal → evaluate the kill rate and mechanism, then choose 6.6.7.3 (shorten time), 6.6.7.4 (change conditions) or 6.6.7.5 (change formulation)
  • Uncertain → first communicate with the regulatory authority to confirm the acceptability of the method

Which modification method to choose depends on the bactericidal mechanism and severity of the product. Generally, try first the method that changes the fewest variables (6.6.7.3 > 6.6.7.4 > 6.6.7.5).

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the positive control filter (0.45 µm) necessary in every bacterial retention study? If the positive control does not recover bacteria, are the test results still valid?
  2. For an injectable product containing 0.1% phenol as a preservative, which modification strategy would you choose, and why?
  3. Compare 6.6.7.4 (modifying parameters) and 6.6.7.5 (modifying the formulation): which one retains more information about the original product/filter interaction?
6.6.8 Filtrate Sampling

Original Text

Analysis of the entire challenge effluent is necessary to determine the ability of a sterilizing-grade filter to retain the bacterial challenge. This can be done either by direct passage through an appropriate analytical membrane installed downstream of the test filter (whereby any penetrating bacteria would be captured on the analytical membrane) or by filtrate collection in a sterile vessel and subsequent filtration through analytical membrane(s). The analytical membranes should have been qualified as fit for purpose and will usually have a maximum rating of 0.4 µm or 0.45 µm. Selection of the size and porosity should be based on the application conditions and should not prevent achieving the desired differential pressure across the test filter. Sampling a portion of the filtrate is insufficient to validate sterilizing filtration, because a small number of cells may have penetrated the filter and remain undetected in the portion of the filtrate not sampled and analyzed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Entire Effluent Analysis: Bacterial retention validation requires analysis of all of the challenge effluent; analyzing only part of the sample is not permitted. Even if only a very few bacteria penetrate the membrane, partial sampling may miss them entirely, leading to a false negative result.

Analytical Membrane: A 0.4/0.45 µm membrane installed downstream of the test filter to capture any bacteria that penetrate the test filter. It can be installed directly in series, or the filtrate can be collected first and then filtered for analysis.

Analogy

This is like full screening vs. spot checking of luggage at an airport: if the airport X-rays only 10% of the luggage (partial sampling), dangerous items could be hiding in the 90% that was never checked. Sterilizing filtration validation follows exactly the same logic -- you must "scan" 100% of the filtrate, because even a single bacterium penetrating means the filter validation has failed.

Key Notes

"Partial sampling is not sufficient to validate sterilizing filtration" -- this is an explicit statement in TR26. In practice, there are two ways to analyze the entire effluent:

  • In-line: The analytical membrane is installed directly in series downstream of the test filter, and the filtrate passes straight through it -- simple and direct
  • Off-line: The filtrate is first collected in a sterile container and then passed through the analytical membrane -- suitable for large filtrate volumes or situations requiring special handling

Note that the choice of analytical membrane must not affect the transmembrane differential pressure of the test filter; otherwise the test conditions will change.

Practice Questions

  1. If the pore size of the analytical membrane is poorly chosen (for example, too small), what effect might this have on the test results?
  2. Why does TR26 emphasize that "the analytical membrane should not affect the transmembrane differential pressure of the test filter"? How does this relate to worst-case design?
6.6.9 Results Interpretation

Original Text

Acceptance criteria for sterilizing-grade filter performance are met when no passage of the bacterial challenge is detected in three test filters with a valid positive control (i.e., bacterial recovery through the 0.45 µm-rated control filter). If passage is detected in one filter, and no assignable cause can be determined, retesting may be conducted (e.g., three filters from the failed membrane lot) following an investigation and risk assessment to determine a potential root cause. If bacterial penetration occurs in any of the triplicate tests, then the filter cannot be considered validated under the specified conditions. If an assignable cause for the failure can be determined (e.g., damage to the filter or breach in aseptic technique), the suspect lot of filters should also be retested for confirmation of the proposed root cause for the initial failure. If the filter fouls prior to achieving the target volume throughput, the bacterial retention study should be terminated and the resultant total challenge determined. An investigation into the cause of fouling should be performed and documented, and a new bacterial retention study design should be proposed to ensure volume throughput is met based on filterability test results.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Acceptance Criteria: All three test filters show zero penetration + the positive control recovers bacteria validly = pass. This is an "all or nothing" standard, with no grey area of "a small amount of penetration is allowed".

Assignable Cause: When a filter shows penetration, an investigation is needed to determine whether there is a clear external cause (such as filter damage or an aseptic technique error). An assignable cause and no assignable cause lead down different follow-up paths.

Filter Fouling: If the filter becomes plugged before reaching the target volumetric throughput, the study must be terminated. This does not mean "pass"; the study protocol must be redesigned.

Analogy

Results interpretation is like the road test for a driver's license:

  • Three candidates take the test at the same time (three test filters), and the model is qualified only if all of them pass
  • If one candidate commits a violation (one filter shows penetration), you must investigate whether it is a test-site problem (an assignable cause, such as a vehicle breakdown) or insufficient ability (no assignable cause)
  • If it is a test-site problem, a new group of candidates can retake the test; if it is insufficient ability, the driving school's teaching method (the validation conditions) needs to be reviewed
  • If the car breaks down in the middle of the test (filter fouling), the test is invalid and must be rescheduled after the cause is identified

Key Notes

Results interpretation decision tree:

  • 3/3 pass + positive control valid → validation successful
  • 1/3 fails + assignable cause → investigate → retest 3 filters from that lot to confirm the root cause
  • 1/3 fails + no assignable cause → investigation + risk assessment → retest may be possible (for example, with 3 filters from the failed lot)
  • Any retested filter still fails → that filter cannot be validated under those conditions
  • Filter fouling → investigate the cause of fouling → redesign the protocol based on filterability testing

Formulas and Calculations: Triplicate Testing Logic

Validation test requirements:
  Number of test filters:   3 (triplicate)
  Positive control filter:  0.45 μm × 1 (tested in parallel)

Decision logic:
  Penetration = 0 / 3 filters  →  PASS (validation successful)
  Penetration = 1 / 3 filters  →  INVESTIGATE → possible RETEST
  Penetration ≥ 2 / 3 filters  →  FAIL (validation fails under these conditions)
  Fouling (target volume not reached) →  VOID → redesign

Practice Questions

  1. Why are three test filters (triplicate) required rather than a single filter? How does this relate to statistical confidence?
  2. If the positive control filter (0.45 µm) also does not recover bacteria, how should the result of the entire test be judged?
  3. Why can filter fouling not simply be judged a "pass" (since no bacteria penetrated)?
6.7 Considerations for Sterilizing-Grade Filters Configured in Series

Original Text

When two final sterilizing-grade filters are configured in series, it is necessary to clarify whether the setup is redundant filtration or double filtration, sometimes referred to as additive filtration (see Section 5.5.1 for detailed descriptions of various filter train designs). When redundant filtration is used (i.e., only one filter is required to meet sterility and only one filter must meet post-use integrity-test specifications), the process-specific bacterial retention study should be based on only one filter. Double filtration means both filters are required to achieve sterility (usually determined from process-specific bacterial retention study), and both filters must therefore meet post-use integrity test specifications.

Regardless of whether the setup is redundant filtration or double filtration, extractables should be considered for both filters.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Redundant Filtration: Two filters in series, where either one alone can achieve sterility. The second filter is a "backup", like an aircraft with two engines that can fly on one. Only one filter needs to pass the post-use integrity test.

Double Filtration / Additive Filtration: Two filters in series, and both are necessary to achieve sterility. Both must pass the post-use integrity test. This typically arises when the bacterial retention study of a single filter cannot achieve zero penetration.

Analogy

Think of the concept of double seat belts:

  • Redundant Filtration = seat belt + airbag: the seat belt alone is enough, and the airbag is additional protection. You only need to confirm that the seat belt works properly.
  • Double Filtration = two seat belts (because one is not strong enough): both must be checked and confirmed to work properly; neither can be omitted.

Key Notes

Impact of the key difference on validation:

  • Redundant: The bacterial retention study is based on a single filter; only one filter needs to pass the post-use integrity test
  • Double: The bacterial retention study may show that a single filter is insufficient; both filters must pass the post-use integrity test
  • Common point: In either case, the extractables of both filters must be evaluated -- because the drug solution contacts both filters

At the decision-hierarchy level, this relates directly to Sterile Filtrate Assurance -- if it is unclear whether the arrangement is redundant or double, the validation protocol and integrity testing strategy cannot be designed correctly.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: A client asks to use two 0.2 µm filters in series on an aseptic filling line. You must first confirm:

  • Is this redundant or double? Review the bacterial retention validation data.
  • If redundant: only one filter needs post-use integrity testing per batch, but E&L must be evaluated for both.
  • If double: both filters need post-use integrity testing for every batch, and both must pass. Failure of either filter = the batch is at risk.

This definition directly affects batch release decisions, so be sure to define it clearly in the validation protocol.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must extractables still consider both filters even in redundant filtration?
  2. If the second filter in a double filtration fails its post-use integrity test, how does the disposition of that batch differ from that of redundant filtration?
6.8 Chemical Compatibility of Filter

Original Text

While compatibility of the filter is assessed during initial filter-selection by the end user, compatibility testing may also be performed as part of the final sterilizing-grade filter validation testing. Chemical compatibility testing should encompass the entire device and depends on the fluid, filtration temperature and contact time.

A summary of worst-case conditions for conducting compatibility testing is shown in Table 6.8-1.

Table 6.8-1🔍 Worst-Case Considerations for Compatibility Testing

An assessment of chemical compatibility could include bacterial retention study data, post-exposure integrity-testing, tensile strength, nonvolatile residue (NVR), extractables, particulates, flow rate, optical or scanning electron microscopy (SEM), burst pressure, and membrane/O-ring thickness (23).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Chemical Compatibility: An assessment of whether the filter and its entire device (including housing, O-rings and support structure) maintain structural integrity and functionality after contact with the drug solution and exposure to sterilization conditions. This is not only a question of the membrane material itself, but of the entire filtration device.

NVR (Nonvolatile Residue): The solid material remaining after the filter soak solution is evaporated; it is one of the indicators of chemical compatibility assessment. An increase in NVR may suggest that the filter material is dissolving or degrading.

Analogy
