Chemical compatibility testing is like choosing the material of a teapot for brewing tea: a Yixing clay teapot is fine for Pu-erh tea, but using it for highly acidic fruit tea may leach harmful substances. Similarly, a filter may be perfectly compatible with a pH 7 buffer, yet contact with a pH 2 acidic solution may cause the material to degrade. You must test with the most challenging conditions (highest temperature, longest time, most extreme pH) to ensure the "teapot" is safe under any "tea" condition.

Key Notes

The complete toolbox for chemical compatibility assessment:

  • Functional indicators: bacterial retention data, post-exposure integrity testing, flow rate changes
  • Physical indicators: tensile strength, burst pressure, membrane thickness/O-ring thickness
  • Chemical indicators: NVR, extractables, particulates
  • Microscopic observation: optical or SEM (scanning electron microscopy) examination for changes in membrane structure

Note that in Table 6.8-1🔍, the sterilization conditions are also one of the worst-case conditions -- the filter first undergoes the maximum number of sterilization cycles and then contacts the most challenging drug solution, which represents the worst case of cumulative stress.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must chemical compatibility testing cover the "entire device" and not just the filter membrane itself? Think about the risks that O-ring and housing materials could introduce.
  2. Of the four worst-case factors in Table 6.8-1🔍, which is the easiest to overlook in practice, and why?
  3. If the post-exposure integrity test passes but SEM shows obvious changes on the membrane surface, how would you judge compatibility?
6.9 Revalidation Considerations

Original Text

Once a filter is validated for use in a given application, further validation may be required if changes to the filter or manufacturing process are made. Some changes that may require revalidation include, but are not limited to:

  • Change in membrane type or filter materials of construction.
  • Increase in process parameters (e.g., volume to be filtered through a given filter area, flux, pressure, duration)
  • Change in filter configuration (e.g., cartridge to encapsulated device). While the process-specific bacterial retention study data will likely not be impacted, consideration should be given to a reassessment of extractables/leachables and compatibility.
  • Product formulation changes (e.g., change in product concentration, pH)
  • Change in sterilization method (revalidation of the extractables/leachables assessment and filter-integrity post-sterilization should be considered based on risk assessment with appropriate justification)
  • Temperature of filtration.
  • Use of a filter with the same membrane type but not from the same supplier.

A well-structured risk assessment should be performed by process and filtration experts to evaluate the potential impact of these changes. In line with internal policy for filter management, the filter end user should approve all changes that potentially impact the current good manufacturing processes (GMP) compliance of the system.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Revalidation: When a change occurs in a validated filtration process, you need to evaluate whether some or all of the validation tests must be repeated. This is not "automatically redo everything", but a risk-assessment-based judgment of which validation elements are affected.

Same Membrane, Different Supplier: This trigger is easy to overlook but very important. Even when the membrane material specification is the same, different suppliers may have different manufacturing conditions and quality controls, so revalidation is required.

Analogy

The logic of revalidation is like a car warranty: you buy a car and it passes the safety inspection (initial validation), but if you modify the engine (membrane change), fit larger tires (increased process parameters), convert the manual transmission to an automatic (configuration change) or use parts from a different brand (same membrane, different supplier), the warranty may become void -- you need to resubmit the car for inspection to confirm it is still safe after the modifications.

The key point is that not every modification requires a full re-inspection. Changing the car air freshener (a minor change) does not, but changing the engine (a major change) certainly does. This is the value of risk assessment.

Key Notes

Change type vs. scope of revalidation:

  • Membrane/material change → full revalidation may be needed (bacterial retention + E&L + compatibility + integrity)
  • Increase in process parameters → mainly affects bacterial retention validation (worst-case conditions need to be re-evaluated)
  • Configuration change (e.g., cartridge to encapsulated) → bacterial retention may be unaffected, but E&L and compatibility need re-evaluation
  • Formulation change → may affect bacterial retention, chemical compatibility and E&L
  • Sterilization method change → mainly affects E&L and post-sterilization integrity
  • Same membrane, different supplier → all validation elements need to be evaluated; full revalidation may be required

Practical Application

CDMO change control scenario: Your supplier notifies you that the PES membrane cartridge you have been using will be discontinued and recommends switching to an "equivalent" product. You should:

  1. Initiate the change control procedure
  2. Evaluate the differences between the new cartridge and the original (membrane material, housing material, structural design)
  3. Perform a risk assessment: is it "same membrane, same supplier, different model" or "same membrane material from a different supplier"?
  4. Develop a revalidation plan: at minimum E&L re-evaluation and chemical compatibility confirmation are needed, and the bacterial retention study may need to be repeated
  5. Make sure the technical documents for all affected products are updated

As a CDMO, you may serve products for multiple clients at the same time, and a single filter supplier change may trigger revalidation for several products -- this is an impact scope that must be considered in operational planning.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR26 specifically mention that "same membrane, different supplier" requires revalidation? What does an identical membrane specification not guarantee?
  2. If a change in process parameters is a "decrease" rather than an "increase" (for example, lower pressure), is revalidation still needed? Why?
  3. In a CDMO environment, how is responsibility for revalidation allocated between a client-initiated formulation change and a supplier-initiated filter change?
6.10 Miscellaneous

Original Text

With the introduction of newer manufacturing modalities, such as advanced therapy medicinal products (ATMPs), additional factors may need to be considered for the purposes of filter validation. For example, small batch sizes, short shelf life, and physical properties (e.g., shear sensitivity) may require careful planning and potentially modified test procedures to achieve the goals of filter validation while meeting regulatory expectations.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

ATMPs (Advanced Therapy Medicinal Products): These include gene therapies, cell therapies, tissue-engineered products and other novel types of medicinal products. Their characteristics (small batch sizes, short shelf life, shear sensitivity) pose new challenges to traditional filtration validation methods.

Shear Sensitivity: Some biological products (such as cell therapy products) may be damaged by shear forces during filtration, which adds complexity to filter selection and validation design.

Analogy

Filtration validation for traditional drugs is like testing whether a standard-size parcel can pass safely through a post office sorting system. ATMPs, however, are like shipping live animals -- the parcel (the product) is itself alive, fears being squeezed (shear sensitivity), cannot be stored for long (short shelf life) and only a small quantity is shipped each time (small batch size). You cannot handle it with the same standard sorting speed and pressure; it needs a whole new set of operating rules.

Key Notes

Although 6.10 is short, it sends an important signal: the principles of TR26 apply to all sterilizing filtration, but the specific methods need to be adapted to the product type. For a CDMO, if your business direction includes cell/gene therapy products, building filtration validation capability must be planned in advance, because:

  • Small batch sizes mean smaller laboratory scaling ratios, and special small-area test devices may be needed
  • Short shelf life means the validation timeline must be closely coordinated with the production schedule
  • Shear sensitivity may limit the usable filtration pressure and flow rate range

Practice Questions

  1. For a shear-sensitive cell therapy product, which worst-case parameters in Table 6.6.6-1🔍 need to be redefined?
  2. In filtration validation of a small-batch product, would performing "entire effluent analysis" (6.6.8) be easier or harder than for a large batch? Why?

Section 6.0 Complete Section Summary

Section 6.0 Product & Process-Specific Validation Testing is the core section of TR26 and covers all elements of sterilizing filtration validation. The following is a systematic review of the whole of Section 6.0:

  1. Purpose of validation testing and QbD thinking (6.1-6.2): Sterilizing filtration validation is not a simple "pass/fail" test but a systematic approach based on Quality by Design. Validation must demonstrate that, under worst-case conditions, the specific combination of filter and product/process reliably achieves sterility assurance.
  2. Adsorption considerations (6.3): Filters may adsorb active ingredients or formulation components of the drug product and affect product quality. Adsorption assessment is an indispensable part of validation, especially for low-dose or high-value biological products.
  3. Extractables & Leachables (6.4): Filters and their components may release chemicals into the drug solution under process conditions. E&L assessment follows the principle that "extractables are a worst-case predictor of leachables", and requires systematic studies under process conditions combined with toxicological assessment.
  4. Bacterial Retention (6.5-6.6): The core of the entire validation -- demonstrating that the filter completely retains the challenge microorganism under simulated worst-case conditions (Brevundimonas diminuta). Key elements include:
    • ASTM F838 as the foundational methodology (the filter's "driving test")
    • Three test filters (triplicate) + a 0.45 µm positive control must be used
    • Worst-case condition selection must be based on a systematic evaluation of Table 6.6.6-1🔍
    • Bactericidal products require modified methods (shorten time / change parameters / change formulation)
    • Entire effluent analysis; partial sampling is not permitted
    • Results interpretation follows a strict decision tree
  5. Series Filters (6.7): Redundant and double filtration must be clearly distinguished, as this directly affects the validation scope and the post-use integrity testing strategy. Regardless of configuration, E&L must be evaluated for both filters together.
  6. Chemical Compatibility (6.8): Assess the integrity of the entire filtration device (not just the filter membrane) after contact with the drug solution and exposure to sterilization conditions. Worst-case conditions include the combination of highest temperature, longest time, most extreme pH and maximum number of sterilization cycles.
  7. Revalidation (6.9): Any change to the filter, process parameters, product formulation or sterilization method requires a risk assessment to determine the scope of revalidation. Pay particular attention to "same membrane, different supplier" as a trigger.
  8. Novel Medicinal Products (ATMPs, 6.10): The small batch sizes, short shelf life and shear sensitivity of advanced therapies such as cell/gene therapy pose new challenges to traditional validation methods and call for innovative validation strategies.

Decision hierarchy reminder: Throughout Section 6.0, all validation decisions consistently follow the TR26 decision hierarchy -- (1) Sterile Filtrate Assurance is always the highest priority, (2) product quality/CQAs protect patient safety, (3) process robustness ensures reliable operations, and (4) commercial and efficiency considerations come only after the first three are satisfied. The fundamental purpose of validation testing is to provide the scientific evidence base for this decision hierarchy.

7

Section 7: Integrity Testing

p56-p70

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) -- Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p56 - p70

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the physical principles of integrity testing: the basic mechanisms of diffusive flow and bubble point
  • Understand the relationship between integrity test results and bacterial retention, and how test specifications are established
  • Learn to select the appropriate test method (bubble point, diffusive flow, pressure hold) based on membrane area, and understand the conversion equations for product-wet testing
  • Understand the considerations and regulatory expectations for PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing)
  • Master the troubleshooting process for integrity test failures and the analysis of insufficient wetting
7.0 Integrity Testing -- Overview

Original Text

Bacterial retention studies are used for validating a filter membrane but are not feasible for routine confirmation of the retentive capabilities of each filter used in the manufacture of sterile pharmaceuticals. Such challenge tests can clearly not be performed prior to the use of the filter in the production setting, as it involves deliberate introduction of contaminating microorganisms to the filter (a "destructive" test). It is also not feasible post-use, as it requires advanced microbiology equipment and multiday incubation periods. Hence the need for a nondestructive, physical-integrity test method that can be correlated with bacterial retention (i.e., can be used as a surrogate method for direct bacterial retention). The main objective of a nondestructive physical integrity test is to determine the presence of defects that may compromise a filter's retention capability without rendering the filter useless. Additionally, the integrity test establishes the similarity of the test filter to validated bacterial retention-challenged filters under process-related conditions.

For integrity tests on hydrophilic membranes, gas flow properties of wetted filter membranes are evaluated over a range of pressures. This can be demonstrated by completely wetting the entire filter membrane and introducing gas to the upstream side of the membrane at a low pressure. Capillary forces within the filter material keep the liquid from being expelled from the pores, and the wetted membrane functions as a barrier to convective gas flow. As this pressure is applied on the upstream side of the filter, a small amount of the gas dissolves into the wetting liquid, diffuses across the wetted membrane, and is released on the downstream side when it is maintained at atmospheric pressure (Figure 7.0-1🔍). The diffusion rate increases as the pressure on the upstream side is increased. Once a critical pressure is achieved ("the bubble point"), the effect of capillary forces keeping the liquid in the pores will be overcome by sufficient pressure, pushing the wetting liquid out through the membrane pores. This happens first for the largest pores, where capillary forces are the weakest (Figure 7.0-2🔍). If the rate of gas flow that passes to the downstream is measured at increasing pressure values, the membrane characterization curve (Figure 7.0-3🔍) can be obtained for the given membrane filter.

Characterization of these dynamics (both the diffusive flow rate at pressures below the bubble point, as well as the exact bubble point pressure) can indicate integrity of the filter in a nondestructive manner, resulting in a method that can be performed on every filter used in production. Diffusive flow theory is discussed further in Section 10.1. Figure 7.0-1🔍 illustrates gas diffusion through the wetted membrane pores at pressures where the wetting fluid is held in the pores by capillary forces. Figure 7.0-2🔍 illustrates gas flow through the membrane at a pressure exceeding the bubble point where the wetting fluid is expelled from the largest pores.

[Figure 7.0-1🔍 Gas Diffusion Through a Wetted Membrane]

[Figure 7.0-2🔍 Bubble Point Through a Wetted Membrane]

Figure 7.0-3🔍 illustrates the relationship between measured air flow downstream of a wetted filter membrane. Three characteristic portions of the curves act as the basis for membrane-filter integrity-testing:

  1. The linear portion on the low end of the pressure axis shows diffusive gas flow through the liquid held in the pores of the membrane.
  2. As the pressure is increased, there is a characteristic bend in the curve followed by another linear portion. This bend indicates the transition between diffusive gas flow and bulk, or viscous, flow.
  3. For an integral filter, bulk gas flow occurs after the bubble point of the largest pores has been exceeded. Before reaching this point, the majority of gas flow is due to free-flowing gas through open pores; a minor portion of the flow results from diffusion through the pores of the membrane that are still wetted.

[Figure 7.0-3🔍 Characteristic Curve of the Membrane]

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Integrity Test: A non-destructive physical test used to confirm that a sterilizing-grade filter is intact before and after use. Because the bacterial retention test is destructive and cannot be performed on every production filter, a "surrogate method" is needed to confirm the filter's retention capability indirectly.

Bubble Point: When the gas pressure applied upstream of a wetted membrane reaches a critical value, capillary forces can no longer hold the liquid in the pores; the liquid is expelled from the largest pores and gas begins to flow in bulk. This critical pressure is the bubble point.

Diffusive Flow: At pressures below the bubble point, a small amount of gas dissolves into the wetting liquid, diffuses across the wetted membrane, and is released on the downstream side. This flow is directly related to membrane integrity.

Capillary Forces: The force that holds liquid in very small pores because of surface tension. The smaller the pore, the greater the capillary force, and the higher the pressure needed to push the liquid out.

Analogy

Checking the gas before leaving home: Imagine you must confirm every day that the gas is turned off before you leave. You cannot call the gas company each time for a full pipeline pressure test (that would be like the bacterial retention test: time-consuming, laborious and destructive). Instead, you use a simple approach: you sniff for a gas smell and check that the burner flame looks normal. This is the idea behind a "non-destructive integrity test".

Blowing up a balloon to find the leaks: A bubble point test is like blowing up a balloon. At low pressure, tiny holes in the balloon surface let a small amount of air seep out slowly (diffusive flow). But when you blow hard enough, the largest hole suddenly lets a large volume of air rush out (bubble point). If the balloon leaks heavily at a very low pressure, it has a large hole (the membrane is not integral).

Key Notes

Why is integrity testing so important? In the decision hierarchy for sterilizing filtration, "sterile filtrate assurance" is always the first priority. Integrity testing is the bridge between "membrane qualification" and "actual production use": it lets us confirm, without destroying the filter, that every production filter has the same quality as the filters whose bacterial retention was validated.

The three regions of the membrane characteristic curve are the basis for understanding all integrity test methods: the low-pressure region (diffusive flow), the transition region, and the high-pressure region (bulk flow beyond the bubble point).

Practical Application

In a CDMO environment, every sterile filtration batch requires integrity testing. Suppose your facility produces 200 batches of sterile injectables a year, and each batch requires at least one post-use integrity test. If one batch fails the test, the whole batch may have to be discarded or reworked: a direct loss of millions of dollars, not counting investigation costs and the impact on customer trust. Understanding the physical principles of integrity testing helps you interpret results correctly and distinguish true filter damage from "false failures".

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the bacterial retention test not suitable as a routine test for every production batch? List at least two reasons.
  2. If a filter's bubble point value is abnormally low, what does this mean physically? What is the impact on sterility assurance?
  3. Which integrity test methods correspond to each of the three regions of the membrane characteristic curve?
7.1 Relationship Between Integrity Test Results and Bacterial Retention

Original Text

During membrane development by the filter manufacturer, retentive capability can be assessed by challenging successively "tighter" samples of the specific membrane under standardized test conditions and analyzing the bacterial passage results. Typically, the retention pattern observed will allow identification of physical integrity-test specifications using standard wetting fluids--acceptance criteria for the measured parameters within which no bacterial penetration will occur under standardized testing conditions (6). The integrity-test value established by this exercise is linked to the sterility of the filtrate. Such establishment of integrity-test value specifications should incorporate a safety margin to account for natural variability in measurement and membrane manufacture (e.g., by applying appropriate statistics with confidence intervals).

[Figure 7.1-1🔍 Gas Flow Profile Values and Filtrate Sterility]

Figure 7.1-1🔍 illustrates a typical relationship between the gas flow profile values and filtrate sterility from a series of five filters with decreasing maximum pore size (from left to right).

The bubble point test relates to the effective diameter of the largest pores present in the membrane, and these pores, along with membrane thickness and pore tortuosity, directly influence the retention properties of the membrane. The resolution of the bubble point test diminishes as a function of increasing filter area, since diffusive gas flow below the bubble point tends to obscure it (41-43).

While diffusion does not bear a direct relationship to pore size, the diffusive flow test provides a quantitative measurement in which maximum flow limit is established by the filter manufacturer at a designated test pressure lower than the minimum bubble point value.

During membrane development and initial integrity-test qualification by the filter manufacturer, testing flow rate at multiple pressure values enables plotting of the gas flow curve from diffusion at low pressures through the bubble point region at elevated pressures. These tests combine the advantages of the bubble-point and single-point diffusive-flow integrity tests in characterizing the pore size distribution. Methods for mathematically characterizing the diffusive-flow to bulk-flow transition region and correlation of the value generated to bacterial titer reduction may be found in reference literature.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Establishing integrity test specifications: During development, the membrane manufacturer tests a series of membrane samples with progressively "tighter" pore sizes. Each sample undergoes both a bacterial retention test and a physical integrity test to find the correspondence between the "integrity test value" and "whether bacteria are completely retained". The final specification value includes a safety margin to cover natural measurement and manufacturing variability.

Bubble point vs diffusive flow are complementary:

  • Bubble Point: Reflects the effective diameter of the largest pores and is a "worst-case" indicator
  • Diffusive Flow: Reflects the integrity of the overall membrane structure and is a quantitative "composite indicator"
  • Together they give a more complete picture of the pore size distribution

Analogy

The passing mark on a driving test: The way a membrane manufacturer sets integrity test specifications is like a transport authority setting driving test standards. Drivers of different skill levels (membranes of different pore sizes) take a road test (the bacterial retention test), and the authority looks for the relationship between the "written exam score" (the integrity test value) and "actual driving safety" (whether bacteria are retained). Finally, a "passing mark" (the specification value) is set, slightly stricter than the true critical point (the safety margin), to ensure that everyone who passes is really safe.

Key Notes

Limitations of bubble point testing on large-area filters: As membrane area increases, the diffusive flow below the bubble point also increases (because diffusive flow is proportional to area), which "masks" the bubble point signal and makes the bubble point harder to determine. This is why large-area filters usually use the diffusive flow method rather than the bubble point method.

The importance of the safety margin: The specification value is not the threshold at which bacteria are "just barely retained"; it is a safe value that incorporates a statistical confidence interval. This reflects the first principle of the decision hierarchy: sterile filtrate assurance always takes priority over efficiency considerations.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must integrity test specifications include a safety margin? What are the risks of not including one?
  2. Why does the resolution of the bubble point test decrease as membrane area increases? Explain from the standpoint of the physical principles.
  3. How does the membrane manufacturer link integrity test values to bacterial retention capability? What are the key steps in this process?
7.1.1 Test Method Selection

Original Text

The method for integrity-testing of production filters by the filter user should be chosen to provide reliable results based on the nature of the filter, product, and processing conditions. Bubble point, multiple-point and single-point diffusive-flow and pressure-hold tests can be used, recognizing that each has strengths and limitations that must be evaluated in terms of the particular circumstances of the test. These test methods can be used for both hydrophilic and hydrophobic membrane filters and can be performed manually or preferentially with automated integrity-test instruments. Section 10.2 contains further discussion of filter integrity-test methods.

The flow rate of the test gas that passes through the wetting fluid in the pores of the filter media via diffusive flow is much lower than the flow rate of bulk gas that passes through the filter media when the wetting fluid is displaced from the larger pores of the filter media (i.e., what occurs at and above the bubble point). The rate at which test gas passes through the filter media via diffusive flow is directly related to the surface area of the filter media (i.e., as the filter surface area increases, the flow rate due to diffusive flow increases).

Integrity-test instruments have a lower limit at which they can reliably determine the flow rate of the test gas passing through the filter media. For this reason, there is typically a minimum value for the surface area of the filter being integrity-tested to perform a diffusive-flow integrity test. Below this minimum surface area, the automated integrity tester may not be capable of determining the diffusive flow at the specific test pressure (i.e., <= 80% of bubble point). In contrast, when using the bubble point method, the algorithm is looking at the change in the flow rate instead of the absolute flow rate as the differential pressure is increased (specifically, the pressure at which bulk flow starts). For this reason, the bubble point integrity-test method is typically used instead of the diffusive-flow integrity-test method to perform an integrity test on a filter with a small surface area.

Above the minimum surface-area threshold for testing via the diffusive-flow integrity-test method, either the diffusive-flow or the bubble point test method may be used for a range of filter surface areas. As the surface area of the filter media being tested increases significantly (e.g., in the case where multiple round housings are used), there comes a point where the diffusive flow of gas passing through the wetted membrane is so high that it will be difficult for the integrity test to distinguish the bubble point from the high rate of "background" diffusive flow through wetted pores. For this reason, the diffusive-flow method is typically selected for the largest filter sizes.

The exact cutoffs for small, medium, and large surface area depend upon the attributes of the filter media being tested, the wetting fluid used, and the capability of the automated integrity tester being used, but general guidance is provided in Table 7.1.1-1.

*These values are approximate values only. Consult Filter Manufacturer for specific guidance.

One approach to diffusive-flow testing of large filter systems with multiple cartridges is to derive an assembly-specific value that is lower than the linear multiplied-limit of each cartridge. A technical evaluation should be conducted to justify this approach. If the assembly's diffusive air flow exceeds the corrected limit, an investigation would be required (e.g., individual tests on each cartridge, and confirmation of the seals with the housing).

When performing tests on filter capsules with associated SUS, the high pressures required for bubble point testing could exceed the pressure ratings of such equipment. For this reason, diffusive-flow tests may be preferred as they typically require lower pressures than bubble point-test methods. Or, in case the bubble point method is required, the SUS is designed to be compatible with a higher working-pressure range.

A modification to the diffusive flow method is a pressure-hold/decay method, where pressure is allowed to drop as diffusive flow across the wetted membrane occurs, instead of being held constant (see Appendix B for more information).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The three main test methods:

  • Bubble Point: Measures the pressure at which the largest pores are breached; suitable for small-area filters
  • Diffusive Flow: Measures the gas diffusion rate at a pressure below the bubble point; suitable for medium and large-area filters
  • Pressure Hold/Decay: A variant of the diffusive flow method that lets the pressure fall naturally to measure diffusive flow indirectly

Relationship between area and method selection: Diffusive flow is proportional to membrane area. When the area is too small, the diffusive flow is too low for instruments to measure reliably, so the bubble point method is used. When the area is too large, the background diffusive flow is so high that it masks the bubble point signal, so the diffusive flow method is used.

Analogy

Imagine you need to check a wall for cracks:

  • Small wall (bubble point method): You can get close and look carefully to find where the biggest crack is, which is easy to judge
  • Large wall (diffusive flow method): The wall is too big to inspect piece by piece, but you can spray a water mist on one side and see how much water seeps out on the other side overall. If the total seepage is within the standard range, the wall as a whole is intact
  • Very large wall (pressure decay method): You seal and pressurize a room, then calculate the leakage from how fast the pressure drops

Key Notes

SUS and pressure limits: Single-use systems (SUS) generally tolerate lower pressures than stainless steel systems. Bubble point tests often require higher pressures than diffusive flow tests, so the diffusive flow method is more commonly used in SUS environments. Forcing a bubble point test on a SUS may damage the tubing or connectors.

Special handling of multi-element assemblies: When several cartridges are installed in the same housing, the upper limit for diffusive flow cannot simply be the single-cartridge limit multiplied by the number of cartridges. An "assembly-specific" specification must be established, and it is usually lower than the linear multiple.

Practical Application

In a CDMO you may handle products for different clients at the same time. According to the guidance in Table 7.1.1-1🔍:

  • Small laboratory filters (< 0.03 m²): bubble point method only
  • Standard 10-inch cartridge (~0.5-0.7 m²): either bubble point or diffusive flow
  • Multi-cartridge assemblies (> 1.8 m²): primarily diffusive flow, supplemented by bubble point

When writing the SOP, confirm the specific area thresholds for each filter model with the filter supplier.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the diffusive flow method not suitable for small-area filters? Explain from the standpoint of instrument sensitivity.
  2. Why is the bubble point method not suitable for large-area filters? Explain from the standpoint of signal-to-noise ratio.
  3. If your process uses a SUS with a sterilizing-grade filter, what additional factors must you consider when selecting the integrity test method?
7.1.2 Filtrate Sterility Assurance

Original Text

Integrity-testing of sterilizing-grade production filters is a fundamental element of sterility assurance for the most critical applications that require a sterile claim (9, 11). However, integrity-testing alone is insufficient to ensure the sterility of the filtrate. Other control elements should also be in place, such as:

  • Filter manufacturer's production controls and quality assurance systems should be in place to ensure the quality and uniformity of the filter membranes and fabricated filters.
  • Filter user should conduct bacterial retention validation studies to demonstrate that product, processing conditions, and sterilizing-grade filter can effectively sterilize the fluid.
  • Filter user should ensure appropriate product and process controls (e.g., system design, operating parameters, bioburden control, process monitoring) are in place so that the filtration operates within the validated parameters defined by the bacterial retention study.

With the appropriate controls in place, the filter integrity test is only one part of the overall sterility-assurance strategy. It is incumbent on the filter user to ensure that all elements are, and continue to remain, in place.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Integrity testing is not the whole of sterility assurance: A complete sterility assurance strategy requires controls at three levels to be in place at the same time:

  1. Membrane manufacturer's quality control: Ensures the quality and consistency of every membrane lot
  2. User's validation studies: Demonstrate, with the specific product and process conditions, that the filter effectively removes microorganisms
  3. User's process controls: Ensure that actual operation stays within the validated parameter ranges

Analogy

Layered security at an airport: Integrity testing is like the airport security gate (metal detector). But airport security does not rely on the gate alone: it also needs baggage X-ray machines (bioburden control), passport checks (validation studies), and the airline's standard operating procedures (process controls). The absence of any one element endangers overall safety, even if the security gate works perfectly.

Key Notes

This again emphasizes sterile filtrate assurance as the highest-priority principle. A passing integrity test only shows that the filter itself is intact; if the bioburden exceeds the validated range, or operating parameters deviate from the validated conditions, the sterility of the filtrate can still be at risk.

1. Sterile Filtrate Assurance
▼
2. Product Quality / CQAs
▼
3. Process Robustness and Commercial Efficiency

Practice Questions

  1. List three scenarios in which the filtrate may still not be sterile even though the integrity test passes.
  2. As a CDMO operations head, how do you ensure that "all elements" of the sterility assurance strategy are continuously in place?
7.2 Automated Integrity-Test Instruments

Original Text

The standard for modern filter integrity-testing utilizes automated integrity-test units, which provide the most reproducible and sensitive methods. Automated integrity-testers employ highly sensitive pressure sensors and/or gas flow meters, in combination with the careful regulation of pressure, to test filters in a highly repeatable manner.

Historically, filter integrity-testing had been performed manually via a combination of manual manipulation of pressure regulators, graduated cylinders, and/or visual detection of increasing gas flow rate via a stream of bubbles. Some manual integrity-test methods require downstream manipulations that could compromise the sterility of the system if performed before use. Automated integrity-test instruments perform the integrity test from the upstream (nonsterile) side of the filters, minimizing the risk for downstream contamination. Manual integrity-testing should now only be performed on production filters in special and justified cases, due to the ready availability of automated integrity-test instruments.

Automatic integrity-test equipment, both hardware and software, should be qualified, as described in Section 7.3. Most automated integrity-test instruments function by measuring pressure only upstream of the filter membrane, and assuming downstream pressure is atmospheric. Care must be taken if the downstream side of the membrane is not in equilibrium with the atmosphere.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Automated Integrity-Test Instruments: The modern standard for sterilizing filtration is to perform integrity tests with automated instruments. These instruments use high-sensitivity pressure sensors and/or gas flow meters together with precise pressure regulation to deliver highly repeatable test results.

Upstream vs downstream testing: Automated instruments test from the upstream (non-sterile) side of the membrane, avoiding the risk of manual methods that require manipulation on the downstream (sterile) side. This is a key advantage of automated instruments: they do not break the established sterile condition.

Analogy

Manual integrity testing is like judging by eye and experience whether a tire is leaking: you submerge the tire in water and watch for bubbles. This method is limited by the observer's subjective judgment. An automated instrument is like the electronic tire pressure monitoring system in a car repair shop: accurate to the millibar level, with consistent and traceable results every time.

Key Notes

Limitations of manual testing: Manual integrity testing "should now be performed on production filters only in special and justified circumstances". This reflects the growing regulatory emphasis on data integrity and reproducibility. In an audit, if you are found still using manual methods without adequate justification, it may lead to an observation.

The downstream pressure assumption: Most automated instruments assume that the downstream pressure equals atmospheric pressure. If the downstream side is not equilibrated with the atmosphere (for example, a closed system), the test results may be inaccurate. This is a practical detail that is often overlooked.

Practice Questions

  1. Why do regulators favor automated integrity test instruments over manual methods? Explain from the perspectives of reproducibility and data integrity.
  2. Under what circumstances might the instrument's assumption that downstream pressure is atmospheric not hold? How would this affect the test results?
7.3 Qualification of Integrity Test Devices

Original Text

Qualification of integrity-test devices is similar to qualifying other process test-equipment. Qualification of an integrity-test device begins with the development of the device and is usually conducted by the device manufacturer. Design qualification and device development documentation are generally generated by the device manufacturer. These documents may be included in the filter user's qualification documentation package. Equipment qualification should cover the key functions of the device; however, it may not cover all possible combinations of functions and configurations. Risk assessment, therefore, should include key functions such as, but not limited to:

  • Test procedures as a main function of the device
  • Detection of error conditions and messages
  • Compliance with data integrity requirements and good documentation practices
  • Qualification of the measurement itself (accuracy and limits)

Typical qualification activities may include:

  • Computer software evaluation--test parameters, test methods, programming the unit, and tests (e.g., ease of use, test options, networking capability, operating systems)
  • Unit sensitivity evaluation
  • Unit startup
  • Unit calibration
  • Performing the tests
  • Integrity-test performance evaluation (bubble point, diffusive flow, pressure hold)
  • Test other functions (volume determination, failure modes, ejecting invalid entries)
  • Test printout evaluation
  • Password protection in combination with appropriate user roles and permission levels to avoid modification of a test program by an unauthorized user.
  • Peripheral function evaluation (date/time clock, memory, cleaning)

Integrity-test devices that measure pressure and/or gas flow should be calibrated using relevant calibration standards and should generate records according to applicable regulatory expectations.

Automated integrity-testers often incorporate sensitivity settings and test algorithms to detect atypical test conditions and to reliably test different filter sizes. These sensitivity settings are of importance and require careful observation to assure the right sensitivity for the particular filter-size is chosen, otherwise the integrity test may lack accuracy and validity. The sensitivity parameter establishment can be performed or confirmed during process qualification (PQ). For thorough qualification strategy, end users should consider confirming not only that the unit can successfully return a "pass" integrity result for integral filters but can also reliably detect broken filters or incorrect test setups.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Equipment Qualification: Qualification of integrity test instruments follows the same principles as other process test equipment: DQ (design qualification), IQ (installation qualification), OQ (operational qualification) and PQ (performance qualification). The equipment manufacturer is usually responsible for DQ and part of OQ, and the end user is responsible for confirming performance under actual conditions of use.

Sensitivity Settings: Automated instruments adjust their sensitivity to different filter sizes. Choosing the wrong sensitivity can lead to inaccurate test results: too sensitive produces false failures, while not sensitive enough may let a truly defective filter pass.

Analogy

Qualifying an integrity test instrument is like calibrating a thermometer. You must not only confirm that it reads correctly at normal body temperature (36.5°C; it can identify "healthy"), but also that it reads accurately at fever temperatures (38°C and above; it can detect "abnormal"). If you have only verified the "normal pass" scenario and not the "should fail" scenario, the qualification of the instrument is incomplete.

Key Notes

Data integrity is critical: Modern regulations place increasingly strict requirements on electronic records. Integrity test instruments must provide password protection, tiered user access rights, an audit trail, and protection against unauthorized modification of test programs. These are all key audit focus items.

Both "pass" and "fail" must be confirmed: A complete qualification strategy must confirm not only that the instrument lets an intact filter "pass", but also that it reliably detects a damaged filter or an incorrect test setup. This is something many sites tend to overlook.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must both "pass" and "fail" scenarios be verified during qualification of an integrity test instrument?
  2. What problems can arise if the sensitivity setting does not match the size of the filter actually used?
  3. From a data integrity perspective, list at least three functions an integrity test instrument must have.
7.4 Product Wet Integrity Tests

Original Text

The default "reference" wetting fluid for physical integrity tests of hydrophilic filters, as validated by membrane manufacturers, is typically water. However, for testing production filters, drug product or buffers may be the most appropriate wetting fluid (e.g., after use, when the filter is already wetted with this fluid). A new integrity-test specification, customized for the unique wetting fluid, can be determined during filter validation by executing a protocol in which the reference fluid specification is "converted" to an appropriate specification for the new wetting fluid. This specification is then, by extension, also correlated to the bacterial retention capabilities of the membrane.

Differences between product-wetted integrity test values and reference fluid-wetted integrity test values are due to differences in test gas solubility, the diffusion constant, and the surface tension of the wetting fluid.

The scale-down studies are used to determine the correction factor between the water-wet integrity-test specification and the required product-wet test limits.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Product-Wet vs Water-Wet: The integrity test specifications provided by the membrane manufacturer are usually based on water as the standard wetting fluid. In actual production, however, the filter has been wetted by the drug product solution after use. Because the surface tension, gas solubility and diffusion coefficient of the drug product differ from those of water, the integrity test values also differ. A "conversion" is therefore needed to establish product-wet specific specifications.

Three factors that cause the test values to differ:

  • Test gas solubility: The solubility of the gas in the product may differ from that in water
  • Diffusion constant: The rate at which the gas diffuses through the wetting fluid differs
  • Surface tension: Directly affects the bubble point pressure

Analogy

What the tea leaves release vs what you actually drink: Imagine brewing different teas in the same teapot: green tea and black tea release their color and flavor at different rates and to different depths. In the same way, a filter wetted with water and a filter wetted with the drug product behave differently in an integrity test. You cannot use the "green tea standard" to judge whether the "black tea" is properly brewed; you need to establish a separate standard for each tea (wetting fluid).

Key Notes

The importance of scale-down studies: The conversion factors are usually established in small-scale experiments using filter discs rather than full-size cartridges. This is because discs are easier to handle and dry, and the water-wet and product-wet results can be tested one after the other on the same membrane.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is product-wet testing more meaningful than water-wet testing in the production environment?
  2. If the surface tension of the drug product is lower than that of water, how would you expect the bubble point value to change? Why?
7.4.1 Product-Wetted Bubble Point Tests

Original Text

Tests for determination of product-wet bubble point limits should be conducted, first with water followed by product-wetting and then by the product-wet test. The technical rationale for establishment of product-wet bubble point values should be documented. Bubble point values are affected by the surface tension and temperature of the wetting fluid. Section 7.4.1.1 describes one method used to establish bubble point specifications using filter discs. Other methods may be appropriate if scientifically justified (see Section 10.2 for further discussion of bubble point tests).

7.4.1.1 Bubble Point Ratio Approach

For determining product-specific bubble point specifications, testing can generally be conducted as follows:

  1. Select multiple samples of filter discs for testing--generally at least three membrane lots. Since the bubble point refers to specific values relating to the membrane's pore size and the wetting-liquid surface tension, filter discs can be used.
  2. Install the membrane discs in the holder and rinse them with the reference wetting liquid (e.g., water, alcohol).
  3. Perform the bubble point test on each disc.
  4. Either completely dry the filter discs or rinse the membranes with an appropriate amount of the product in question to ensure complete removal of the wetting fluid used in the first integrity test.
  5. Perform the bubble point test with the product. These values are used to calculate the product-wet bubble point limit.

The bubble point limit for product can be calculated using the formula given in Equation 1. This example uses water as the reference fluid, which correlates the product-wetted-filter bubble point test data to the minimum water-wetted bubble point value given by the filter manufacturer.

Equation 1:
PBPmin = WBPmin x (PBPavg / WBPavg)

Where:
PBPmin = Product-wetted bubble point, minimum (minimum validated product-wetted bubble point)
WBPmin = Water-wetted bubble point, minimum (minimum water-wetted bubble point specified by the filter supplier)
PBPavg = Average product-wetted bubble point of the test samples recorded in the test
WBPavg = Average water-wetted bubble point of the test samples recorded in the test

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Bubble Point Ratio Approach: This is a standard method for "converting" a water-wet bubble point specification into a product-wet bubble point specification. The core logic is that there is a stable ratio between the product-wet bubble point and the water-wet bubble point (determined mainly by surface tension). This ratio can be determined in small-scale experiments and then applied to the minimum water-wet bubble point value provided by the manufacturer.

Formula

Equation 1: Product-Wetted Bubble Point Minimum

PBP_min = WBP_min x (PBP_avg / WBP_avg)

Worked example:
Assume:
  WBP_min (supplier specification) = 3200 mbar
  WBP_avg (measured water-wet average) = 3800 mbar
  PBP_avg (measured product-wet average) = 3040 mbar

Then: ratio = PBP_avg / WBP_avg = 3040 / 3800 = 0.80

PBP_min = 3200 x 0.80 = 2560 mbar

Meaning: the minimum product-wet bubble point specification is 2560 mbar

Analogy

This is like currency conversion. The membrane manufacturer gives you the "US dollar price" (the water-wet bubble point specification), but your plant operates in "euros" (product-wet). You need to find the exchange rate (the ratio) and convert the dollar price into a euro price. The exchange rate is calculated by measuring the "dollar" and "euro" values on the same lot of filter discs and working out the ratio.

Key Notes

At least three membrane lots: The test must use at least three different membrane lots to ensure that the ratio is statistically representative. Data from a single lot may not reflect normal lot-to-lot variability.

Complete removal of the previous wetting fluid: Before switching from water-wet to product-wet, you must ensure the water has been completely removed (dried or thoroughly flushed out). Residual water will affect the accuracy of the product-wet bubble point.

Practice Questions

  1. If the surface tension of the product is 80% that of water, approximately what ratio would you expect for PBP_avg / WBP_avg?
  2. Why does the test require at least three different membrane lots? What is the risk of using only one lot?
  3. Using the data from the example above, if your plant measures a product-wet bubble point of 2400 mbar, does this filter pass the test?
7.4.2 Product-Wetted Diffusive Flow Test

Original Text

The determination of product-wetted test specifications for diffusive flow is a two-step process, which requires first, identifying the bubble point ratio as described in Equation 1.

Step 1: Determination of Test Pressure

The test pressure for the product-wetted diffusive flow test is typically calculated using the following formula and the determined product wet bubble point values:

Equation 2:
TPPW = MTPWW x (PBPavg / WBPavg)

Where:
TPPW = Test pressure, product-wetted (varies with surface tension)
MTPWW = Manufacturer test pressure, water-wetted (or other test solvent) (see Section 10.2)
PBPavg = Average product-wetted bubble point of the test samples recorded in the test
WBPavg = Average water-wetted bubble point of the test samples recorded in the test

Step 2: Determination of the Product-Wetted Diffusive Flow Limit

The product-wetted diffusive-flow limit is then typically determined as follows:

  1. Conduct replicate water-wetted diffusive-flow tests on the filter assembly at the test pressure specified by the filter manufacturer (small-scale devices may be used as long as an accurate determination of flow is possible).
  2. Dry the previously water-wet tested filter assembly and conduct several replicates of the product-wetted diffusive-flow test at the appropriate test pressure determined in Step 1, Equation 2 above.
  3. Calculate the diffusive-flow test limit using the formula expressed in Equation 3:
Equation 3:
DFLPW = DFLWW x (DFPW / DFWW)

Where:
DFLPW = Diffusive flow limit, product-wetted
DFLWW = Diffusive-flow test limit specified by the supplier, water-wetted
DFPW = Diffusive flow, product-wetted
DFWW = Diffusive flow, water-wetted

Diffusion values are affected by the solubility of the test gas in the wetting fluid and its diffusion constant through the wetting fluid, therefore acceptance criteria are specific to a particular test gas being used.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The two-step conversion process: Establishing a product-wet diffusive flow specification is more complex than for the bubble point and requires two steps:

  1. Step 1 -- Convert the test pressure: Use the same ratio as for the bubble point to convert the water-wet test pressure to the product-wet test pressure
  2. Step 2 -- Convert the diffusive flow limit: At the new test pressure, compare the water-wet and product-wet diffusive flows and calculate the conversion factor

Formula

Step 1: Test pressure conversion (Equation 2)
TP_PW = MTP_WW x (PBP_avg / WBP_avg)

Example:
  MTP_WW = 2500 mbar (specified by supplier)
  ratio = 0.80 (calculated from Equation 1)
  TP_PW = 2500 x 0.80 = 2000 mbar

Step 2: Diffusive flow limit conversion (Equation 3)
DFL_PW = DFL_WW x (DF_PW / DF_WW)

Example:
  DFL_WW = 12 ml/min (supplier specification)
  DF_WW = 8 ml/min (measured water-wet)
  DF_PW = 10 ml/min (measured product-wet)
  DFL_PW = 12 x (10/8) = 15 ml/min

Key Notes

The effect of the test gas: Diffusive flow values are also affected by the type of test gas. Different gases have different solubilities and diffusion coefficients in the wetting fluid, so the acceptance criteria must be specific to the test gas used. In practice, compressed air and nitrogen are the most commonly used test gases, but their diffusion characteristics differ; when you switch gases, the specification must be re-established.

Why can product-wet diffusive flow be higher than water-wet? If the test gas has higher solubility or a larger diffusion coefficient in the product, the product-wet diffusive flow of the same intact filter will be higher than the water-wet value. Therefore, the product-wet limit (DFL_PW) is usually also higher than the water-wet limit (DFL_WW).

Practice Questions

  1. Why does the diffusive flow conversion require two steps, rather than being done in one step as for the bubble point?
  2. If you switch the test gas during validation (for example, from compressed air to nitrogen), do you need to re-establish the product-wet specification? Why?
7.5 When a Sterilizing-Grade Filter Should be Integrity-Tested

Original Text

Where the claimed purpose of the filter is to sterilize, post-use integrity-testing is mandatory. This confirms that the filter remained integral throughout its use and was capable of retaining microorganisms. If truly nonintegral filters are detected post-use, a thorough investigation to determine the root cause is required. Guidance on best practice is to leverage the supplier's or manufacturer's expertise. In most cases, it also results in discarding a drug batch (or reprocessing, where validated) to protect patient safety from exposure to a nonsterile product.

For filters marketed for critical sterilizing filtration, each filter element should also be integrity-tested by the filter manufacturer prior to release.

Integrity-testing can also be performed by the end user prior to use, either before the filter itself is sterilized (when sterilized by the end user) or after sterilization (Figure 7.5-1🔍). This allows early detection of nonintegral filters or improperly installed filters prior to execution of the filtration process. Whether to test filters in-line or off-line will depend on actual process requirements, but the test must include the cartridge already installed in the filter-housing (if applicable) so the seal between the filter element and the housing is also confirmed. Regulatory expectations regarding pre- and post-filtration integrity-testing may differ regionally (9, 11). In general, the objectives of pre- and post-filtration integrity-testing vary, and the need for each should be informed by risk assessment.

[Figure 7.5-1🔍 Filter Lifecycle -- Opportunities for Integrity-Testing]

Pre-use, pre-sterilization integrity tests performed by the end user would be redundant to the integrity test performed by the filter manufacturer to release the sterilizing-grade filter from their production. However, a presterilization test will additionally confirm that an integral filter of the proper pore size has been correctly installed in the housing, and that damage has not occurred during shipping and handling. A post-sterilization integrity test provides the same information as a pre-sterilization test plus confirmation that the filter was not damaged during sterilization. Steps must be taken to ensure that the downstream side of the system remains sterile when performing PUPSIT.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Test timing across the filter lifecycle:

  • Manufacturer's release test: Every sterilizing-grade filter undergoes an integrity test before it leaves the factory
  • User's pre-use, pre-sterilization test: Confirms correct installation and no transport damage (duplicates the manufacturer's release test)
  • User's pre-use, post-sterilization test (PUPSIT): Additionally confirms that the sterilization process did not damage the filter
  • User's post-use test (mandatory): Confirms that the filter remained integral throughout use

PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity test): A test performed after the filter has been sterilized and before the product is filtered. It must be ensured that the sterility of the downstream system is not compromised.

Analogy

Checking the gas before leaving home (reinforced again):

  • Release test = acceptance check after the gas company installs the equipment: Confirms the equipment left the factory in a conforming state
  • Pre-use test = sniffing it yourself: Confirms the pipes are fine after moving into a new home
  • PUPSIT = confirming it is shut off after each use of the gas: Confirms everything is fine after the most recent use
  • Post-use test = the final check after cooking: This is mandatory; you must confirm that the gas has been safely turned off

Key Notes

The post-use test is mandatory: If the filter's claimed purpose is sterilization, the post-use integrity test is "mandatory", with no exceptions. If the test fails, it usually means the whole batch must be discarded or reworked.

Regulatory differences: For pre-use tests (especially PUPSIT), regulatory expectations may differ by region. EU GMP Annex 1 has clearer expectations for PUPSIT, while the FDA's requirements are relatively flexible.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the post-use integrity test "mandatory" rather than "recommended"? Explain from a patient safety perspective.
  2. How does the pre-use, pre-sterilization test overlap with the manufacturer's release test? Why does it still have value?
  3. When performing PUPSIT, what specific measures are needed to "ensure that the sterility of the downstream system is not compromised"?
7.5.1 Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Test (PUPSIT) Considerations

Original Text

The post-use test is designed to detect any filter damage that occurred throughout its lifecycle, up to and including the filtration process itself. However, under extreme circumstances, post-filtration integrity test results may be influenced (i.e., shifted) by high-foulant fluids, which may mask a filter flaw or larger pore structure (44-47). In this scenario a PUPSIT result could identify filter flaws or filter damage when a post-use integrity may not. Recent publications have shown that these conditions are atypical for terminal filtration steps as filter-fouling is typically avoided within these filtration applications. Filterability studies, performed to determine the appropriate filter size and area before any filter design is established, will indicate the fluid-fouling propensity and filter flaw-masking risk, and gauge potential preventive measurements against any fouling (47).

In filtration applications with significant pore-plugging or large-pore structure-blockage potential, it is advisable to know the filter's prefiltration integrity-test value prior to the influences of contact with the full batch of product (or more strictly, the potential of foulants to shift the measured post-use integrity-test results in the direction that might cause false passes.) Any excessive fouling conditions would be also evidenced by significant flux decay or required pressure increase to maintain flow. True post-use integrity-test failures are typically a result of damages due to extreme mechanical or thermal process conditions. Such damages are typically catastrophic, and flaw-masking would not be possible for catastrophic or gross damage to filter membranes, as this damage is not capable of being "masked" by components of the fluid to an extent that allows a post-use test to appear to pass (48). However, in cases where significant fouling of the filter is possible, it could potentially mask rare marginal membrane flaws, which may not be detected by the post-use integrity test. Excessive filter-fouling could also be detected by special customizations to the post-use test, as the decrease in porosity and blockage of the largest pores would be manifested in a decrease in diffusive flow and in an increase in the bubble point, respectively (49). Alternative means also exist to detect any fouling that has the potential to modify post-use test value, including flow and differential-pressure monitoring during filtration, and possible modifications to the post-use test to detect unexpectedly low diffusive-flow values or unexpectedly high bubble points. Further, any risk of such flaw-masking (due to product-specific mechanisms of shifting the integrity-test results after fouling) can be evaluated during process design. Decades of experiences of filter suppliers and end-users showed that this type of behavior is scarce and evaluated during filterability tests respectively filter area sizing and process validation.

Any potential risks involved in the sterilizing filtration activity must be thoroughly assessed (28, 50, 51). While PUPSIT is a common regulatory expectation, there may be process constraints where it is not feasible (11). In such cases, and in relevant jurisdictions, a thorough risk assessment is required not to justify omission, but to evaluate and manage the risks associated with not performing PUPSIT. This includes assessing the filter sterilization process, supply chain controls, packaging, product characteristics, and pre-filtration steps. The goal of this assessment is to ensure that adequate controls are in place to mitigate the risk of using any non-integral filters.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Flaw-Masking: This is the core of the PUPSIT debate. In some extreme cases, high-foulant fluids may "mask" filter flaws in the post-use test: clogged pores reduce diffusive flow and raise the bubble point, making a defective filter appear to pass the test. PUPSIT can detect these flaws before product contact.

But this is very rare: The document clearly states that decades of experience show this behavior "is scarce". In addition, catastrophic damage (such as severe mechanical or thermal damage) cannot be "masked" by product components to the point that the post-use test passes.

Analogy

Imagine you are checking a water pipe for cracks. If the pipe has a lot of scale (fouling), the scale may plug small cracks and make a leak test look normal. This is flaw-masking. PUPSIT is like testing before the scale forms, to confirm that the pipe itself is intact. In practice, though, if the pipe has a large breach (catastrophic damage), scale cannot plug it; only tiny marginal flaws could be masked.

Key Notes

Risk assessment when PUPSIT is not feasible: The document stresses that the risk assessment needed when PUPSIT is not performed is "not to justify omission", but to "assess and manage the associated risks". This wording is very important: regulators do not accept "we did not do it because it is inconvenient"; they require you to demonstrate that sufficient alternative controls are in place to reduce the risk.

Alternative ways to detect clogging: Monitoring flow rate and differential pressure during filtration, and special tailoring of the post-use test (detecting abnormally low diffusive flow or an abnormally high bubble point), are both means of detecting clogging.

Practical Application

As a CDMO, you may face situations where a client or regulator requires PUPSIT. The evaluation should consider:

  • The fouling tendency of the product (assessed through filterability studies)
  • Whether the SUS can withstand the pressure required for PUPSIT
  • The feasibility of maintaining downstream sterility
  • Whether sufficient alternative controls exist (such as redundant filtration and bioburden monitoring)

If you decide not to perform PUPSIT, the risk assessment document must be reviewed by the quality unit and be available at any time during regulatory inspections.

Practice Questions

  1. What is flaw-masking? In what types of products is it most likely to occur?
  2. Why can catastrophic filter damage not be masked by flaw-masking?
  3. If you decide not to perform PUPSIT, what elements must the risk assessment include?
7.5.2 Post-Filtration Integrity-Test Considerations

Original Text

For operational reasons, the post-filtration integrity test should be accomplished as soon as feasible to avoid any detrimental test influences, for example, filter membrane wetting problems. Product residues should not dry out on the membrane and should be removed, if possible (by sufficiently flushing the filter with an appropriate fluid, post-use), if not testing in product. Filters should not be discarded until the integrity-test results are reviewed. Storage conditions and duration should be qualified to ensure they do not adversely affect the integrity of the filter. In cases of abnormally long delay between the use of the filter and its integrity test, the possibility of false passes should be evaluated.

The post-use test is not only a test of membrane integrity, but of the junction between the filter element and its housing or capsule. The filter element to be tested should therefore remain within the filter-housing to ensure that the filter and its junction with the housing has been integral throughout the filtration process. The post-use integrity test could be performed in-line or off-line within specific integrity-test laboratories within the end user's facility. The post-use test can be performed as a product-wet or water-wet procedure. Confirmation of integrity must be obtained before any product batch is released; therefore, the filter must not be discarded before the test result is obtained. If the filter receives an apparently failing integrity-test result, specific activities for retesting or root cause analysis are required and should be documented in the relevant procedure or batch record.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Practical requirements for the post-use test:

  • Perform it as soon as possible: Avoid product residue drying on the membrane and affecting wetting and test results
  • Do not discard the filter: The filter must be retained until the test result is confirmed; this is a prerequisite for batch release
  • Keep it in the housing: The test covers not only the membrane itself but also the seal between the membrane and the housing
  • Storage conditions must be confirmed: If there is a delay between use and testing, the storage conditions and time must be validated

Analogy

A post-use integrity test is like crime scene forensics: it must be completed at the "scene" as soon as possible (do not let the evidence be spoiled), and the "physical evidence" (the filter) must be kept (do not discard it) until the "case is closed" (batch release). If you remove the filter from its housing and then test it, it is like moving the evidence from the crime scene before analyzing it: you cannot confirm its state in its original position.

Key Notes

The threshold for batch release: "Confirmation of integrity must be obtained prior to release of any product batch". This is a clear regulatory expectation. If the integrity test result is a failure or is doubtful, the product batch cannot be released and an investigation must be conducted.

The risk of delayed testing: If the delay between use and testing is unusually long, the possibility of a "false pass" must be evaluated. Dried product residue can change the wetting characteristics of the membrane and lead to inaccurate test results.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should the post-use test be performed "as soon as possible"? What problems can a delayed test cause?
  2. Why must the filter remain in its housing before testing? What information would be missed if it were removed?
7.5.3 Integrity Testing for Serial or Redundant Filtration

Original Text

Manufacturing processes may be designed with trains of multiple filters, but the purpose of these extra filters varies from process to process (see Section 5.0). This purpose determines the need for integrity-testing of each filter. If one filter has been validated to achieve sterilization with a specific product, then the single sterilizing filter must satisfactorily pass integrity-testing after use.

7.5.3.1 Two-Filter Sterilization (Double Filtration)

In double filtration, the filter train is considered to be a sterilizing unit, and all sterilizing filters within the train must satisfactorily pass integrity-testing after use.

7.5.3.2 Pre-Filtration

For prefiltration, requirements for integrity-testing must be determined by the end user in an overall assessment during process design. Depending on the purpose of the filter, integrity-testing (correlated to complete bacterial retention) may not always be necessary.

7.5.3.3 Redundant Filtration

The primary filter (generally the farthest downstream) must be integrity-tested post-use. The secondary (redundant) sterilizing filter may not require post-use integrity-testing unless the primary sterilizing filter fails. In that case, the redundant filter must satisfactorily pass post-use integrity-testing and the reason for the failure of the primary filter should be investigated including confirmation of the sterile boundary. If the sterilizing-filtration process is redundant and registered as having PUPSIT, both filters should undergo PUPSIT for them to be considered comparable.

Performing PUPSIT on both filters in a redundant filtration configuration can be quite complex. Each filter must be tested individually after sufficient wetting; a single test cannot typically be used to test both filters simultaneously. Precautions should be taken to maintain the sterility of the fluid pathway between the two filters, as that region becomes critical for product sterility should the downstream filter fail, and the upstream filter is relied upon for sterility. Controls can include the use of additional sterilizing filters to vent the integrity-test gas from the upstream filter and introduce the test gas for integrity-testing the downstream filter. To test the downstream filter, there must be a valve or other closure between it and the upstream filter. In this type of setup, the integrity-test process-method development should include a risk assessment to ensure that the effects of wetting, or nonintegrity of the product and vent filters, does not interfere with the filter integrity-test result (52, 53). Further details on these considerations were discussed in PDA's Points to Consider for Implementation of Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Testing (PUPSIT).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Test requirements for the three multi-filter configurations:

  • Double Filtration: Both filters are used for sterilization, and both must pass the post-use test
  • Pre-Filtration: Not a sterilizing purpose; integrity testing is not necessarily required (depending on the intended use)
  • Redundant Filtration: The primary filter (downstream) must be tested; the redundant filter (upstream) must be tested only if the primary filter fails

Analogy

Double seat belts: Redundant filtration is like the spare engine on an aircraft. The primary engine is checked after every flight. The spare engine needs a full inspection only if the primary engine has a problem, but once it has to be brought into service, you must confirm that it is intact. If your system is registered as "with PUPSIT", it is like committing to inspect both engines before every takeoff.

Key Notes

The complexity of PUPSIT in redundant configurations: Performing PUPSIT in a redundant filtration system is extremely complex: each filter must be tested separately, sterility must be maintained between the two filters, and an additional sterilizing-grade vent filter must be used. This is a practical challenge that many sites must consider carefully when assessing the feasibility of PUPSIT.

The sterile zone between the two filters: If the downstream primary filter fails, the fluid path between the two filters becomes critical to the sterility of the product. The sterile condition of this zone must be protected throughout the entire PUPSIT process.

Practice Questions

  1. In double filtration, why must both filters pass the post-use test?
  2. In redundant filtration, if the primary filter passes the test but the redundant filter is not tested, can the batch be released?
  3. When performing PUPSIT on a redundant configuration, why is an additional sterilizing-grade vent filter needed?
7.6 Failure Analysis/Troubleshooting

Original Text

If a sterilizing filter fails an integrity test, it could be damaged, but there may be other causes for the failure that include incorrect assembly (incomplete sealing) and incomplete wetting (see Section 7.7.1). Due to the physical mechanisms of filter integrity-testing, "false failures" may occur, and controlled retesting with defined actions may be permitted. When procedures are followed and appropriately justified, an ultimate passing result can be treated as a valid result even if initial tests resulted in reported failures. Filter-failure investigations and retest procedures should be documented.

To distinguish between filter damage and possible wetting or other problems, the following verification steps may be taken to determine that the:

  • Appropriate integrity test has been selected
  • Correct test parameters are being used
  • Correct wetting fluid and wetting procedure are being used
  • No leaks in the test system
  • Filter assembly temperature has remained stable and within specification during testing (e.g., adiabatic effect*) (54)
  • Equipment has been properly calibrated (55)
  • Test setup is properly assembled and functions properly
  • Correct filter has been installed

*Note: Adiabatic effects can influence the test result if temperature is not held constant. To overcome this, extended stabilization and test times may be required for these systems.

After verifying the above, troubleshooting steps (including re-wetting and re-testing) can be performed to rule out possible false failure causes. Example steps in a troubleshooting procedure include:

  • Rewet the filter according to the specifications and repeat the test (see Figure 7.6-1🔍, Step 1).

If the filter integrity-test fails again, the following step may be employed:

  • Apply more aggressive wetting conditions by increasing the flush volume/time, increasing the pressure differential, and/or applying back pressure (see Figure 7.6-1🔍, Step 2).

If the filter-integrity test fails again, the following steps may be employed:

  • Perform the integrity test in a lower surface-tension reference fluid to assess filter wettability changes independent of filter integrity (see Figure 7.6-1🔍, Step 3).
  • If the filter fails using the reference fluid, the filter fails the test.

If the filter passes the integrity test at any point during this failure analysis, it can be considered integral and capable of yielding a sterile effluent. Figure 7.6-1🔍 provides an example of a decision tree that may be used to evaluate integrity-test failures. The total number of test repetitions allowed should be defined and documented, and all actions should be proceduralized. When the total number of test repetitions allowed is exceeded, investigations should be initiated and documented.

[Figure 7.6-1🔍 Example Integrity-Test Failure Analysis Decision Tree]

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

False Failures: Because of the physical mechanisms of integrity testing, "false failures" can occur, where the filter is intact but the test result shows a failure. The most common cause is incomplete wetting. TR26 allows retesting provided it is done under a procedure and documented.

The three-step troubleshooting process:

  1. Step 1: Rewet according to the specification and retest
  2. Step 2: Intensify the wetting conditions (increase flush volume/time, increase differential pressure, apply back pressure) and retest
  3. Step 3: Test with a low surface tension reference fluid (to rule out wettability problems); if the filter still fails, it is confirmed as non-conforming

Analogy

Imagine the buttons on your TV remote do not respond. You would not immediately conclude that the TV is broken:

  • Step 1: First try new batteries (rewet and retest)
