  • Step 2: If it still does not work after changing the batteries, try pressing harder and adjusting the angle (intensify the wetting conditions)
  • Step 3: Try another remote on the TV (test with a reference fluid); if the other remote does not work either, the TV really has a problem (the filter is confirmed non-conforming)

Key Notes

Adiabatic Effect: When gas expands or is compressed in a closed system, its temperature changes. This temperature change affects the test results. The solution is to extend the stabilization time and test time so that the system reaches thermal equilibrium. Large systems (with a large upstream volume) are especially susceptible.

The number of retests must be predefined: The number of permitted retests must be clearly defined and documented in the procedure in advance. You cannot retest indefinitely until the filter passes; this would raise regulatory concerns about "testing into compliance".

Practical Application

In day-to-day CDMO operations, integrity test failure is a common deviation event. It is essential to establish a clear troubleshooting SOP:

  • Clearly define the maximum number of permitted retests (for example, up to 2 rewet retests and 1 intensified-wetting retest)
  • Record the reason and result of every retest
  • Automatically trigger a deviation investigation when the retest limit is exceeded
  • Make sure operators are trained to know when to stop retesting and escalate

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR26 allow retesting after an integrity test failure? How is this different from "testing into compliance"?
  2. In what types of systems is the adiabatic effect most significant? How can its impact be mitigated?
  3. If the reference fluid test passes in Step 3 but the product-wet test still fails, what problem does this indicate?
7.6.1 Insufficient Wetting Failure Analysis

Original Text

Filter integrity-test failures are often caused by insufficient wetting of the filter membrane matrix. Incomplete wetting may be due to either inadequate flushing to wet out all pores, adsorption of hydrophobic contaminants, or other formulation components that can change the surface-wetting characteristics of the filter membrane. A change in the wetting characteristics may affect integrity-test performance. For example, leachables from upstream materials may be adsorbed into the filter membrane, impacting its wetting properties and resulting in an integrity-test failure (56).

To obtain an appropriate integrity-test result, the filter's porous structure must be completely wetted, as the physics of integrity-test measurement rely on either gas flow through the wetting liquid layer or displacement of the wetting fluid from the largest structure. Wetting of the filter membrane can be influenced by:

  • Membrane polymer: Some polymers are easier to wet than others, depending on the critical surface tension of the membrane material
  • Pore size: The smaller the pore size, the more difficult the pores are to wet
  • Wetting fluid: Some wetting fluids may interact unfavorably with the polymeric matrix
  • Product residues or contaminants: Product residues or contaminants can alter the hydrophilicity of the membrane polymer and repel the wetting fluid or lower the surface tension
  • Pressure conditions: Manufacturer's pressure recommendations should be followed to completely wet the membrane
  • Temperature conditions: Temperature influences the surface tension of the wetting fluid

In addition to the above, there may be process- and application-specific factors that could affect integrity tests that are not covered by the manufacturer's description of use. The troubleshooting steps described in Section 7.6 and the decision tree provided in Figure 7.6-1🔍 may be useful to evaluate integrity-test failures. Additional troubleshooting information is provided in Section 10.3.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Insufficient Wetting: This is the most common cause of integrity test failure. The physical principles of integrity testing rest on the premise that the membrane is completely wetted by the liquid. If any pores are not wetted, gas can pass freely through these "dry" pores, producing abnormally high flow readings and causing the test to fail.

Six factors that affect wetting:

  • Membrane Polymer: PES (polyethersulfone) wets more easily than PVDF (polyvinylidene fluoride)
  • Pore Size: 0.1 µm is harder to wet than 0.2 µm
  • Wetting Fluid: Some solutions may be incompatible with the membrane polymer
  • Product residues/contaminants: May change the hydrophilicity of the membrane
  • Pressure conditions: Wetting must be performed at the pressure recommended by the manufacturer
  • Temperature conditions: Temperature affects the surface tension of the wetting fluid

Analogy

Imagine wetting a sponge with water. If the sponge surface has a layer of oil (a hydrophobic contaminant), water has difficulty penetrating. Even if you soak most of the sponge, the oily spots stay dry, and these "dry" areas make a water test look as if the sponge has holes. In the same way, if leachables from upstream equipment are adsorbed by the filter, they may change the hydrophilicity of the membrane surface and lead to incomplete wetting.

Key Notes

The unexpected effect of leachables: Leachables released from upstream materials (such as silicone tubing and gaskets) may be adsorbed by the filter and change its wetting characteristics. This is an easily overlooked root cause of failure: if your plant changes upstream tubing materials, integrity test failures may suddenly start to appear.

The smaller the pore size, the harder to wet: This is important practical knowledge. A 0.1 µm sterilizing-grade filter is harder to wet completely than a 0.2 µm one and may require a higher wetting pressure and a longer wetting time. If your process uses a 0.1 µm filter, validation of the wetting procedure is especially important.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does insufficient wetting cause the integrity test to "fail" rather than "pass"? Explain from the physical mechanism.
  2. If your plant starts to see frequent integrity test failures after upstream tubing is changed, which directions would you investigate?
  3. List at least three ways to improve filter wetting.

Section Summary

  • The nature of integrity testing: A non-destructive physical test that serves as a surrogate method for the bacterial retention test, using the principles of gas diffusion through a wetted membrane and bubble point to determine filter integrity
  • Test method selection: Depends on membrane area: bubble point for small areas, either method for medium areas, and diffusive flow for large areas. In SUS environments, give priority to the diffusive flow method to avoid overpressure
  • Integrity testing is only one part of sterility assurance: It must be combined with manufacturer quality control, bacterial retention validation, process controls and other elements to form a complete sterility assurance strategy
  • Establishing product-wet specifications: The Bubble Point Ratio Approach and the diffusive flow conversion equations (Equations 1-3) are needed to convert the standard water-wet specifications into product-specific product-wet specifications
  • The post-use test is mandatory: Whenever a filter's claimed purpose is sterilization, a post-use integrity test is required. Whether to perform PUPSIT depends on risk assessment and regulatory requirements
  • Troubleshooting follows a three-step process: rewet → intensify wetting → reference fluid test. Insufficient wetting is the most common cause of failure and is influenced by membrane polymer, pore size, wetting fluid, contaminants, pressure and temperature
  • Equipment qualification and data integrity: Automated integrity test instruments are the modern standard; they must undergo full equipment qualification (including "pass" and "fail" scenarios) and meet data integrity requirements
  • Decision hierarchy: In all decisions related to integrity testing, sterile filtrate assurance is always the highest priority, above process convenience and commercial efficiency
8

Section 8: Sterilization

p71-p73

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p71 - p73

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the critical role of filter sterilization in sterilizing filtration, and how a sterilization failure affects sterile filtrate assurance
  • Master the principles, scope of application and operating precautions of the three sterilization methods: steam, irradiation and gas
  • Learn to distinguish the operational differences and the respective critical control points of autoclave and SIP (sterilize-in-place) sterilization
  • Understand the restrictions and validation requirements for resterilization, and the risk assessment considerations for filter reuse
8.0 Sterilization of Filters (General)

Original Text

A key element of a successful sterilizing filtration process is the sterility of the filtration assembly, especially on the filtrate side. Proper sterilization of filters involves applying the right physicochemical conditions, and with careful attention, this process can be highly effective while preserving the integrity of the filter membranes and components. By following the manufacturer's recommendations for sterilization, the risk of thermal, mechanical, or chemical damage to the filters can be minimized. The sterilization process should be appropriately qualified to ensure that there is no variation during the cycle and that it achieves the respective sterility goal and does not cause harm to the filtration device. In instances when the sterilization process is not automated, a routine integrity test of the filter after sterilization may be performed. An integrity test after the sterilization process can confirm that the filter was not damaged during sterilization.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Filtration Assembly Sterility: Sterilizing filtration requires not only that the filter membrane itself can retain bacteria, but also that the entire filtration assembly (housing, tubing and connections) is sterile. The filtrate side is especially emphasized, because it is the "clean side" where the product has already passed through the sterilizing-grade filter, and any contamination there directly affects the sterility assurance of the final product.

Sterility Goal: The level of microbial kill that the sterilization process must achieve. For steam sterilization, this is normally a 10-6 SAL (Sterility Assurance Level), meaning only one non-sterile unit is permitted per one million sterilized units.

Analogy

Imagine that before surgery you must disinfect not only the scalpel, but also the whole operating table, the instrument tray and every item that touches the wound. Sterilizing filtration works the same way: a sterile membrane alone is not enough, and the entire "filtrate-side flow path" must be sterilized, just like the sterile field in an operating room. If you disinfect only the scalpel and forget the instrument tray, the patient can still get an infection.

Key Notes

This section opens by pointing out a core tension: sterilization must be thorough and effective, yet it must not damage the filter. This is why the manufacturer's recommendations must be followed: the combination of temperature, pressure and time must balance "killing microorganisms" against "protecting the membrane material".

In addition, when the sterilization process is not automated, TR26 recommends performing an integrity test after sterilization. This echoes the PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity test, as discussed in Section 7.0) concept: the sterilization process itself can damage the membrane, and the integrity test is the last line of defense for confirming whether damage has occurred.

Decision Hierarchy

1. Sterile Filtrate Assurance
▼
2. Product quality / CQAs
▼
3. Process robustness & commercial efficiency

Filter sterilization directly affects the first tier: if the filtration assembly is not properly sterilized, there can be no sterile filtrate assurance. This is why the sterilization procedure must be properly qualified rather than relying on operator experience alone.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR26 specifically emphasize the sterility of the "filtrate side" rather than of the entire assembly? Think about it from the perspective of product flow.
  2. If the sterilization process is fully automated, is a post-sterilization integrity test still required? Why?
  3. Should the manufacturer's recommended sterilization parameters or user-developed parameters be the starting point? Why?
8.1 Steam Sterilization

Original Text

Steam under pressure is widely recognized as one of the most effective sterilization methods, typically carried out in an autoclave or in situ as SIP. Steam sterilization cycles are required to be properly validated and performed to ensure the entire filter and membrane matrix is sterilized. The steam sterilization cycles are often automated and well controlled within the end-user site to assure maintaining the validated state. Validation studies typically include integrity-testing the filters post-sterilization and the use of biological indicators (BI). BI's may be placed in the filter core or embedded into the pleat packs. These studies play a critical role in confirming that the cycle achieves a 10-6 sterility assurance level and that the filters are integral.

Steam sterilization parameters should be considered carefully when designing a sterilization process for any filter. Refer to the filter manufacturer's literature for additional information regarding temperature and pressure limitations together with the maximum allowable sterilization cycles.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Steam Sterilization: A method that uses pressurized saturated steam (typically above 121°C) to kill microorganisms; it is the most widely used sterilization method in the pharmaceutical industry. It is carried out in two ways:

  • Autoclave: Items are placed in a closed chamber for sterilization; suitable for detachable filter assemblies
  • SIP (Sterilize-in-Place): Steam is introduced directly into the installed piping system; suitable for fixed stainless steel housing systems

Biological Indicator (BI): A carrier containing a known number of highly heat-resistant spores, used to verify that the sterilization conditions are sufficient to kill microorganisms. A BI can be placed inside the filter core or within the pleated media to confirm that steam actually penetrates to the most difficult-to-reach location.

10-6 SAL (Sterility Assurance Level): The probability of no more than one viable microorganism surviving per one million sterilized units. This is the standard requirement of pharmacopoeias and regulations for terminally sterilized products.

Analogy

Steam sterilization is like disinfecting a baby bottle in a pressure steamer: plain hot water is not enough, and it must be pressurized saturated steam to make sure every corner reaches the lethal temperature. A BI is like placing a "temperature test strip" in the innermost corner that steam reaches least easily: if the microorganisms there are killed, you can be confident that everywhere else is fine too.

Key Notes

TR26 stresses that the validation study must confirm both of the following:

  • Sterilization effectiveness: Confirm through BIs that a 10-6 SAL is achieved
  • Filter integrity: Confirm through a post-sterilization integrity test that the membrane has not been damaged

Neither can be omitted. Even if the BI proves that sterilization succeeded, if the membrane is damaged by steam pressure or temperature and loses its integrity, the whole sterilizing filtration process is still invalid.

Practice Questions

  1. Why should the BI be placed in the "filter core" or "within the pleated media" rather than on the outside of the housing or on the tubing?
  2. What type of filtration system is each of autoclave and SIP suited to? Which is more common in a multi-product CDMO facility?
8.1.1 Autoclave Sterilization

Original Text

Most sterilizing filters are qualified for steam sterilization by their manufacturers to at least 121°C and, in some cases, up to 130 – 134°C. Temperatures higher than this may make many of the plastics used for filter construction unstable and may affect the physical integrity of the filter or increase the level of extractables.

Filter assembly preparation for autoclave sterilization is critical. Sterilized filter components should be protected with a suitable microbial barrier to avoid recontamination after sterilization; however, the barrier must allow air to pass from the filter and steam enter the filter during sterilization. It is critical to ensure that filter inlets and outlets are left open to avoid excessive differential pressures during sterilization. Wrapped articles should be covered with only enough wrapping material to protect critical surfaces while allowing free transfer of saturated steam and air through the material. Use of sterilization tape should be minimal. The weight of any attached fittings and hoses should be supported to avoid potentially deforming of capsule filter inlet and/or outlet at high temperatures. Hoses should not be kinked or closed to allow the free flow of steam and air. The filter user should refer to the manufacturer's specifications when developing an autoclave sterilization cycle for the filter assembly. Slow exhaust (e.g., liquid) cycles may be considered for filter sterilization to avoid excessive pressure differentials across the filter during the cool-down phase of the cycle. Filters should be removed promptly after the sterilization cycle to avoid potential oxidative damage from heated air in the chamber.

One of the major difficulties encountered in autoclave sterilization is air removal. Air can be effectively removed at the initiation of the sterilization cycle by applying a series of vacuum and steam purge cycles. Large filter assemblies may require adjustments to the sterilization parameters, especially the prevacuum phase, to optimize air removal and steam penetration (57). During the sterilization cycle, the filter assembly should be oriented to allow condensates to drain. If the filter is integrity-tested immediately post-sterilization, it does not have to be dry.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Extractables: Chemicals extracted from the filter material under extreme conditions (such as high-temperature sterilization). The higher the temperature and the longer the time, the more extractables. These substances may affect product quality, so the sterilization temperature should not be too high.

Microbial Barrier: The protective layer used to wrap the filter after sterilization; it must meet the dual requirement of "blocking microorganisms while allowing air and steam to pass". Common materials include sterilization wrap paper or breathable sterilization pouches.

Differential Pressure: The pressure difference between the upstream and downstream sides of the membrane. During high-temperature sterilization, if the inlet and outlet are sealed, the internal pressure cannot equalize, which may cause structural damage or even rupture of the membrane.

Air Removal: The core challenge of autoclave sterilization. Air is the biggest barrier to steam penetration: if residual air forms "air pockets", those areas cannot reach the sterilization temperature and become "cold spots".

Analogy

The air removal problem in autoclave sterilization is like steaming buns in a rice cooker: if the cold air in the steamer is not fully purged, the buns look "done" on the outside but are still raw inside. Steam must completely displace the air to transfer heat evenly. That is why an autoclave uses "alternating vacuum and steam purge cycles", repeatedly drawing a vacuum and then admitting steam, much like repeatedly lifting the steamer lid to let the cold air out.

Key Notes

This subsection lists a number of practical autoclave operating details, each of which is a common source of deviations:

  • Inlet and outlet must remain open: Closed lines create a pressure differential and may tear the membrane during the cool-down phase
  • Use an appropriate amount of wrapping material: Too many layers hinder steam penetration, but too few cannot maintain sterility after sterilization
  • Connectors and hoses need support: Plastics soften at high temperature, and gravity may deform the inlet and outlet of capsule filters
  • Remove promptly after sterilization: Prolonged exposure to hot, dry air causes oxidative damage
  • Consider a slow exhaust cycle: Avoids the impact of rapid pressure changes on the membrane during the cool-down phase

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your facility uses both 10" and 30" capsule filters. Can both sizes be sterilized in the same autoclave load?

The answer is: with great caution. The air removal and steam penetration requirements of a large 30" filter differ from those of a small 10" filter. If the same prevacuum parameters are used, cold air may remain inside the large filter, resulting in incomplete sterilization. It is advisable to validate the sterilization parameters separately for each filter size, or at least to validate using the "maximum load / most difficult-to-sterilize configuration" as the worst case.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does TR26 recommend that the sterilization temperature not exceed the manufacturer's upper limit (typically 130-134°C)? What risks does exceeding it bring?
  2. "Keeping the inlet and outlet open" and "protecting the microbial barrier" seem contradictory. In practice, how can both requirements be met at the same time?
  3. If an integrity test is performed immediately after autoclave sterilization, the membrane does not need to be dried. Why? (Hint: think about the wetting requirement of the integrity test.)
  4. Why do large filtration assemblies need a specially adjusted prevacuum phase? How is this related to "cold spots"?
8.1.2 Sterilize-in-Place (SIP)

Original Text

The SIP method is limited to filters in housings that can withstand steam pressures of 15–30 psig (1–2 bar). Disposable filter capsules with polypropylene or polyester housings should not be subjected to in-situ steam under pressure, except for high-temperature polymers, such as polyetherimide.

To ensure optimal performance during the sterilization process, it is important to monitor pressure differentials by installing pressure gauges both upstream and downstream of the filter (58). Adhering to the differential pressure limits set by the filter manufacturer for each specific housing or assembly is critical, especially at elevated temperatures. High differential pressure may occur due to restricted drainage or blocked steam flow but, with proper practices, this can be effectively avoided.

To protect wetted filters, steam should be introduced gently in forward flow at low pressure. This approach allows the filters to heat up gradually, ensuring that any liquid in the membrane matrix is evaporated before steam flow begins. An effective way to facilitate this is by opening the upstream vent and housing drain before the steaming process. This allows steam to efficiently heat the filter, ensuring a smooth transition to sterilization. Once downstream pressure increase is observed, upstream steam pressure can be increased to target the sterilizing pressure by partially closing the vent and drain valves. Alternatively, the system can be designed to introduce steam from both sides of the filter to avoid excessive steam-pressure differentials.

During the cool-down phase of an SIP process, it is critical that air or other suitable gas maintain a positive pressure across the filter. Excessive reverse pressure differential can arise if the upstream side of the filter is cooled more rapidly than the filter core. Gas-assisted cooling is preferred over liquid cooling, because the latter may generate thermal shock and damage the filter (58, 59).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

SIP (Sterilize-in-Place): Steam is introduced directly into a filter housing that is installed on the process line, so sterilization can be performed without disassembly. It is typically used for fixed filtration systems with stainless steel housings.

Forward Flow: Steam is introduced in the normal process direction (from upstream to downstream). This is key to protecting a wetted membrane: it lets residual liquid on the membrane evaporate before the steam pressure rises.

Reverse Pressure Differential: Occurs when the downstream pressure is higher than the upstream pressure. During cool-down, if the upstream side cools first (steam condensation creates a vacuum) while the filter core is still at a higher pressure, a reverse pressure differential forms and may tear the membrane in the reverse direction.

Thermal Shock: A sudden change in temperature (for example, liquid cooling water contacting a hot filter) may cause structural damage to the material. Gas-assisted cooling is therefore recommended so that the temperature falls slowly.

Analogy

Operating SIP is like maintaining the heating pipes at home: you cannot pour scalding steam straight into cold pipes (it would shatter them, like putting a cold glass into boiling water), so you must heat up slowly. Likewise, you cannot flush with ice water during cooling. It must be allowed to cool down like a stove after the flame is turned off (or be blown cool with a fan, which is gas-assisted cooling), rather than being doused with water.

Key Notes

SIP has several key operating points that are easily overlooked:

  • Material limitations: Single-use capsule filters with polypropylene (PP) and polyester (PET) housings cannot undergo SIP, unless they are made of a special high-temperature polymer such as polyetherimide (PEI)
  • Pressure monitoring: Pressure gauges must be installed both upstream and downstream, because differential pressure is the primary risk of membrane damage during SIP
  • Slow pressurization: Steam must be introduced slowly in the forward direction at low pressure to first drive out residual liquid from the membrane
  • Steam from both sides: Consider introducing steam from both sides of the membrane at the same time to eliminate the transmembrane pressure differential
  • Maintain positive pressure during cooling: Positive pressure must be maintained with air or inert gas to avoid forming a reverse pressure differential

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your client requires a SIP sterilizing filtration system. You need to evaluate the following questions:

  • Can the housing material withstand 1-2 bar steam pressure? (Stainless steel housings are normally fine, but polymer housings need to be confirmed)
  • Does the piping design allow condensate drainage? (If condensate cannot be drained, standing water will hinder steam penetration)
  • Is the cooling system equipped with gas-assisted cooling? (If only water cooling is available, the thermal shock risk needs to be evaluated)
  • Can the PLC control system monitor the upstream/downstream differential pressure and abort automatically when the limit is exceeded?

These design considerations must be settled at the start of the project, because they affect the piping layout, instrumentation and automation programming.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can a single-use polypropylene capsule filter not undergo SIP? What happens if it is done anyway?
  2. TR26 mentions that steam can be introduced from both sides of the membrane at the same time. What additional equipment does this design require in the piping layout?
  3. How does the "reverse pressure differential" during the cooling phase form? Explain using the relationship between temperature and pressure.
8.2 Irradiation Sterilization

Original Text

Common irradiation sterilization processes are gamma radiation and X-ray. Gamma and X-ray irradiation sterilization can be validated using a bioburden and dosimetry approach. Follow current International Organization for Standardization (ISO) standards such as ISO-11137: Sterilization of Health Care Products — Radiation (60).

Some polymers used in filter manufacturing have limited resistance to sterilizing irradiation. Filters may be constructed of radiation-resistant materials or polymers formulated with antioxidant additives designed to protect the polymer from excessive radiation-induced degradation. Irradiation sterilization, as with all sterilization methods, requires validation to establish sterility and stability.

Irradiation sterilization has several advantages, such as a high sterility-assurance level, no residual gas- or chemical-sterilization components, dry filters, and minimal interference by packaging components with the sterilization process. Disadvantages include potential lack of filter compatibility to high-dose and limited filter shelf life (most irradiated filters are not validated to withstand a second sterilization cycle involving irradiation or steam). Steam sterilization of previously irradiated filters may increase extractables and leachables and could lead to loss of filter integrity, which could be confirmed by an integrity test.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Irradiation Sterilization: Uses high-energy radiation (gamma rays or X-rays) to destroy the DNA of microorganisms and achieve sterilization. It is a "penetrating" sterilization: the radiation passes directly through the packaging, so filters can be sterilized in their final packaging.

Bioburden and Dosimetry Approach: The initial microbial load on the product (bioburden) is measured first, and then the radiation dose (dosimetry) needed to achieve the required sterility assurance level is calculated. This differs from the BI approach used for steam sterilization.

ISO 11137: The international standard for radiation sterilization, which specifies the requirements for dose establishment, dose audit and routine sterilization.

Shelf Life: Irradiated filters usually have a shorter shelf life, because radiation continues to cause polymer degradation even after sterilization is complete.

Analogy

Irradiation sterilization is like sending food through an airport X-ray machine: the rays pass straight through the packaging and kill the germs, and the food itself does not need to be unwrapped or heated. The advantage is that it is a "dry" sterilization (no steam or water needed). The disadvantage is that some materials get "sunburned" by radiation: just as skin becomes fragile after sunburn from ultraviolet light, some plastics also degrade after exposure to gamma rays, and this degradation accumulates over time, so the shelf life is limited.

Key Notes

TR26 points out an extremely important cross-sterilization risk:

Steam sterilization of previously irradiated filters may increase extractables and leachables and could lead to loss of filter integrity.

This means that a filter that has already been irradiated should not be steam sterilized again. Radiation has already weakened the polymer structure, and the high temperature and pressure of steam will further accelerate degradation, resulting in:

  • A significant increase in extractables and leachables (E&L), affecting product quality
  • Loss of the membrane's physical integrity, affecting sterility assurance

In a CDMO environment, this means that if you buy "pre-irradiated" ready-to-use filters, you must never autoclave them again before use.

Practice Questions

  1. Irradiation sterilization is validated with the "bioburden + dosimetry" approach, while steam sterilization is validated with the "biological indicator" approach. What is the core difference between these two approaches?
  2. Why is the shelf life of irradiated filters usually shorter than that of steam-sterilized filters? How is this related to the effect of radiation on polymers?
  3. A CDMO purchased a batch of pre-irradiated capsule filters, and an operator mistakenly placed them in an autoclave for another sterilization. Can these filters still be used? How should they be handled?
8.3 Gas Sterilization

Original Text

The most common sterilization gas is ethylene oxide, which may be used at 100% or diluted in a carrier gas. In addition to the increased environmental impact from using ethylene oxide as a sterilant, there may be safety factors such as the formation of ethylene chlorohydrin and ethylene glycol reaction products and ethylene oxide that may remain in the filter matrix. For successful filter sterilization by ethylene oxide, the filters should be dry and wrapped in a manner that allows for the free entry of water vapor and gas into the filter matrix. Following sterilization, the package also should permit the removal of residual gas and other undesirable byproducts. Due to the difficulty of ensuring gas penetration into and out of the filter membrane, this method of sterilization is typically not used for pleated filters but only for very small, encapsulated disc-style filters.

Parameters influencing gas sterilization are preconditioning factors, gas concentration, relative humidity, temperature, and time. Since there is no established correlation between lethality and the interrelationship between the sterilization parameters, destruction of a suitable biological indicator is the preferred method for validation and process monitoring. Spores of Bacillus atrophaeus have been established as the indicator of choice for this sterilization method.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Ethylene Oxide (EO / EtO): A gas with strong microbicidal power that can kill microorganisms at relatively low temperatures, making it suitable for materials that cannot tolerate high heat. However, it is a known carcinogen and leaves residues in materials, creating harmful by-products.

Ethylene Chlorohydrin and Ethylene Glycol: By-products formed when EO reacts with chlorides and moisture in the filter material or packaging. These residues are toxic and must be removed after sterilization through an adequate aeration process.

Bacillus atrophaeus: The biological indicator organism dedicated to EO sterilization validation (formerly also called B. subtilis var. niger). Kill kinetics are not established with a mathematical model because the interaction of multiple parameters in EO sterilization is too complex, and no reliable predictive formula exists.

Analogy

EO gas sterilization is like using a cockroach fumigant at home: toxic gas is released in a sealed space and kills all the pests (microorganisms), but the space must then be thoroughly ventilated (aeration), otherwise the residual toxic gas will harm the people (patients) who live there. Moreover, fumigant has difficulty penetrating deep into folded clothes, which is why EO is not suitable for pleated filters: the gas cannot effectively penetrate the multi-layer pleated membrane structure.

Key Notes

TR26 takes a rather cautious attitude toward EO sterilization and basically restricts it to "very small disc filters". The main reasons are:

  • Penetration problem: EO gas has difficulty penetrating the complex structure of pleated membranes
  • Residue risk: Toxic by-products must be thoroughly removed, but the removal efficiency inside the membrane is difficult to verify
  • Environment and safety: EO is a carcinogen, and the global regulatory trend is to restrict its use
  • Lack of a mathematical model: Unlike steam sterilization, the microbicidal interaction among the five EO parameters cannot be predicted by a formula and can only be verified with BIs

In modern pharmaceutical manufacturing, EO-sterilized filters are already very rare. Most applications have been replaced by irradiation pre-sterilization or steam sterilization.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is EO sterilization suitable only for small disc filters and not for large pleated filters? Analyze from two angles: "gas penetration" and "residue removal".
  2. Steam sterilization can use D-values and Z-values to calculate the required sterilization time, but EO sterilization cannot. Why? What impact does this have on the validation strategy?
  3. If regulations completely prohibited the use of EO, what alternative sterilization methods could be used for the small filters that need EO sterilization?
8.4 Resterilization of Filters

Original Text

Many sterilizing filters are designed to withstand multiple autoclave or in situ SIP cycles, either to demonstrate robustness or to meet filter-user resterilization requirements. Multiple gamma-irradiation sterilization cycles are generally not applicable due to cumulative dosage effects. Autoclave resterilization of previously radiation-sterilized filters may also not be possible. Suitability of resterilization processes should be confirmed with the filter manufacturer.

Where the resterilization of sterilizing filters is elected, the filter user should ensure that the resterilization process and the number of resterilization cycles does not adversely affect the filter or the product. Resterilization, including sterilization conditions, number of sterilization cycles and cumulative sterilization times should be validated.

Sterilizing filters should be routinely discarded after the processing of a single lot. In instances where repeated use can be justified, however, the sterilizing-filter validation including integrity-testing, bacterial retention studies, and cleaning should incorporate the maximum number of lots to be processed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Resterilization: Performing multiple sterilization cycles on the same filter. This may occur in two situations:

  • Process need: For example, after SIP, filtration could not start immediately for some reason, and SIP needs to be repeated
  • Multi-batch use: A filter is used for one batch, then cleaned and resterilized for use in the next batch

Cumulative Dosage Effects: The doses from multiple irradiation sterilizations add up. Even if each dose is within the material's tolerance, the cumulative total dose may exceed the material's limit.

Single-Lot Use: TR26's default position is that a sterilizing filter should be discarded after processing one batch. Reuse requires additional validation.

Analogy

Resterilization is like drying the same garment over and over in a hot dryer: each cycle shrinks, fades and embrittles it a little more. The effect of steam sterilization on a filter is likewise cumulative: the first cycle may show no difference, but after the 10th or 20th cycle, the membrane's mechanical strength, chemical stability and extractables profile may change. This is why manufacturers specify a "maximum number of permitted sterilization cycles", like the "maximum washing temperature" on a garment care label.

Key Notes

This subsection involves three levels of risk management:

  1. Cumulative effects of the sterilization method:
    • Steam can be used multiple times (up to a limit)
    • Irradiation is not recommended for multiple use (cumulative dose)
    • Cross-sterilization (irradiation + steam) is generally not feasible
  2. Resterilization validation requirements: Integrity, bacterial retention capability and extractables levels must be validated at the maximum permitted number of cycles
  3. Additional validation for reuse: If a filter is reused across multiple batches, the validation scope must be extended to include cleaning validation, integrity testing at the maximum number of batches, and bacterial retention studies

In the decision hierarchy, the economic benefit of resterilization and reuse belongs to the lowest tier (commercial and efficiency). If resterilization could in any way affect sterile filtrate assurance (the highest tier), it should not be done.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: A client asks to reuse one 30" stainless steel housing sterilizing filter across 5 consecutive batches to reduce filtration consumable costs. As the process engineer, how should you evaluate this?

  • Confirm the maximum number of SIP cycles stated by the filter manufacturer (for example, 50) and ensure that 5 uses plus the necessary SIP cycles fall within this range
  • The validation plan must use the "maximum number of uses" (5 batches plus the corresponding number of SIP cycles and cleaning cycles) as the validation condition
  • An integrity test must be performed after each use, and it must pass before proceeding to the next batch
  • Cleaning validation must demonstrate that product residues will not affect the quality of the next batch
  • Bacterial retention studies must be performed after the maximum number of uses

Important reminder: in a multi-product CDMO facility, filter reuse also involves cross-contamination risk. A sterilizing filter should never be shared between different products.

Practice Questions

  1. TR26's default position is that "a sterilizing filter should be discarded after processing one batch". Under what circumstances can reuse be justified?
  2. Why is multiple irradiation sterilization generally not feasible, while multiple steam sterilization is acceptable within certain limits? How do the mechanisms of their effects on the material differ?
  3. If a filter fails the integrity test after autoclave sterilization, but the number of previous sterilizations is still within the manufacturer's stated limit, what are the possible causes?
  4. From the perspective of the decision hierarchy: are the cost savings from reusing filters sufficient to justify the additional validation burden and the potential quality risk?

Section Summary

  • Core objective of filter sterilization: Achieve the sterility assurance level (10-6 SAL) without damaging the physical integrity and chemical stability of the filter. This is a "dual requirement" that must be balanced.
  • Steam sterilization (Section 8.1) is the most widely used method and is divided into autoclave (for detachable assemblies) and SIP (for fixed systems). Key control points include: air removal (autoclave), differential pressure control (SIP), slow heating and cooling, and maintaining positive pressure during the cooling phase.
  • Irradiation sterilization (Section 8.2) offers a dry, residue-free method of sterilization, but is limited by material compatibility and shelf life. The most critical warning: a filter that has been irradiated should not be steam sterilized again, otherwise extractables increase and integrity may be lost.
  • Gas sterilization (Section 8.3) is mainly EO, but because of the difficulty of gas penetration and toxic residues, it is limited to small disc filters. Validation must rely on biological indicators (B. atrophaeus), because there is no reliable mathematical kill model.
  • Resterilization (Section 8.4) requires attention to cumulative effects: steam sterilization can be performed multiple times within the manufacturer's limits, but irradiation and cross-sterilization are generally not feasible. The default principle for filters is "discard after a single batch", and reuse requires comprehensive additional validation.
  • Decision hierarchy reminder: Whichever sterilization method is chosen, Sterile Filtrate Assurance is always the first priority. Any cost-saving measure that could jeopardize sterility assurance should be rejected.
A1

Section A1: Appendix I

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p78 - p81

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the basic physics and mathematics of diffusive flow testing
  • Apply Fick's law of diffusion to membrane integrity testing (Equations 4 & 5)
  • Understand how the Hagen-Poiseuille equation describes viscous flow and how it relates to the bubble point (Equations 6 & 7)
  • Interpret the linear relationship between diffusive flow and test pressure, and identify the critical point at which diffusive flow gives way to bulk flow
  • Understand the inverse relationship between pore size and bubble point pressure (Equation 8) and its use in quality control
10.0 Appendix I: Diffusive Flow Theory

Original Text

The theory of diffusive flow requires a comprehensive discussion that may be found in reference literature (1-5); however, for the purposes of this report the following is provided.

When quantifying diffusive flow during integrity-testing of a wetted membrane, test gas movement (at sufficiently low pressures) follows well-established laws of diffusion. In a symmetric membrane, the diffusive flux of test gas to atmospheric pressure, as a function of the test pressure applied, may be described by:

Equation 4
N = (D · H / L) · P · φ

Where:
N = the diffusive flux of the test gas
D = the diffusivity of the test gas through the wetting liquid
H = the solubility coefficient of the test gas in the wetting liquid
P = the applied differential pressure (or gauge pressure, if collected at atmospheric conditions)
φ = the overall porosity of the structure
L = the thickness of the wet layer (thickness of the membrane corrected by a "tortuosity" factor)

The diffusive flux, N, can be expressed in moles per unit area and unit time, but since these are measured at a fixed set of atmospheric pressure and temperature conditions, moles of gas can be converted to volumetric (ml/min or cc/min) units. Because the wetting fluid, test gas, filter thickness, porosity, and area are fixed, the expression for a volumetric diffusive flow in symmetric membranes further reduces to:

Equation 5
F = K1 · P

Where:
F = the volumetric diffusive flow
K1 = a proportionality constant
P = the applied differential pressure (or gauge pressure, if collected at atmospheric conditions)

Note that the diffusive flux of gas is independent of the actual filter-pore size, providing the pores are filled with the wetting liquid. Further, Equation 5 predicts a linear relationship between the diffusive flow and the applied test pressure. This relationship ceases to exist if the applied test pressure exceeds that required to displace the wetting liquid with gas. Once the bubble-point pressure of the largest pore(s) is reached, bulk or viscous flow of air will occur in addition to the diffusive flow. This viscous flow of test gas through the pores from which the liquid has been displaced will obey Newton's laws of viscous transport, often modeled by the Hagen-Poiseuille equation for flow through cylindrical tubes.

Equation 6
Q = (π · d4 / 128μ) · (P / L)

Where:
Q = the volumetric flow rate of the test gas
P = the applied differential pressure (or gauge pressure, if collected at atmospheric conditions)
d = the diameter of the pore
μ = the viscosity of the test gas
L = the length test gas must travel to the downstream side, or the length of wetted pores through the membrane

The pressure at which a given pore will open to viscous flow can be estimated from the cylindrical capillary relationship attributed to Laplace, often referred to as the "Bubble Point equation."

Equation 7
P = (4 · k · γ · cosθ) / d

Where:
P = the differential pressure at which a given pore will open
k = the correction factor for the shape of the largest pores
γ = the surface tension of the wetting liquid
cosθ = the contact ("wetting") angle between the liquid and the membrane
d = the diameter of the largest pores

To demonstrate the dependence on the liquid used to wet the pores and its interaction with the filter material, Equation 7 shows an inverse relationship between the pore diameter and the test pressure necessary to free the wetting liquid from the pore. If the wetting liquid and membrane surface chemistry are held constant, the expression can be simplified to read:

Equation 8
d = K2 / P

Where:
d = the diameter of the largest pore
K2 = a proportionality constant
P = the differential pressure at which a given pore will open

K2 is a correction factor accounting for shape of the largest pores as well as wetting properties for a given membrane/liquid combination. The value of the constant, and therefore the bubble point, in relationship to its retentive capabilities for a given contaminant, is established empirically.

The theory behind diffusive flow-testing can best be summarized by the extended integrity-test profile in Figure 7.0-3🔍, depicting the gas-flow properties of a wetted filter as a function of the applied test pressure. The linear portion at the lower test pressures corresponds to the diffusive flow regime described by Equation 4 or Equation 5, while viscous flow becomes the main transport mechanism for the steeper portion at higher pressures. The transition from diffusive to bulk flow (diffusive plus viscous flow) represents the maximum end of the pore-size distribution, as the larger pores are being voided of their wetting liquid. The relative size of the membrane's largest pores can be estimated from the test pressure using Equation 8.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Diffusive Flow: When a wetted membrane is tested at a pressure below the bubble point, gas molecules cross the liquid-filled pores by a solution-diffusion mechanism. The gas does not pass directly through open holes; it first dissolves in the wetting liquid and then diffuses from the high-pressure side to the low-pressure side.

Viscous Flow: When the test pressure exceeds the bubble point, the wetting liquid is expelled from the pores and gas passes directly through the open pores as bulk flow. Flow is proportional to the fourth power of pore diameter (d4), so even a small increase in pore size produces a dramatic change in flow.

Tortuosity: The pores in a membrane do not run straight through it; they follow winding, curved paths. The actual diffusion path is longer than the membrane thickness, and the tortuosity factor corrects for this difference.

Formula: Equation 4 (application of Fick's law of diffusion)

N = (D × H / L) × P × φ

Physical meaning:
  N (diffusive flux) ∝ D (diffusion coefficient) × H (solubility)
  → The more readily the gas dissolves in the wetting liquid, the more it diffuses

  N ∝ P (pressure differential)
  → The greater the pressure differential, the greater the driving force

  N ∝ φ (porosity) / L (effective thickness)
  → The higher the porosity and the thinner the membrane, the more diffusion occurs

Important: diffusive flux is independent of the actual pore size. As long as the pores are filled with wetting liquid, diffusive flow depends only on the solution-diffusion properties of that liquid.

Formula: Equation 5 (simplified linear relationship)

F = K₁ × P

When the following are held constant:
  - Type of wetting liquid (fixed D, H)
  - Type of test gas
  - Membrane thickness and porosity (fixed L, φ)
  - Membrane area

all variables combine into a single constant, K₁
→ Diffusive flow is strictly linear with pressure

This linear relationship is the theoretical basis of integrity testing: as long as the membrane is intact (all pores filled with wetting liquid), the measured flow should be proportional to pressure.

Analogy

Imagine a wall built from sponge bricks that are completely saturated with water. You apply air pressure to one side of the wall:

  • At low pressure (diffusive flow): Air molecules act like "divers": they first dissolve into the water in the sponge and then slowly emerge on the other side. The flow is very small and proportional to pressure. This is the diffusive flow described by Fick's law.
  • At high pressure (bubble point breakthrough): The pressure becomes high enough to push the water out of the largest pores in the sponge, and air can pass straight through, like a tunnel that has just been opened, so the flow suddenly surges. This is the bubble point.
  • At even higher pressure: More and more pores are cleared, like more and more tunnels being opened, and the flow rises sharply.

Formula: Equation 6 (Hagen-Poiseuille equation)

Q = (π × d⁴ / 128μ) × (P / L)

Key finding: Q ∝ d⁴

This means:
  Pore diameter increases 2× → flow increases 16× (2⁴)
  Pore diameter increases 10% → flow increases by about 46% (1.1⁴)

This is why integrity testing is so sensitive to defects:
even a slightly oversized pore causes a measurable increase in flow.

Formula: Equations 7 & 8 (bubble point equations)

Equation 7 (Laplace capillary equation):
P = (4 × k × γ × cosθ) / d

Equation 8 (simplified form):
d = K₂ / P

Core concept:
  P (bubble point pressure) ∝ 1/d (pore diameter)
  → The smaller the pore, the higher the pressure needed to expel the liquid
  → The higher the bubble point pressure, the smaller the largest pore

Factors affecting the bubble point:
  γ (surface tension) ↑ → bubble point pressure ↑
  cosθ (contact angle) → cosθ ≈ 1 for complete wetting
  k (shape correction factor) → real pores are not perfect cylinders

Key Notes

Independence of diffusive flow: Equation 4 reveals a key fact: diffusive flow does not depend on pore size. Below the bubble point, whether the membrane is 0.2 μm or 0.45 μm, diffusive flow is theoretically the same as long as the chemistry and thickness are identical. This is why a diffusive flow test must be paired with a bubble point test to assess pore size.

Transition from linear to non-linear: On the integrity test curve (Figure 7.0-3🔍), the point where the response turns from linear (diffusive flow) to non-linear (bulk flow after the bubble point) is the key to judging membrane quality. If the transition occurs too early (at too low a pressure), abnormally large pores are present.

K2 is an empirical value: The constant K2 in the bubble point equation must be established experimentally and cannot be calculated from theory alone. Each membrane/wetting liquid combination has a different K2, which is why the manufacturer-specified test conditions must be used.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: In aseptic filling production, when operators perform filter integrity tests, understanding the practical meaning of these equations is essential:

  • The pressure set for a diffusive flow test must be below the bubble point pressure, typically about 80% of the bubble point, to ensure measurement in the pure diffusive flow region
  • If the measured diffusive flow exceeds the manufacturer's specification, it may indicate a membrane defect or oversized pores, which directly affects sterility assurance
  • Changing the wetting liquid (for example, from WFI to the product) changes D and H, so the diffusive flow limit must also be revalidated

Practice Questions

  1. Why does Equation 5 state that diffusive flow is linear with test pressure, while the actual test curve becomes non-linear at high pressure? What physical phenomenon does this transition represent?
  2. According to the Hagen-Poiseuille equation (Equation 6), flow is proportional to the fourth power of pore diameter. If a 0.2 μm membrane has a single defect pore of 0.4 μm, how many times greater is the viscous flow through this single defect pore than through a normal 0.2 μm pore?
  3. If the wetting liquid is changed from water (surface tension 72 mN/m) to an IPA/water mixture (surface tension about 25 mN/m), how does the bubble point pressure change according to Equation 7? What effect does this have on the test parameter settings?

Section Summary

  • Diffusive flow follows Fick's law: Below the bubble point pressure, gas crosses the wetted membrane by solution-diffusion; flow is linear with pressure (F = K1P) and independent of pore size
  • Viscous flow follows the Hagen-Poiseuille equation: Above the bubble point, gas flows directly through the open pores, and flow is proportional to the fourth power of pore diameter (Q ∝ d4), making integrity testing highly sensitive to defects
  • Bubble point is inversely proportional to pore size: P = 4kγcosθ/d; the smaller the pore, the higher the bubble point; the relationship is affected by the surface tension of the wetting liquid and the contact angle
  • The transition point on the integrity test curve: The change from linear (pure diffusive flow) to non-linear (diffusive flow plus viscous flow) marks the point at which the largest pores begin to empty, and is a key indicator for assessing membrane quality
  • Practical implication: The constants in all the equations (K1, K2) are empirical values that must be established experimentally for each membrane/liquid combination; theoretical calculations cannot be applied directly
A2

Section A2: Appendix II

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p81 - p88

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the principle, key operating points and scope of application of the Bubble Point Test
  • Understand how the Diffusive Flow Test measures flow quantitatively and why it suits large-area filters
  • Learn the indirect measurement principle of the Pressure Hold/Decay Test and the effect of upstream volume
  • Understand how Automated Integrity-Test Instruments work
  • Understand the mathematical relationship between pressure decay and diffusive flow (Equations 10-14) and its role in validation
11.1 Bubble Point Test

Original Text

The bubble point test is based on the premise that membrane pores can be represented as a multiplicity of capillaries, and the knowledge that liquid is held in a capillary tube due to surface tension and other factors. The minimum gas pressure required to force liquid out of the pores is a direct function of pore diameter. The bubble point test is used to determine the pressure at which there is bulk flow of the test gas through the largest pores in the membrane that are the first from which liquid is displaced. Since the observed bubble point is a function of the wetting-liquid surface tension, the contact angle, the pore shape, and the pore size, results are specific to each membrane polymer/surface chemistry, structure, wetting fluid combination, methods, equipment and surface area. While bubble point is an indication of the largest pores, it cannot be used directly to calculate their diameter. The bubble point test also does not indicate the number of largest pores, which can affect the probability of bacterial retention (if the organisms are smaller than the pores).

The test can be performed manually by subjecting a wetted filter membrane to gas pressure on the upstream side. Tubing on the membrane's downstream side is immersed in a liquid. The differential pressure at which a steady stream of bubbles, or in some cases the displacement of liquid, is observed is the bubble point. The typical test equipment for performing a manual bubble point test is illustrated in Figure 11.0-1.

The manual bubble point test is a sensitive, visual technique for disc filters and other small surface-area membrane filters; it may be performed as one of the quality assurance tests for membranes during the casting process. The bubble point test detects major and minor defects and out-of-size pores and can be correlated with the bacterial retention study. It is also a reliable integrity test of larger surface-area filters until the diffusional flow across the membrane masks the flow rate from the pores from which the wetting fluid has been displaced.

The following are points to consider when performing the bubble point test manually:

  • Use a pressure gauge with easy-to-read incremental steps and increase slowly in a step-wise manner.
  • Allow pressure to stabilize at each step in the pressure increase. The true bubble point value may be overshot if pressure is increased too rapidly. The test cannot be repeated without rewetting the membrane.
  • Minimize downstream connections and avoid kinking the downstream tubing.
  • Check for leaks in the system.
  • Clearly define the bubble point as bulk air flow. The most common error is misidentifying bubbles that are not the result of bulk flow. Some bubbles will appear because of diffusion across the wetted membrane. Free flowing air is the indication, not the first bubble.
  • Manually performed, bubble point-test determination can be subjectively different from one operator to another; therefore, operators should be properly trained to conduct the test and to interpret test results.
  • Keep upstream volume to a minimum. With extremely high upstream volumes, longer stabilization between pressure increases may be required.
  • Maintain the temperature within the specified range.

Test attributes:

  • Relatively fast, with a short stabilization time
  • Can be directly related to the membrane pore size
  • Relatively easy to perform for small-to-medium area filters
  • Easy to establish correlation between bacterial retention and physical integrity test values
  • Sensitivity may be limited for large-membrane-area systems if diffusive air flow below the bubble point is significant
  • Manual testing requires downstream (i.e., sterile side) manipulations

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Bubble Point: The upstream side of a wetted membrane is pressurized. When the pressure is high enough to push the wetting liquid out of the largest pores, gas passes through as "bulk flow" and produces a continuous stream of bubbles in the liquid downstream. That pressure value is the bubble point.

Key limitations:

  • The bubble point reflects the size of the largest pore, but it does not tell you how many such pores there are
  • A bubble point value belongs to a specific membrane/wetting liquid combination; values for different combinations cannot be compared with each other
  • For large-area membranes, the background "noise" of diffusive flow can mask the bubble point signal

Analogy

The bubble point test is like blowing up a balloon in reverse. Imagine a sponge (the membrane) with many pores of different sizes, all filled with water. You slowly increase the air pressure from one side:

  • At low pressure: Air "permeates" through the water (diffusive flow), and you see only occasional small bubbles. This is not the bubble point!
  • At the bubble point: The pressure is high enough to "blow" the water out of the largest pore, air flows straight through, and you see a continuous, steady stream of bubbles. This is the true bubble point.

The most common operating error is mistaking the occasional bubbles produced by diffusion for the bubble point. It is like telling the difference between a tap that is dripping and a tap that has been turned on.

Key Notes

"Free flowing air is the indication, not the first bubble": This is the most important operating judgment in the bubble point test. Many investigations of integrity test failures have found that the operator called the bubble point too early.

Not repeatable: Once the bubble point has been exceeded (the wetting liquid has been expelled), the membrane must be re-wetted before it can be tested again. Unlike the diffusive flow test, this adds pressure on the operator to get the test right the first time.

Operator subjectivity: The result of a manual bubble point test varies from operator to operator, so operator training and qualification are critical. Automated instruments can remove this subjectivity.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: On a CDMO aseptic filling line, the bubble point test is most commonly used for:

  • Validation of small-area filters: Quality testing of 47 mm disc membranes in the laboratory
  • PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing): In some situations, as a quick pass/fail check
  • Incoming inspection: Quality assurance testing of membranes at release by the filter supplier

For production-scale, large-area cartridges (such as 10-inch cartridges), however, diffusive flow or pressure decay testing is usually preferred, because at large areas the background diffusive flow interferes with reading the bubble point.

Practice Questions

  1. Why can the sensitivity of the bubble point test be limited for large-area membrane systems? How is this related to diffusive flow?
  2. In a manual bubble point test, why can increasing the pressure too quickly cause "overshoot"? How does this affect the test result?
  3. The bubble point tells you the pressure threshold of the largest pore, so why is it said that it "cannot be used to calculate pore size directly"?
11.2 Diffusive Flow Test

Original Text

In a wetted membrane filter under pressure, gas molecules migrate through the liquid-filled pores at differential pressures below the bubble point via a diffusion process following Fick's Law of Diffusion. The overall rate of diffusion for a filter is proportional to the applied test pressure, its membrane surface area and wetted thickness, and the solubility and diffusivity of the test gas in the wetting liquid. In small surface-area filters, such as flat-disc filters, this gas flow may be too low to be measured accurately. In large surface-area filter systems, it is significant and can be measured to perform an integrity test. To accurately measure the test limit, one must use the correct limit based on the gas type. This only applies to Diffusion/Pressure Hold test; the bubble point test is not influenced by gas type.

The diffusive flow test quantitatively measures the sum of diffusive flow through the membrane and flow through any open pores. The filter user should use the manufacturer's recommended testing method for each filter, ensuring that the method is appropriate and has been validated for the specific case. Diffusive flow measurements may be performed downstream of the wetted membrane under constant test pressure, or on the upstream side by measuring the air flow required to maintain constant test pressure. These tests are performed on membrane filters by first wetting out the filter with an appropriate liquid and draining excess fluid. The upstream side of the filter is pressurized, and the diffusive flow is measured. Test equipment for a diffusive-flow integrity test with flow measurement on the downstream side is illustrated in Figure 11.0-2. The inverted burette or measuring cylinder filled with liquid for the collection of diffused gas can be replaced with an appropriate gas flowmeter.

The following are points to consider when performing the diffusive flow test manually:

  • Minimize downstream connections and downstream volume.
  • Avoid kinking downstream tubing.
  • Check for leaks in the system.
  • Use a volume-collection vessel with the proper resolution.
  • Use downstream tubing with the smallest practical diameter and shortest practical length to ensure proper flow resolution.
  • Keep upstream volume to a minimum; with extremely high upstream volumes, proper stabilization time must be allowed.
  • Maintain a stable testing temperature throughout the testing period.
  • Allow sufficient equilibration time after pressurizing the system.
  • The test should be performed at the filter manufacturer's specified pressure.

Test attributes:

  • Developed to provide increased sensitivity for large membrane surface-area systems
  • Provides an objective, quantitative measure of gas flow
  • Can be correlated to bacterial retention (as discussed in Section 7.1)
  • May be sensitive to temperature fluctuations
  • Manual testing requires downstream (i.e., sterile side) manipulations

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Diffusive Flow Test: At a fixed test pressure below the bubble point, the total gas flow through the wetted membrane is measured quantitatively. This total flow has two components:

  • Diffusive Flow: Gas dissolves in the wetting liquid and then diffuses to the downstream side. This is normal.
  • Open Pore Flow: If there are pores that have not been wetted, gas passes straight through. This is abnormal and indicates a possible defect.

There are two ways to measure:

  • Downstream: The volume of gas that has diffused through is collected on the downstream side (for example, the inverted measuring cylinder method)
  • Upstream: The gas flow needed to maintain a constant upstream pressure is measured (for example, the mass flow meter method)

Analogy

The diffusive flow test is like checking whether a wetsuit leaks. You jump into the water wearing the wetsuit:

  • Normal situation: Water molecules slowly "permeate" through the wetsuit material. This is like diffusive flow: it is a property of the material itself, and the amount is small and predictable.
  • With a hole: Water rushes straight in. This is like bulk flow through an open pore, and the amount clearly exceeds the normal value.

The value of the diffusive flow test is that it can distinguish "normal material permeation" from "abnormal leakage through a hole", and by measuring quantitatively it gives a clear pass/fail decision.

Key Notes

Effect of the test gas: Different gases (for example air vs nitrogen vs CO2) have different solubilities and diffusion coefficients in the wetting liquid, so the diffusive flow limit must be set for the specific gas type. The bubble point test is not affected by this, because the bubble point depends on surface tension rather than gas solubility.

Temperature sensitivity: Temperature changes affect the gas solubility, the diffusion coefficient and the surface tension of the wetting liquid at the same time. Even a temperature fluctuation of 1-2°C can bias the measured value, and the effect is more pronounced in large-area membrane systems.

Sterility risk of downstream operations: A manual diffusive flow test requires making line connections and collecting gas on the downstream (sterile) side. In a PUPSIT scenario this carries a risk of compromising sterility, which is one of the main reasons the industry has adopted automated test instruments.

Formula and Calculation: Relationship Between Diffusive Flow and Area

The diffusive flow limit is usually expressed in ml/min

For membranes of the same material:
  Diffusive flow ∝ area (A)

For example:
  One 10" cartridge, area ≈ 0.7 m²
  → diffusive flow limit ≈ X ml/min

  Three 10" cartridges in parallel
  → total area = 2.1 m²
  → diffusive flow limit ≈ 3X ml/min

Important: The user must calculate the correct
diffusive flow limit based on the number of
cartridges actually installed

Practice Questions

  1. Why is the diffusive flow test "designed for large-area membrane systems"? What difficulties arise when this method is used on small-area membranes?
  2. In manual testing, why should downstream line connections and volume be "minimized"? How does an excessive downstream volume affect measurement accuracy?
  3. If the product liquid used in the process changes the properties of the wetting liquid, how does this affect the diffusive flow test result? How should it be handled?
11.3 Pressure Hold/Decay Test

Original Text

The pressure hold (or pressure decay) test is an indirect method of upstream diffusive flow-testing. In this method, the filter-housing is pressurized to a predetermined setting and then isolated from the pressure source. Gas flow across the membrane is quantitatively measured as a decay in pressure over a specific period of time. This test has been indirectly correlated to bacterial retention through its relationship to the diffusive flow test.

The allowable pressure drop is not published by filter manufacturers because it is unique to a given filter assembly and piping system, and it is calculated by taking into account the diffusive-flow specification at a given pressure and constant temperature, the upstream system volume, and the duration of the test. Therefore, this test requires a determination of the upstream volume of the system and a calculation of the allowable pressure drop.

The pressure hold/decay test measures the diffusive gas (typically air or nitrogen) flow through all the wetted pores, and the bulk gas flow through larger non-wetted pores, when applying a predetermined gas pressure to a wetted filter in a system that has no upstream leaks. Pressure decay on the upstream side is then measured, after isolating the incoming gas supply. Upstream integrity tests are particularly useful in critical fluid applications since they can be performed without compromising the sterility of the downstream system.

The pressure decay is a function of the gas flow through the filter and the upstream volume of the filter system. The pressure hold/decay test will confirm the integrity of the entire assembly, including housing seals. Therefore, failure analysis for lack of seal integrity is required prior to reaching the conclusion that the filter element itself is not integral.

If the pressure decay is below the maximum allowable value, the filter passes the physical integrity test. If the decay and concomitant pressure drop exceed the correlated maximum-allowable value, the filter fails the test, and an investigation should be performed (see Section 7.6).

In performing the pressure hold/decay test, the physical conditions must be held constant, as the test is affected by interactions of temperature, system volume and filter area. The pressure hold/decay test equipment is illustrated in Figure 11.0-3. This test commonly is performed preprocess, post-sterilization of the filter assembly, and again after sterilizing filtration.

The following are points to consider when performing the pressure hold/decay test:

  • There should be no leaks in the upstream side of the system.
  • Upstream volume should be determined to ensure a proper maximum-allowable-pressure drop calculation. It is recommended that upstream volume be kept to a minimum.
  • The downstream side of the filter should be vented to the atmosphere.
  • Sufficient stabilization time should be allowed before beginning the test; stabilization time increases with larger upstream volumes and increased filter surface-areas.
  • A pressure gauge or transducer with the proper degree of accuracy for determining the pressure drop during testing should be used.
  • Test time may need to be extended if the pressure-drop rate is extremely small relative to the sensitivity of the gauge or transducer.
  • The specified temperature should be maintained to ensure that the temperature does not change during the integrity test. Note that lower upstream volumes will result in increased sensitivity to temperature changes.

Test attributes:

  • Relatively easy to perform
  • No downstream manipulation is required
  • Can be correlated to bacterial retention (as discussed in Section 7.1)
  • Sensitivity of the test decreases as the upstream volume of the system is increased
  • Resolution of the test is limited by the resolution of the pressure sensor used
  • Process conditions for the test, including temperature and gas used for pressurization, should be defined for the issuance of integrity test values.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Pressure Hold/Decay Test: This is an indirect method of diffusive flow testing. The principle is simple: the upstream side of the membrane is pressurized to a specified pressure, the gas supply is then shut off, and the rate at which the pressure falls is observed.

  • Why does the pressure fall? Because gas keeps diffusing through the wetted membrane to the downstream side (plus any leakage through defective pores)
  • Why is it "indirect"? Because it measures a change in pressure rather than measuring gas flow directly. The upstream volume must be known to convert the result into an equivalent flow
  • Greatest advantage: No operation is needed on the downstream (sterile) side, so the downstream sterile environment is not compromised

Analogy

The pressure decay test is like checking whether a bicycle tire is leaking:

  • Inflate (pressurize): You inflate the tire to a specified pressure and then remove the pump (shut off the gas supply)
  • Watch the gauge (decay measurement): If the pressure has dropped only slightly after 10 minutes, the tire is fine (passes the test); if the pressure falls quickly, there is a leak (fails the test)
  • Effect of tire size (upstream volume): A large truck tire and a small bicycle tire with the same size of hole show completely different rates of pressure drop. The larger the volume, the smaller the pressure change caused by the same leak.

This is why the "allowable value" for pressure decay is not a fixed number, but must be calculated individually from the upstream volume of each system.

Key Notes

The dual effect of upstream volume:

  • Larger volume → lower test sensitivity: The same amount of gas leakage causes a smaller pressure change in a large-volume system, so it is harder to detect
  • Larger volume → longer stabilization time: After pressurization, more time is needed for temperature and pressure to reach equilibrium
  • Smaller volume → higher temperature sensitivity: In a small-volume system, a minor temperature change causes a noticeable pressure fluctuation, which may be misjudged as a leak

Whole-assembly test: The pressure decay test checks not only the membrane itself but the entire filtration assembly, including the housing seals, O-rings and line connections. If the test fails, the possibility of a seal leak must be ruled out before concluding that the "membrane is non-integral".

Formula and Calculation: Calculating the Allowable Pressure Decay

Basic concept of pressure decay:

  Allowable pressure drop = f(diffusive flow specification, upstream volume, test time)

Conceptual formula:
  ΔP_allowable ≈ (F_limit × t_test × P_atm) / V_up

Where:
  F_limit = diffusive flow specification limit (ml/min)
  t_test = test time (min)
  P_atm = atmospheric pressure (mbar)
  V_up = upstream volume (ml)

Example:
  F_limit = 15 ml/min
  t_test = 10 min
  V_up = 5000 ml
  P_atm = 1013 mbar

  ΔP_allowable ≈ (15 × 10 × 1013) / 5000
               ≈ 30.4 mbar

Note: This is a simplified concept. The actual calculation
must use the exact exponential decay model of
Equation 14.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: the preferred method for PUPSIT:

The pressure decay test is the most favored method for performing PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing), because:

  • It operates entirely on the upstream side and does not require touching the downstream sterile side, which maintains Sterility Assurance
  • It can be performed after sterilization and before filtration to confirm the integrity of the whole filtration system (membrane + housing + seals)
  • Combined with an automated instrument, it can remove the subjectivity of human judgment

Note, however, that accurate determination of the upstream volume is a prerequisite for this method. In a CDMO, different product lines may use differently configured piping systems, and the upstream volume must be re-determined after every change.

Practice Questions

  1. If a pressure decay test fails, where should your investigation start? Why can you not simply conclude "membrane failure"?
  2. When a pressure decay test is performed on a system with a 20 L upstream volume, what additional considerations apply compared with a 2 L system?
  3. Why do filter manufacturers not publish an allowable pressure decay value directly, and publish only the diffusive flow specification?
11.4 Automated Integrity-Test Instruments

Original Text

Numerous automated integrity-test instruments are available for use in the pharmaceutical industry to determine a bubble-point pressure value using incremental pressure hold/decay tests. The pressure hold/decay tests are performed at increasing pressures, with the bubble point identified where the pressure decay become nonlinear, based on an algorithm specific to each instrument manufacturer. Automated integrity-test units determine diffusive-flow values, either by measuring pressure hold/decay at a specified test pressure and calculating the diffusive-flow value using a predetermined measurement of the upstream volume of the filter assembly or by direct-flow measurement using an upstream mass flow meter.
