Another method to determine diffusive-flow values is a system with additional small volumes to maintain constant test pressure during the test, which eliminates the need to measure upstream volume.

Irrespective of the technique used to determine filter bubble point or diffusive flow, the automated integrity-test instrument must be qualified against an independent reference method.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Automated Integrity-Test Instruments: These instruments automate the steps of manual testing. There are three main measurement methods:

  • Incremental pressure decay method: Pressure decay is performed at stepwise increasing pressures, and an algorithm detects the turning point where the pressure decay changes from linear to non-linear, thereby determining the bubble point automatically
  • Calculated diffusive flow method: Pressure decay is measured at a specified pressure, and the equivalent diffusive flow value is calculated using a previously measured upstream volume
  • Direct Flow measurement method: An upstream mass flow meter directly measures the gas flow needed to maintain constant pressure, with no need to know the upstream volume

Analogy

Manual vs automated integrity testing is like weighing with a balance vs an electronic scale:

  • Manual (balance): Requires operator skill, judgment and experience, and results may vary from person to person
  • Automated (electronic scale): One-button measurement, the number is displayed directly, and the result is objective and repeatable. But an electronic scale also needs periodic calibration (qualification against a reference method).

Key Notes

Instrument qualification is required: Whichever automated instrument is used, it must be qualified against an independent reference method. This usually includes:

  • IQ (Installation Qualification): The instrument is correctly installed and the hardware is intact
  • OQ (Operational Qualification): The instrument is tested under standard conditions using reference standards, and the results meet specifications
  • PQ (Performance Qualification): The instrument performance is verified under actual production conditions

Automated instruments from different manufacturers use different algorithms, so it cannot be assumed that the test results of different instruments are fully equivalent. Comparing across instruments requires additional validation work.

Practice Questions

  1. Compared with the calculated pressure decay method, what is the greatest technical advantage of the Direct Flow measurement method? Why does it remove the need to measure the upstream volume?
  2. If your CDMO uses automated test instruments from both manufacturer A and manufacturer B, what must be considered in the validation strategy?
11.5 Correlation Between Pressure Hold/Decay and Diffusive Flow

Original Text

Diffusive flow prior to the initiation of any bulk flow through the first opened pore can be expressed by Equation 10, derived from Fick's Law and Henry's Law.

Equation 10
Vs = (M / ρS) · AF · D · (p2 - p1) · k / (Hc · L)

The measured flow resulting from a specified differential pressure can be related to the microbial retentive capability of a filter membrane.

The relationship between this flow, measured by the nondestructive pressure hold/decay test and the destructive microbial challenge test, is the objective of the validation for each filter medium and each filter configuration.

Equation 10 shows that diffusive flow is proportional to the difference between the test pressure and atmospheric pressure. Since the pressure in the upstream volume decreases, due to the effluent diffusive flow, the flow also decreases.

From the ideal gas equation,

Equation 11
p2 · Vup = N · R · T

Equation 11a — the change of state per time, dt, can be derived:
(dp2/dt) · Vup = dN/dt
Equation 12
-V · ρS = dN/dt

Equations 10 and 11a can be combined to yield:

Equation 13
-(dp2/dt) · Vup / (R · T) = M · AF · D · (p2 - p1) · k / (Hc · L)

Equation 13a
[1 / (p2 - p1)] · (-dp2) = (R · T / Vup) · (M · AF · D · k) / (Hc · L) · dt

Equation 13b — carrying out the integration between limits and simplifying:
p2I - p2II = (p2I - p1) · [1 - exp(-(R · T / Vup) · (M · AF · D · k) / (Hc · L) · (tII - tI))]

To obtain an equation for the pressure decay (as a function of volume flow in the standard state), introducing Equation 10 in Equation 13b and using pS = ρSRT/M yields:

Equation 14
p2I - p2II = (p2I - p1) · [1 - exp(-(tII - tI) / Vup · Vs · ρS / (p2I - p1))]

Measurements of pressure and time are necessary for the determination of Vs because the retentive capability of the filter membrane is related to the flow Vs, as shown in Equation 14. Additionally, the upstream volume must be known and the temperature in the upstream volume must be constant.

Table 11.5-1🔍 Nomenclature for Symbols Used in Equations 10-14

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Mathematical relationship between pressure decay and diffusive flow: The mathematical derivation in this section builds a key bridge: how to calculate the equivalent standard volumetric flow (Vs) from a measured pressure decay (p2I - p2II).

The logical chain of the derivation:

  • Equation 10: Fick's law + Henry's law → the physical model of diffusive flow
  • Equation 11/11a: The ideal gas law → the relationship between pressure change and change in the number of moles
  • Equation 12-13: Combining the above → the differential equation for the rate of pressure decay
  • Equation 13b/14: Integration → the exponential decay model of pressure decay

The final result (Equation 14) tells us that pressure decay follows an exponential decay pattern rather than a simple linear drop.

Formula and Calculation: Physical Interpretation of Equation 14

Equation 14:
ΔP = (p₂ᵢ - p₁) × [1 - exp(-t/τ)]

Where the time constant τ is related to:
  τ ∝ V_up / V_s

Physical meaning:
  - Pressure decay follows an exponential decay, not a linear drop
  - Decay is faster at first, then gradually slows
  - The larger the upstream volume (V_up), the slower the decay
  - The larger the diffusive flow (V_s), the faster the decay

Practical simplification:
  When the decay is much smaller than the initial pressure difference
  (i.e. ΔP << p₂ᵢ - p₁)
  the exponential function can be approximated as linear:
  ΔP ≈ V_s × ρ_S × t / V_up

This is why, in short tests,
the pressure decay looks almost linear.

Analogy

The exponential decay of pressure is like a hot cup of coffee cooling down (Newton's law of cooling):

  • Freshly brewed coffee (high pressure difference) → heat is lost fastest (highest rate of pressure decay)
  • As the temperature approaches room temperature (smaller pressure difference) → the cooling rate gradually falls
  • The larger the cup (the larger the upstream volume) → the slower the cooling (the slower the pressure decay)

Observed over a short period, the cooling is almost linear. This is why, in actual testing (usually only a few minutes), a linear approximation is normally accurate enough.

Key Notes

The core goal of validation: To establish a correlation between the non-destructive physical test (pressure decay/diffusive flow) and the destructive microbial challenge test. This means:

  • The limit of the physical test must reliably predict the microbial retention capability of the membrane
  • This correlation must be established independently for each membrane material and configuration
  • Establishing the correlation is the responsibility of the filter manufacturer, and the user needs to verify that it is applicable

Three necessary conditions: Using Equation 14 requires (1) accurate pressure and time measurement, (2) a known upstream volume, and (3) constant temperature. Uncertainty in any one of them affects the reliability of the calculated result.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: validation strategy:

A CDMO manufactures multiple products at the same time, and the filtration system configuration changes frequently. Understanding the mathematical relationship between pressure decay and diffusive flow is therefore essential:

  • New line installation: The upstream volume must be determined accurately, which is usually calculated from a gas source of known flow and the pressure response
  • Configuration change management: Any change to piping or components may change the upstream volume, requiring re-determination and an update of the allowable pressure decay value
  • Cross-method equivalence: If it is necessary to switch between the diffusive flow test and the pressure decay test, a conversion relationship must be established through Equation 14

Practice Questions

  1. Why does Equation 14 show that pressure decay is exponential rather than a linear drop? Under what conditions is a linear approximation reasonable?
  2. If the measurement of the upstream volume has a 10% error, how does this affect the calculation of the allowable pressure decay value? What risk does it pose to the judgment of the test result?
  3. What physical meaning does the symbol Hc (Henry's Law coefficient) in Table 11.5-1🔍 represent? If the test gas is changed (from air to nitrogen), which symbols will change in value?

Section Summary

  • Bubble Point: Detects the pressure threshold at which the largest pores open; suited to small-area membranes; manual operation is subjective, so care is needed to distinguish diffusion bubbles from true bulk flow
  • Diffusive Flow: Measures quantitatively the gas flow through a wetted membrane; designed for large-area membrane systems; affected by the type of test gas and by temperature
  • Pressure Hold/Decay: An indirect upstream diffusive flow test that needs no downstream operation and is the preferred method for PUPSIT; requires accurate determination of the upstream volume
  • Automated instruments: Remove human subjectivity and can perform bubble point and diffusive flow tests, but must be qualified against an independent reference method
  • Mathematical relationship between the methods (Equations 10-14): Pressure decay follows an exponential decay model, and Equation 14 converts a pressure decay value into an equivalent diffusive flow value, provided the upstream volume is known and the temperature is constant
  • The ultimate goal of validation: To establish a reliable correlation between non-destructive physical tests and microbial retention capability
A3

Section A3: Appendix III

PDA Technical Report No. 26 (Revised 2025) — Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids | p89 - p92

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the basic lines of investigation when a manual integrity test fails (insufficient wetting, inappropriate wetting fluid, wrong pore size rating)
  • Learn to use Table 12.0-1🔍 to systematically troubleshoot the various failures of automated integrity test instruments
  • Understand the possible causes of different failure symptoms (no pressure build-up, abort during stabilization, bubble point failure, diffusive flow failure, pressure decay failure, etc.)
  • Build a "symptom to root cause" investigation mindset and avoid blaming the filter membrane for a defect by default
  • Understand the role of common root causes (temperature, wetting, sealing, tubing connections) across multiple failure modes
12.0 Integrity Test Troubleshooting Guide

Original Text

Problems may occur when performing integrity tests, manually or with an automatic test unit. The following tables are a troubleshooting guide to facilitate failure investigations. It is important to note that an automatic test device avoids human subjectivity and, therefore, may be more reproducible.

When investigating a manual integrity test failure, the following items should be considered:

  • Insufficiently wetted filter
  • Inappropriate wetting medium (e.g., solvent instead of water)
  • Incorrect filter pore size rating

Table 12.0-1🔍 lists items for consideration when investigating an automatic device integrity-test failure.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Integrity Test Troubleshooting: When an integrity test result is not as expected, the root cause must be investigated systematically. The key shift in thinking is: a test failure is not the same as a filter failure.

Failure sources fall into three main categories:

  • Membrane-related: a genuine membrane defect, the wrong pore size rating, or product residue left on the membrane
  • Operation-related: insufficient wetting, an inappropriate wetting fluid, incorrect parameter settings, or insufficient stabilization time
  • System-related: tubing leaks, damaged seals, gas supply problems, internal instrument faults, or temperature drift

Manual testing adds a fourth category: human judgment error (for example, mistaking diffusive bubbles for the bubble point during a bubble point test). Automated instruments eliminate this category of problem.

Analogy

Troubleshooting an integrity test is like investigating a car that fails its inspection:

  • Failed emissions test: This does not necessarily mean the engine is broken. It could be a faulty sensor, the wrong fuel, a previous repair that was not done properly, or incorrect test conditions (temperature)
  • The right investigation order: First check the simplest and most common causes (sensor, connections, fuel), then work toward the more complex ones (the engine itself)
  • The same logic applies: When a filter test fails, check the wetting, seals, tubing and parameters first, and only then consider a problem with the filter itself

Key Notes

The three most common causes of manual test failure:

  • Insufficiently Wetted Filter: This is the most common root cause. If the membrane is not completely wetted, dry pores let gas pass straight through, giving a high diffusive flow or a low bubble point. Solution: follow the manufacturer's recommended wetting procedure and ensure sufficient wetting time and pressure
  • Inappropriate Wetting Medium: Using a solvent instead of water as the wetting fluid changes the surface tension and contact angle, so the test parameters are completely different. The wetting fluid specified by the manufacturer must be used
  • Incorrect pore size rating: If a 0.45 μm filter is installed but the test parameters for a 0.2 μm filter are used, the test will inevitably "fail"

Formula and Calculation: Priority Model for Troubleshooting

"Outside-in" troubleshooting priority:

Level 1 (outermost - check first):
  ├── Are the tubing connections correct?
  ├── Are the seals / O-rings intact?
  ├── Are the valves in the correct position?
  └── Is the gas supply normal?

Level 2 (operational):
  ├── Was the wetting procedure fully executed?
  ├── Is the wetting fluid correct?
  ├── Are the test parameters set correctly?
  ├── Is the stabilization time sufficient?
  └── Is the temperature stable?

Level 3 (instrument):
  ├── Are the internal pneumatic components working normally?
  ├── Is the reference volume correct?
  └── Is the test code correct?

Level 4 (innermost - check last):
  └── Does the filter membrane itself have a defect?

Statistical pattern:
  ~70% of test failures are caused by Levels 1-2
  ~20% by Level 3
  only ~10% are genuine filter problems

Practice Questions

  1. Why can an automated test instrument still produce test failures, even though it eliminates human subjectivity? List at least three causes of failure that are specific to automated instruments.
  2. If the same cartridge passes a manual test but fails an automated test, how would you investigate? What about the reverse?
Table 12.0-1🔍 Automatic Device Integrity Test Troubleshooting Guide🔍

Original Text

Table 12.0-1🔍 Automatic Device Integrity Test Troubleshooting Guide

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Reading the table structure: Table 12.0-1🔍 lists 10 different failure symptoms (Issue), each linked to several possible causes (Possible Cause). The key to studying this table is not memorization but understanding the following patterns:

  • High-frequency root cause: "Insufficiently wetted filter" appears in almost every type of failure, making it the most widespread problem
  • Systemic problem: "Internal pneumatic works improperly" is also a frequent cause, pointing to the importance of instrument maintenance
  • Parameter errors: "Incorrect test parameter settings" and "incorrect test gas" remind us of the risk of human setup errors

Analogy

This troubleshooting table is like the "symptom to differential diagnosis" chart in a hospital emergency room:

  • Symptom (Issue): The patient has a fever (= the test fails)
  • Differential diagnosis (Possible Cause): It could be a cold, pneumonia, a urinary tract infection, or even appendicitis; one symptom can have many different causes
  • Investigation strategy: You do not prescribe antibiotics the moment you see a fever (= you do not declare the filter bad the moment a test fails); you work through a systematic investigation

Likewise, some "diseases" (= root causes) show up as several different "symptoms" (= failure symptoms). For example, the root cause "insufficient wetting" may appear as a bubble point failure, a diffusive flow failure, or a pressure decay failure.

Key Notes

Failure symptoms that deserve special attention:

  • "No Pressure Decay": This is the only case that may indicate a "false pass". If the downstream valve is closed or the line is blocked, gas cannot diffuse downstream and the pressure does not decay. The test appears to "pass" but the filter has not actually been tested at all! This is more dangerous than a test "failure"
  • "Elevated temperature during test time": This appears under "No Pressure Decay" because a temperature rise increases system pressure and offsets the pressure drop caused by diffusion
  • "Large multiple-cartridge assembly": This appears only under bubble point failure, because when many cartridges are in parallel, the large diffusive flow masks the bubble point signal

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: establishing a standard investigation procedure (SOP):

In a CDMO environment, investigating an integrity test failure affects not only the release decision for the batch but also the regulatory requirements for deviation investigation and CAPA. The following investigation flow is recommended:

  1. Step 1: Record the status on site. Record the test parameters, ambient temperature, cartridge lot number and the wetting procedure execution records
  2. Step 2: Rule out operational and system factors. Check the non-filter factors one by one against Table 12.0-1🔍
  3. Step 3: Re-wet and retest. If insufficient wetting is suspected, re-wet using the complete wetting procedure and then retest
  4. Step 4: Replace the cartridge and retest. If the test still fails, test with a new cartridge to confirm whether the problem lies in the system
  5. Step 5: Make the determination and document it. Reach a final determination based on the investigation results, and record the root cause and corrective measures

Key principle: Sterility Assurance is always the first priority. If a filter defect cannot be ruled out, the batch must not be released.

Practice Questions

  1. Among the 10 failure symptoms in Table 12.0-1🔍, which is the most dangerous (one that could cause a non-integral filter to be wrongly judged as "pass")? Explain your reasoning.
  2. If, in a CDMO, you encounter three consecutive batches whose diffusive flow tests are "high but just passing" (not exceeding the limit but trending upward), how would you handle it? Is this a deviation or a matter of trend management?
  3. The root cause "Filter contains traces of product solution or extraneous material from wetting fluid" appears under multiple failure types. Why does product residue affect the integrity test result? Does it make the result higher or lower?
  4. Your team reports a "pressure decay test failure". List the first five possible causes you would investigate, in priority order, and explain why you chose that order.

Key Notes: Common Root Cause Analysis

Statistics of common root causes across failure types:

Conclusion: The top three root causes are not "a bad filter". Wetting, instrument maintenance and wetting fluid selection are what need the most attention.

Section Summary

  • A test failure is not a filter failure: Statistically, most integrity test failures are rooted in operation (insufficient wetting, wrong parameters) and the system (tubing leaks, instrument problems), not in a defect of the filter itself
  • Three lines of investigation for manual tests: Insufficiently Wetted Filter, Inappropriate Wetting Medium, Incorrect Pore Size Rating
  • "No Pressure Decay" is the most dangerous failure: It can cause a non-integral filter to be wrongly judged as passing, because a downstream blockage or a temperature rise offsets the pressure decay
  • Common high-frequency root causes: Insufficient wetting (appears in 8 of 10 failure types), internal pneumatic problems (7/10) and inappropriate wetting fluid (7/10) are the factors that most need preventive management
  • Systematic investigation mindset: Use an "outside-in" order: check tubing, seals and operation first, then the instrument, and consider a filter defect last. A standard investigation SOP ensures consistent and complete investigations
  • Sterility Assurance comes first: Where a filter defect cannot be clearly ruled out, the Sterility Assurance first principle must be followed and the product must not be released
